In the case of the superrotations the situation is a bit more subtle. With the general parametrization where
m\in\mathbb{Z}
there are singularities at
z=0,\infty
, i.e. the functions are only meromorphic. Only the ordinary Lorentz rotations and boosts with
m=0,1,2
are holomorphic on the entire sphere and thus globally well defined as the vector fields
R_{m}\equiv z^{m}\partial_{z},m<0
and, after redefining
\omega=z^{-1}
,
R_{m}=\omega^{2-m}\partial_{\omega},m>2
, have a singularity at the origin
[
68
]
.
A general superrotation will thus map a flat spacetime to one that is flat everywhere except at the singularities which is then called asymptotically locally flat (ALF)
[
70
]
. While some authors argued that superrotations have to be excluded because of the singularities, others suggested that they might have a physical interpretation related to cosmic strings. Cosmic strings are one-dimensional topological defects which were linked to the following construction due to Penrose
[
71
]
,
[
70
]
. Consider a Minkowski spacetime that is cut along the light-cone
u=0
. After performing a diffeomorphism on the patch with
u>0
it is glued together such that the metric is continuous across the cut. This procedure introduces singularities, e.g. at
z=0,\infty
which describe a cosmic string. In our universe such strings might exist if at some early time the universe was not simply connected yet (cf. also section
Decay of Cosmic Strings
). Vacua with strings would then be ALF spacetimes and the snapping of a string can therefore be seen as a transition from an ALF spacetime to an inequivalent asymptotically flat one which is indeed just a superrotation.
In analogy to the supertranslations a further consequence of the existence of superrotations was suggested to be the (sub-leading) spin-memory effect
[
135
]
.

Surface Charges

A cornerstone of theoretical physics is the principle that continuous symmetries lead to conserved quantities as expressed by the Noether theorem. In the case of a “rigid” or global symmetry of a field theory on the
D
-dimensional manifold
\mathcal{M}
with a parameter
\epsilon

[
72
]

\displaystyle x\mapsto x+\delta_{\epsilon}x,\quad\phi\mapsto\phi+\delta_{\epsilon}\phi,

(156)

one can obtain the associated Noether current
j^{\mu}
by replacing
\epsilon
with an arbitrary function
\epsilon(x)
and reading it off from the variation of the action

\displaystyle\delta S=-\int_{\mathcal{M}}d^{D}xj^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\epsilon.

(157)

If the transformation (
156
) is indeed a symmetry of the theory the variation vanishes on-shell, i.e. if the fields satisfy the equations of motion, which in turn implies that
j^{\mu}
is conserved. For a space-like slice
\Sigma
of
\mathcal{M}
the associated conserved Noether charge then reads

\displaystyle Q=\int_{\Sigma}d^{D-1}xn_{\mu}j^{\mu},

(158)

where
n_{\mu}
is the time-like normal vector field of
\Sigma
. If, however, we are dealing with a gauge symmetry then
\epsilon
will be depending on the spacetime coordinates from the start and thus the rhs of (
157
) vanishes identically leaving us with no possibility to determine
j^{\mu}
. The situation can be rescued by noting that we can modify the current
j^{\mu}\rightarrow j^{\mu}+\partial_{\nu}k^{\mu\nu}
and (
157
) is unaffected if
k^{\mu\nu}
is antisymmetric. The Noether charge then receives a contribution

\displaystyle Q_{\text{surface}}=\int_{\Sigma}d^{D-1}xn_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}k^{\mu\nu}=\int_{\partial\Sigma}d^{D-2}n_{\mu}n^{\prime}_{\nu}k^{\mu\nu},

(159)

where
n^{\prime}_{\nu}
is normal to
\partial\Sigma
. We can now associate a two-form
k^{\mu\nu}
to the symmetry by using (
157
) with
\partial_{\nu}k^{\mu\nu}
and take (
159
) as a definition for the charge, it is conserved if
\nabla_{\mu}k^{\mu\nu}=0
on-shell, where
\nabla_{\mu}
is the covariant derivative.

Conserved charges
Q
of a symmetry described by a group
G
generate infinitesimal symmetry transformations in the following way
[
72
]
. Let
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
be a Poisson manifold with Poisson bracket
\{,\}
, the classical phase space of a theory and
F
an observable, i.e. a function on
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
. One can identify the infinitesimal generator
\xi_{X}
of the action of
G
on
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
, which is a vector field on
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
related to an element
X
of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
associated to
G
by

\displaystyle[\xi_{X},\xi_{Y}]=-\xi_{[X,Y]},

(160)

with the charge via

\displaystyle-\{Q[\xi_{X}],\}\equiv\xi_{X},\rightarrow\{Q[\xi_{X}],F\}=\xi_{X}F\equiv\delta_{X}F.

(161)

One says that
Q
4
4

4

Or rather the map
\tilde{Q}:\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}^{*}
,
\braket{\tilde{Q}(),X}\equiv Q[\xi_{X}]
.
is a momentum map of the action of
G
on
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
.

For the symmetry transformation on the fields
\phi
we can write

\displaystyle\{Q[\xi],\phi\}=-\delta_{\xi}\phi

(162)

and the Jacobi identity for the Poisson bracket implies (using (
160
))

\displaystyle\{\phi,\{Q[\xi],Q[\zeta]\}\}=-\{Q[\zeta],\{Q[\xi],\phi\}\}-\{Q[\xi],\{\phi,Q[\zeta]\}\}=[\delta_{\xi},\delta_{\zeta}]\phi=\{\phi,Q[[\xi,\zeta]]\}.

(163)

As this has to hold for arbitrary fields, one can conclude that the terms in the bracket have to coincide up to a constant term

\displaystyle\{Q[\xi],Q[\zeta]\}=Q[[\xi,\zeta]]+c(\xi,\zeta)

(164)

such that
\{c,\phi\}=0
.
The Jacobi identity then enforces

\displaystyle c(\{Q[\xi_{1}],Q[\xi_{2}]\},Q[\xi_{3}])+\text{cycl.}=0,

(165)

which is the condition that
c
is a 2-cocycle of the Chevalley-Eilenberg cohomology with values in the field
\mathbb{C}

5
5

5

The first line in (
11
) vanishes because the action of the field is trivial and thus we obtain (
165
)
. Also, if
c
were a coboundary derived from a 1-cocycle
c^{\prime}
we could redefine

\displaystyle Q^{\prime}[\cdot]=Q[\cdot]+c^{\prime}(\cdot)

(166)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\{Q^{\prime}[\xi_{1}],Q^{\prime}[\xi_{2}]\}=Q[[\xi_{1},\xi_{2}]]+c(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})=Q[[\xi_{1},\xi_{2}]]+c^{\prime}([\xi_{1},\xi_{2}])=Q^{\prime}[[\xi_{1},\xi_{2}]]

(167)

so we find that the algebra of the surface charges is in general a central extension of the algebra of the symmetry generators classified by the cohomology group
H^{2}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{C})
.

In the flat
(\Lambda=0)
case one finds for the surface charges associated to the supertranslations
[
61
]

\displaystyle Q[f]=\frac{1}{4\pi G}\int d^{2}z\gamma_{z\bar{z}}fm_{B},

(168)

where the integration is over the boundary of a space-like slice, e.g. a sphere on
\mathcal{I}^{\pm}
which is a point on the boundary of the Penrose diagram. Defining
Q^{+}[f]
and
Q^{-}[f]
as the charges from integration over
\mathcal{I}^{+}_{-}
and
\mathcal{I}^{-}_{+}
, one has a matching condition implying the conservation of the charges, i.e.

\displaystyle Q^{+}[f]=Q^{-}[f].

(169)

Similarly, for the superrotations we have

\displaystyle Q^{+}[R^{A}]=\frac{1}{8\pi G}\int_{\mathcal{I}^{+}_{-}}d^{2}z(R_{\bar{z}}N_{z}+R_{z}N_{\bar{z}})=\frac{1}{8\pi G}\int_{\mathcal{I}^{-}_{+}}d^{2}z(R_{\bar{z}}N_{z}+R_{z}N_{\bar{z}})=Q^{-}[R^{A}],

(170)

where the conservation follows from a matching condition for the angular momentum aspect
N_{A}
.

For the
\mathfrak{B}_{4}
(eq. (
139
)-(
140
)) it was shown in
[
73
]
that the only consistent central extensions are those of the Witt subalgebras. There, the second cohomology group with values in the trivial module is one-dimensional and given by the Gelfand-Fuks cocycle leading to the Virasoro algebra (
\mathfrak{Vir}
), i.e. the centrally extended Witt algebra

\displaystyle[l_{m},l_{n}]=(m-n)l_{m+n}+\frac{c_{l}}{12}(m^{3}-m)\delta_{m+n,0}

(171)

with central charge
c_{l}
. We denote this surface charge algebra by
\mathfrak{B}_{4,c}\equiv V_{4}\rtimes(\mathfrak{Vir}\oplus\mathfrak{Vir})
. In four dimensions the calculation of these charges
(c_{l},c_{\bar{l}})
in general is quite involved as they are non-integrable in the presence of gravitational waves
[
74
]
.

Asymptotically (Anti) de Sitter Spacetimes

As the solutions to the Einstein equations for a globally source-free configuration are, besides the Minkowski spacetime, also (Anti) de Sitter ((A)dS) spacetimes depending on the cosmological constant it is natural to investigate the concept of asymptotic symmetries for these solutions.
Recently, this has been done in
[
75
]
, where the symmetry group of a spacetime that is asymptotically Anti de Sitter was investigated.
Even though the de Sitter case is algebraically very similar there are several unresolved conceptual difficulties in the gravitational description of its asymptotics as described for example in
[
76
]
. The main issue appears to be that the null infinities
\mathcal{I}^{\pm}
are space-like for
\Lambda>0
which makes it difficult to even formulate boundary conditions.

In
[
75
]
, just as in the asymptotically flat case, a generic spacetime metric is constrained by fall-off conditions in the radial coordinate
r
such that in the limit
r\rightarrow\infty
the AdS metric is recovered. The Killing vectors
\xi_{f,R}=f\partial_{u}+R^{A}\partial_{A}
of this asymptotically AdS metric satisfy in first order of
1/r

\displaystyle[\xi_{1},\xi_{2}]=\hat{\xi}=\hat{f}\partial_{u}+\hat{R}^{A}\partial_{A},

(172)

\displaystyle\hat{f}=R_{1}^{A}\partial_{A}f_{2}+\frac{1}{2}f_{1}\partial_{A}R^{A}_{2}-(1\leftrightarrow 2),\quad\hat{R}^{A}=R^{B}_{1}\partial_{B}R_{2}^{A}+\frac{\Lambda}{3}f_{1}\gamma^{AB}\partial_{B}f_{2}-(1\leftrightarrow 2).

(173)

Parametrizing the Killing vector fields by whole numbers

\displaystyle\xi_{f_{pq},0}\equiv T_{pq}\equiv\frac{z^{p}{\bar{z}}^{q}}{1+z\bar{z}}\partial_{u},\quad\xi_{0,R^{z}_{n}}\equiv l_{n}\equiv-z^{n+1}\partial_{z},\quad\xi_{0,R^{\bar{z}}_{n}}\equiv\bar{l}_{n}\equiv-\bar{z}^{n+1}\partial_{\bar{z}},

(174)

yields the following structure

\displaystyle[T_{p,q},T_{m,n}]

\displaystyle=\Lambda\frac{z^{p}\bar{z}^{q}}{1+z\bar{z}}\frac{(1+z\bar{z})^{2}}{2}\left(\partial_{\bar{z}}\frac{z^{m}\bar{z}^{n}}{1+z\bar{z}}\partial_{z}+\partial_{z}\frac{z^{m}\bar{z}^{n}}{1+z\bar{z}}\partial_{\bar{z}}\right)-(mn\leftrightarrow pq)

\displaystyle=\Lambda((n-q)z^{p+m}\bar{z}^{q+n-1}\partial_{z}+(m-p)z^{p+m-1}\bar{z}^{q+n}\partial_{\bar{z}})

(175)

\displaystyle[l_{m},l_{n}]

\displaystyle=(m-n)l_{m+n},\quad[\bar{l}_{m},\bar{l}_{n}]=(m-n)\bar{l}_{m+n}

(176)

\displaystyle[l_{m},T_{p,q}]

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{m+1}{2}-p\right)T_{p+m,q},\quad[\bar{l}_{m},T_{p,q}]=\left(\frac{m+1}{2}-q\right)T_{p,q+m}.

(177)

Thus, the result (
175
) can not be written as the sum of basis elements
\xi_{f_{mn},R^{A}_{p}}
but is a general vector field on the sphere, i.e. the four dimensional
\Lambda
-BMS is a Lie algebroid. Technically this is a consequence of the appearance of the coordinate dependent metric on the sphere in (
173
). However, it is still true that the ordinary symmetry algebra of Anti de Sitter spacetime (denoted by
AdS_{4}
)

\displaystyle[K_{+},K_{-}]

\displaystyle=-i\Lambda M_{+-},\quad[K_{+},K_{a}]=-i\Lambda M_{+a},

(178)

\displaystyle[K_{-},K_{a}]

\displaystyle=-i\Lambda M_{-a},\quad[K_{a},K_{b}]=-i\Lambda M_{ab},

(179)

\displaystyle[M_{\mu\nu},M_{\rho\sigma}]

\displaystyle=i(\eta_{\mu\sigma}M_{\nu\rho}-\eta_{\nu\sigma}M_{\mu\rho}+\eta_{\nu\rho}M_{\mu\sigma}-\eta_{\mu\rho}M_{\nu\sigma}),

(180)

\displaystyle[M_{\mu\nu},K_{\rho}]

\displaystyle=i(\eta_{\nu\rho}K_{\mu}-\eta_{\mu\rho}K_{\nu}),

(181)

is embedded into (
175
) which can be seen by considering the subalgebras spanned by

\displaystyle l_{0},l_{1-2m},l_{-1+2m},\bar{l}_{0},\bar{l}_{1-2m},\bar{l}_{-1+2m},T_{mm},T_{1-m,m},T_{m,1-m},T_{1-m,1-m},

with the rescaling

\displaystyle l_{n}\rightarrow\frac{l_{n}}{1-2m},\quad\bar{l}_{n}\rightarrow\frac{\bar{l}_{n}}{1-2m},\quad\Lambda\rightarrow(1-2m)^{2}\Lambda.

(182)

We have e.g.

\displaystyle[K_{-},K_{+}]

\displaystyle=[T_{mm},T_{1-m,1-m}]=\Lambda(1-2m)(z\partial_{z}+\bar{z}\partial_{\bar{z}})

\displaystyle=(1-2m)^{2}\Lambda(\xi_{l_{0}}+\xi_{\bar{l}_{0}})=i(1-2m)^{2}\Lambda M_{+-},

(183)

and similar for the rest of the
AdS_{4}
.

Although there are attempts to define generalizations of bialgebras on some versions of algebroids, e.g. in
[
77
]
,
[
78
]
, there is no established notion of quantum groups on a Lie algebroid and therefore deformations of asymptotically (A)dS spacetime symmetries in four dimensions will not be studied directly in this work. Nonetheless, one can hope to infer valuable insights from the three dimensional theory of gravity.

3D Gravity

As we will see, Einsteins theory of GR in two spatial and one time dimension is a useful toy model for studying asymptotic symmetries as well as other problems that are harder to address in four dimensional gravity. On the first glance, the theory seems to be rather trivial and completely different from what one would associate with gravity because it does not have a Newtonian limit and massive particles do not attract each other
[
79
]
,
[
80
]
,
[
81
]
. In fact, there are no local gravitational degrees of freedom at all in three dimensions! This means in particular that no gravitational waves exist and gravitational energy can not be radiated away. Formally this is a consequence of the fact that the three dimensional Riemann tensor, which can be expressed in terms of the Ricci and the Weyl tensor, has the same number of degrees of freedom as the Ricci tensor. Thus, the Weyl tensor vanishes leading to the peculiar features mentioned above.

Nonetheless, because of topological and asymptotic properties, there are still interesting physical phenomena. For example particles can be introduced in the form of topological defects with the geometry of a cone
[
82
]
. The fact that topological but no local gravitational degrees of freedom exist is also exemplified by the equivalence to Chern-Simons theory which is a topological field theory
[
83
]
. Because Chern-Simons theory can be quantized, it is hoped that the reformulation can shed light on the problem of quantum gravity
[
84
]
.
It was also shown that black hole solutions with an event horizon exist
[
85
]
. These so-called BTZ black holes admit (if they are non-extremal) a finite temperature implying that they decay like their four dimensional counterparts.

Asymptotic Symmetries

Depending on the value of the cosmological constant, the three dimensional spacetime is locally isometric to either Minkowski, de Sitter or Anti de Sitter space. We will start the discussion of the asymptotic symmetries in the case of curved spacetime (following
[
72
]
) and then view the asymptotically flat spacetime as a contraction limit
\Lambda\rightarrow 0
.

Analogously to the four dimensional case one can start from a general metric

\displaystyle ds^{2}=-\left(1-r^{2}\Lambda\right)dt^{2}+\left(1-r^{2}\Lambda\right)^{-1}dr^{2}+r^{2}d\phi^{2}

(184)

and impose fall-off conditions so that it asymptotically approaches (A)dS. The Killing vectors are
\xi_{f,R}=f\partial_{u}+R\partial_{z}
where
R=R(z),f=T(z)+u\partial_{z}R
and we have

\displaystyle[\xi_{1},\xi_{2}]=\hat{\xi}\equiv\hat{f}\partial_{u}+\hat{R}\partial_{z}

(185)

\displaystyle\hat{f}=R_{1}\partial_{z}f_{2}+f_{1}\partial_{z}R_{2}-(1\leftrightarrow 2),\quad\hat{R}=R_{1}\partial_{z}R_{2}-\Lambda f_{1}\partial_{z}f_{2}-(1\leftrightarrow 2).

(186)

Here,
u=t-r
is again the retarded time and
z=e^{i\phi}
with the angular coordinate
\phi
.
Parametrizing
f_{m}\equiv T_{m}=z^{m+1}
and
R_{m}\equiv l_{m}=z^{m+1}
, we find

\displaystyle[l_{m},l_{n}]

\displaystyle=(m-n)l_{m+n},\quad[l_{m},T_{n}]=(m-n)T_{m+n},

(187)

\displaystyle[T_{m},T_{n}]

\displaystyle=-\Lambda(m-n)l_{m+n}.

(188)

As in the four dimensional case the symmetry algebra of the globally AdS spacetime (
AdS_{3}
)

\displaystyle[\tilde{P}_{+},\tilde{P}_{-}]=-i\eta_{+-}\Lambda M_{+-},\quad[\tilde{P}_{\pm},\tilde{P}_{2}]=-i\eta_{+-}\Lambda M_{\pm 2},

(189)

\displaystyle[M_{+2},M_{-2}]=i(-\eta_{22})M_{+-},\quad[M_{+-},M_{\pm 2}]=\pm i\eta_{+-}M_{\pm 2},\quad[M_{+-},\tilde{P}_{\pm}]=\pm i\eta_{+-}\tilde{P}_{\pm},

(190)

\displaystyle[M_{\pm 2},\tilde{P}_{2}]=i\eta_{22}\tilde{P}_{\pm},\quad[M_{\pm 2},\tilde{P}_{\mp}]=-i\eta_{+-}P_{2},

(191)

where we choose the metric to be
\eta_{+-}=1,\eta_{22}=-1
,
is a subalgebra of (
187
) and there are infinitely many embeddings of the form

\displaystyle\tilde{P}_{2}=-i\frac{T_{0}}{n},\quad\tilde{P}_{\pm}=i\frac{T_{\pm n}}{\sqrt{2}n},\quad M_{+-}=-i\frac{l_{0}}{n},\quad M_{\pm 2}=\pm\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}n}l_{\pm n}.

(192)

The embeddings are indeed isomorphic to (
189
) with the rescaling
\Lambda\rightarrow n^{2}\Lambda
. Alternatively, one could set
n=1
in (
192
) and rescale the ambient metric
\eta_{\mu\nu}\rightarrow\frac{\eta_{\mu\nu}}{n^{2}}
instead of
\Lambda
.

(
189
) can also be obtained from the four dimensional Lorentz algebra

\displaystyle[M_{\mu\nu},M_{\rho\lambda}]=i(\eta_{\mu\lambda}M_{\nu\rho}-\eta_{\nu\lambda}M_{\mu\rho}+\eta_{\nu\rho}M_{\mu\lambda}-\eta_{\mu\rho}M_{\nu\lambda})

(193)

if
M_{\pm 3},M_{23}
is identified with
K_{\pm},K_{2}
. Here the metric is given by
\eta_{+-}=1,\eta_{22}=-1,\eta_{33}=-1
.
K_{i}
has to be rescaled according to

\displaystyle\tilde{P}_{i}=R^{-1}K_{i},

(194)

where
R^{-1}=\sqrt{|\Lambda|}
is the inverse AdS radius. Thus, the generators
\tilde{P}_{i}
are actually boosts in a higher dimensional spacetime and acquire the interpretation as momenta only in the contraction limit
\Lambda\rightarrow 0
when they commute among themselves and form an ideal.

(A)dS_{3}
as real forms

On the algebraic level one can also obtain the de Sitter case by simply setting
\Lambda>0
. Depending on the choice of
\Lambda
, (
187
) is a different real algebra with one reality condition
6
6

6

We warn the reader that keeping it real might also go wrong, as exemplified in
[
86
]
.

\displaystyle l^{\dagger}_{m}=-l_{m},\quad T_{m}^{\dagger}=-T_{m}.

(195)

These two algebras can also be expressed as two different real forms of the complex Lie algebra
\mathfrak{W}\oplus\mathfrak{W}(\mathbb{C})

7
7

7

In the following, if no real form is specified, we often refer to the complex algebra without making it explicit in the notation.
defined by

\displaystyle[L_{m},L_{n}]=(m-n)L_{m+n},\quad[\bar{L}_{m},\bar{L}_{n}]=(m-n)\bar{L}_{m+n},\quad[L_{m},\bar{L}_{n}]=0.

(196)

This is established by the isomorphism

\displaystyle L_{m}=\frac{1}{2}\left(l_{m}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{-\Lambda}}T_{m}\right),\quad\bar{L}_{m}=\frac{1}{2}\left(l_{m}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{-\Lambda}}T_{m}\right).

(197)

The embeddings now take the form

\displaystyle L_{0},\bar{L}_{\pm n},\bar{L}_{0},\bar{L}_{\pm n},

(198)

\displaystyle L_{m}\rightarrow\frac{L_{m}}{n},\quad\bar{L}_{m}\rightarrow\frac{\bar{L}_{m}}{n},

(199)

they are isomorphic to the algebra
\mathfrak{o}(4,\mathbb{C})

\displaystyle[L_{0},L_{\pm 1}]=\mp L_{\pm 1},\qquad[L_{+1},L_{-1}]=2L_{0},

(200)

\displaystyle[\bar{L}_{0},\bar{L}_{\pm 1}]=\mp\bar{L}_{\pm 1},\qquad[\bar{L}_{+1},\bar{L}_{-1}]=2\bar{L}_{0}.

(201)

For negative
\Lambda
, i.e. the AdS case, we have from (
197
) and (
195
) that

\displaystyle L_{m}^{\dagger}=-L_{m},\quad\bar{L}_{m}^{\dagger}=-\bar{L}_{m},

(202)

whereas for the de Sitter case one finds

\displaystyle L_{m}^{\ddagger}=-\bar{L}_{m},\quad\bar{L}_{m}^{\ddagger}=-L_{m}.

(203)

Restricted to (
200
), which is related to the standard Cartan-Weyl form

\displaystyle[H,E_{\pm}]=\pm E_{\pm},\qquad[E_{+},E_{-}]=2H,

(204)

\displaystyle[\bar{H},\bar{E}_{\pm}]=\pm\bar{E}_{\pm},\qquad[\bar{E}_{+},\bar{E}_{-}]=2\bar{H},

(205)

via

\displaystyle H=-L_{0},\quad E_{\pm}=iL_{\pm 1},\quad\bar{H}=-\bar{L}_{0},\quad\bar{E}_{\pm}=i\bar{L}_{\pm 1},

(206)

the real forms correspond to two copies of
\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{R})\simeq\mathfrak{o}(2,1)
for AdS and the Kleinian algebra
\mathfrak{o}(2,2)\simeq\mathfrak{o}(2,1)\oplus\bar{\mathfrak{o}}(2,1)
for dS expressed by

\displaystyle H^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=-H,\quad E_{\pm}^{\dagger}=E_{\pm},\quad\bar{H}^{\dagger}=-\bar{H},\quad\bar{E}_{\pm}^{\dagger}=\bar{E}_{\pm},

(207)

\displaystyle H^{\ddagger}

\displaystyle=-\bar{H},\quad E^{\ddagger}_{\pm}=\bar{E}_{\pm}.

(208)

This can be identified with the real structures listed in
[
87
]
with the automorphism
E_{\pm}\rightarrow-E_{\pm},\bar{E}_{\pm}\rightarrow-\bar{E}_{\pm}
. Note that this automorphism of the
\mathfrak{sl}(2)

\displaystyle\Phi(E_{\pm})=-E_{\pm},\quad\Phi(H)=H,\rightarrow\Phi(L_{\pm 1})=-L_{\pm 1},\quad\Phi(L_{0})=L_{0},

(209)

can be extended uniquely to an automorphism of the Witt algebra. On the algebraic level we thus can describe both dS and AdS spacetime symmetries simultaneously by switching between the two real forms.

Algebra of
AdS_{3}
Surface Charges

Following
[
72
]
, one can also introduce light-cone coordinates
x^{\pm}=\frac{t}{\sqrt{-\Lambda}}\pm\phi
leading to the metric (in Fefferman-Graham gauge)

\displaystyle ds^{2}=-\frac{1}{r^{2}\Lambda}dr^{2}-\left(rdx^{+}-\frac{4G}{\sqrt{-\Lambda}r}\bar{p}(x^{-})dx^{-}\right)\left(rdx^{-}-\frac{4G}{\sqrt{-\Lambda}r}p(x^{+})dx^{+}\right)

(210)

and instead of
f
and
R
the Killing vectors are then parametrized by
X(x^{+})
and
\bar{X}(x^{-})
. In analogy to (
170
) one has

\displaystyle Q[X,\bar{X}]=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\phi\left(X(x^{+})p(x^{+})+\bar{X}(x^{-})\bar{p}(x^{-})\right).

(211)

The algebra of these charges is a central extension of the algebra of the Killing vectors, i.e.
\mathfrak{W}\oplus\mathfrak{W}
. As noted at the end of section
Surface Charges
, the Witt algebras only have one possible central extension and so the general form of the central charge algebra is

\displaystyle[L_{m},L_{n}]=

\displaystyle(m-n)L_{m+n}+\frac{c_{L}}{12}(m^{3}-m)\delta_{m+n,0},

(212)

\displaystyle[\bar{L}_{m},\bar{L}_{n}]=

\displaystyle(m-n)\bar{L}_{m+n}+\frac{c_{\bar{L}}}{12}(m^{3}-m)\delta_{m+n,0},

(213)

\displaystyle[L_{m},\bar{L}_{n}]=

\displaystyle 0.

(214)

Brown and Henneaux calculated explicitly that
[
56
]

\displaystyle c_{L}=c_{\bar{L}}=\frac{3}{2G\sqrt{-\Lambda}}.

(215)

Asymptotic Flatness

One possibility of obtaining the symmetry algebra of asymptotically flat spacetime in three dimensions would be to consider a general metric, impose fall-off conditions and analyze the algebra of the Killing vectors just as it was done for the four dimensional case. However, since we already know the symmetry algebra of asymptotically (A)dS spacetime and it has the form of a Lie algebra, we can simply calculate the contraction limit
8
8

8

On the algebraic level such a procedure is also called Inonu-Wigner contraction
[
69
]
.

\Lambda\rightarrow 0

[
68
]
. Starting from the algebra of the surface charges (
212
)-(
214
) and using (
197
) with the automorphism
\bar{L}_{m}\rightarrow-\bar{L}_{-m}
, i.e.

\displaystyle T_{m}=\sqrt{-\Lambda}(L_{m}+\bar{L}_{-m}),\quad l_{m}=L_{m}-\bar{L}_{-m},

(216)

we obtain

\displaystyle[T_{m},T_{n}]=

\displaystyle-\Lambda([L_{m},L_{n}]+[\bar{L}_{-m},\bar{L}_{-n}])=-\Lambda(m-n)l_{m+n}

\displaystyle+\delta_{m+n,0}(m^{3}-m)\frac{c_{L}}{12}+\delta_{-m-n,0}((-m)^{3}+m)\frac{\bar{c}_{L}}{12},

(217)

\displaystyle[l_{m},l_{n}]=

\displaystyle(m-n)l_{m+n}+\delta_{m+n,0}(m^{3}-m)\frac{c_{L}}{12}+\delta_{-m-n,0}((-m)^{3}+m)\frac{\bar{c}_{L}}{12},

(218)

\displaystyle[l_{m},T_{n}]=

\displaystyle(m-n)T_{m+n}+\sqrt{-\Lambda}\left(\delta_{m+n,0}(m^{3}-m)\frac{c_{L}}{12}-\delta_{-m-n,0}((-m)^{3}+m)\frac{\bar{c}_{L}}{12}\right).

(219)

Now the contraction limit and (
215
) yields the algebra of the surface charges in three dimensions

\displaystyle[T_{m},T_{n}]

\displaystyle=0,\qquad[l_{m},l_{n}]=(m-n)l_{m+n},

(220)

\displaystyle[l_{m},T_{n}]

\displaystyle=(m-n)T_{m+n}+\delta_{m+n,0}(m^{3}-m)c^{\prime}_{L},

(221)

with
c^{\prime}_{L}=\frac{1}{4G}
, which we denote
\mathfrak{B}_{3,c}
in the following. Without the central extension terms, (
220
)-(
221
) defines the three dimensional BMS algebra
\mathfrak{B}_{3}
.

The three dimensional Poincaré algebra (
\mathfrak{P}_{3}
)

\displaystyle[M_{+2},M_{-2}]

\displaystyle=i\eta_{11}M_{+-}\,,\quad[M_{+-},M_{\pm 2}]=\mp i\eta_{+-}M_{\pm 1}\,,

(222)

\displaystyle[M_{+-},P_{\pm}]

\displaystyle=\mp i\eta_{+-}P_{\pm}\,,\quad[M_{\pm 2},P_{2}]=-i\eta_{22}P_{\pm}\,,

(223)

\displaystyle[M_{\pm 2},P_{\mp}]

\displaystyle=i\eta_{+-}P_{2}\,,

(224)

is infinitely often embedded in
\mathfrak{B}_{3}
with

\displaystyle M_{+-}=-il_{0},\quad M_{\pm 2}=\pm\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}}l_{\pm n},\quad P_{2}=-iT_{0},\quad P_{\pm}=\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}}T_{\pm n},

(225)

if the metric is rescaled as
\eta_{+-}=\eta_{-+}=-\eta_{22}=n
, otherwise the Lorentz generators have to be rescaled by
1/n
.

Note that while the symmetry algebras without central extension in three dimensions are subalgebras of their four dimensional counterparts, this is not true for the centrally extended algebras, i.e.
\mathfrak{B}_{3,c}\not\subseteq\mathfrak{B}_{4,c}
. Thus, arguments that results from the three dimensional theory can be transferred to four dimensions as one is a subset of the other, have to be particularly wary of the influence of contributions from the central extension.

.

Quantum Groups on Asymptotic Symmetry Algebras

In this chapter, the consistent quantum groups on the symmetry algebras of three dimensional asymptotically (anti) de Sitter spacetimes are analyzed. To this end, we first classify Lie bialgebra structures and for several examples we will explicitly construct the associated Hopf algebras and investigate their properties and also restrictions they might impose on the symmetries themselves based on both physical and mathematical constraints. Subsequently the asymptotically flat limit and the four dimensional case are considered and compared.

Deformations of Asymptotically (Anti) de Sitter Symmetries in 3D Gravity

Lie Bialgebra Structures on
\mathfrak{W}\oplus\mathfrak{W}

Recall from section
Coboundary and Triangular Lie Bialgebras
that all Lie bialgebra structures on an algebra
\mathfrak{g}
are either coboundary or have a cobracket from the cohomology group
H^{1}(\mathfrak{g},\bigwedge^{2}\mathfrak{g})
. In Appendix
Proof of the Cohomology Theorems
, the following theorems are proven
9
9

9

In
[
89
]
the authors independently prove a slightly more general result than our first theorem at the cost of a longer proof. The proofs presented here conceptually follow the proof of
H^{1}(\mathfrak{W},\mathfrak{W})=\{0\}
in
[
90
]
.

Theorem 1
.

The first cohomology group
H^{1}\left(\mathfrak{W},\bigwedge^{2}\mathfrak{W}\right)
vanishes.

Theorem 2
.

The first cohomology group
H^{1}\left(\mathfrak{W}\oplus\mathfrak{W},\bigwedge^{2}\mathfrak{W}\oplus\mathfrak{W}\right)
vanishes.

Therefore all LBA can be obtained from a classical r-matrix satisfying the mCYBE (
14
). This is also true for the LBA on the symmetry algebra of globally (A)dS space, the real forms of
\mathfrak{o}(4,\mathbb{C})
, as a consequence of the Whitehead lemma. What is different, though, is that in the infinite dimensional algebras
\mathfrak{W}
and
\mathfrak{W}\oplus\mathfrak{W}
there is no ad-invariant element
\Omega\in\left(\bigwedge^{3}\mathfrak{W}\right)_{\mathfrak{W}}
, resp.
\left(\bigwedge^{3}(\mathfrak{W}\oplus\mathfrak{W})\right)_{\mathfrak{W}\oplus\mathfrak{W}}
anymore. This follows easily from

\displaystyle 0\overset{!}{=}

\displaystyle L_{m}\triangleright\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}L_{p_{i}}\wedge L_{q_{i}}\wedge L_{s_{i}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}((m-p_{i})L_{p_{i}+m}\wedge L_{q_{i}}\wedge L_{s_{i}}+(m-q_{i})L_{p_{i}}\wedge L_{q_{i}+m}\wedge L_{s_{i}}

\displaystyle+(m-s_{i})L_{p_{i}}\wedge L_{q_{i}}\wedge L_{s_{i}+m})

(226)

if we choose
m>0
and focus on the term with
s_{i^{\prime}}>s_{i},p_{i},q_{i}
without loss of generality for the Witt algebra. Similarly, one shows that

\displaystyle\left(\bigwedge^{3}(\mathfrak{W}\oplus\mathfrak{W})\right)_{\mathfrak{W}\oplus\mathfrak{W}}=\{0\},\qquad\left(\bigwedge^{2}\mathfrak{W}\right)_{\mathfrak{W}}=\left(\bigwedge^{2}(\mathfrak{W}\oplus\mathfrak{W})\right)_{\mathfrak{W}\oplus\mathfrak{W}}=\{0\}.

(227)

The latter implies that the map from an r-matrix to the cobracket derived from it is injective. Otherwise, i.e. if there was an ad-invariant element
a\wedge b\in\bigwedge^{2}\mathfrak{W}
, the cobrackets from
r
and
r+a\wedge b
would be identical.
Furthermore, as noted in section
Coboundary and Triangular Lie Bialgebras
, only r-matrices belonging to different orbits under automorphisms of the algebra lead to inequivalent LBA.

Thus, one can conclude that all LBA on
\mathfrak{W}\oplus\mathfrak{W}
are uniquely given by triangular r-matrices that are classified up to automorphism. The simple observation that, in contrast to the situation for the embedded
\mathfrak{o}(4,\mathbb{C})
, only triangular LBA are coassociative will exclude a wide range of deformations.

Classification of Triangular r-matrices

Since the Witt algebra and its central extension, the Virasoro algebra, play a vital role in various parts of math and physics, including string theory
[
91
]
, there were attempts to classify all LBA structures on them, most notably by
[
92
]
. These results remained incomplete though except for some subalgebras, including the one-sided Witt algebra
\mathfrak{W_{+}}
(generated by
\{L_{m},m\geq-1\}
), where all r-matrices were shown to be r-matrices of one of the embeddings. For the two copies of the Witt algebra that are studied here it is also easy to see that e.g. any element of the form

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}L_{i}\right)\wedge\left(\sum_{j}\beta_{j}\bar{L}_{j}\right)

(228)

is a triangular r-matrix.

However, one has to take into account that the asymptotic symmetry is spontaneously broken in the bulk in the sense that the vacua related by supertranslations and superrotations are physically distinguishable (cf. section
Asymptotic Flatness
and
[
61
]
).
There is a correspondence between these vacua and the embeddings of Poincaré or (A)dS subalgebras which leave the associated vacuum invariant. Therefore, we require that the restriction of the Hopf algebra deformed by the twist associated with a given r-matrix to the (A)dS algebra (which is the
n=1
embedding for an observer) is a sub-Hopf algebra and we are interested in r-matrices from (real forms of)
\mathfrak{o}(4,\mathbb{C})
, where
\{H,E_{\pm},\bar{H},\bar{E}_{\pm}\}
is replaced with the embedding
10
10

10

In the following, we often leave the embedding characterized by the index
n
unspecified. Also note that from a mathematical point of view there is no reason the generators that appear in the r-matrix have to be part of a specific embedding and we will comment on this possibility later on.
.
Note that while in the case of positive
\Lambda
the involution mixes left and right-handed elements, this is not happening for negative
\Lambda
, leaving the potential possibility to use different embeddings for them.

The classification of r-matrices of (real forms of)
\mathfrak{o}(4,\mathbb{C})
was done in
[
95
]
and has been discussed as well in
[
94
]
and
[
87
]
. Filtering out the quasi-triangular r-matrices we are left with

\displaystyle r_{I}

\displaystyle=\chi(E_{+}-\bar{E}_{+})\wedge(H+\bar{H}),

(229)

\displaystyle r_{II}

\displaystyle=\chi E_{+}\wedge H+\bar{\chi}\bar{E}_{+}\wedge\bar{H}+\zeta E_{+}\wedge\bar{E}_{+},

(230)

\displaystyle r_{III}

\displaystyle=\eta H\wedge\bar{H}.

(231)

\displaystyle r_{V}

\displaystyle=\bar{\chi}\bar{E}_{+}\wedge\bar{H}+\rho H\wedge\bar{E}_{+}.

(232)

An important caveat remains; the classification (
229
)-(
232
) is up to automorphisms of the
\mathfrak{o}(4,\mathbb{C})
but not all of these automorphisms are also automorphisms of
\mathfrak{W}\oplus\mathfrak{W}
, only the inverse statement holds in general
11
11

11

Here we understand the automorphisms of
\mathfrak{W}\oplus\mathfrak{W}
to be restricted to the subalgebra
\mathfrak{o}(4)
and write
\text{Aut}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{W}\oplus\mathfrak{W})
for this (and similar) restriction(s) so that quotients are well defined.
.
Therefore, in Appendix
Classification of
r
-matrices
the classification of triangular r-matrices on
\mathfrak{o}(4)
is revised along the lines of
[
95
]
but using only the
\mathfrak{W}\oplus\mathfrak{W}

automorphisms

\displaystyle\varphi_{(\gamma,\bar{\gamma},\epsilon,\bar{\epsilon})}(L_{m})=\gamma^{m}\epsilon L_{\epsilon m},\quad\varphi_{(\gamma,\bar{\gamma},\epsilon,\bar{\epsilon})}(\bar{L}_{m})=\bar{\gamma}^{m}\bar{\epsilon}\bar{L}_{\bar{\epsilon}m},

(233)

\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime}(L_{m})=\bar{L}_{m},\quad\varphi^{\prime}(\bar{L}_{m})=L_{m},

(234)

where
0\neq\gamma,\bar{\gamma}\in\mathbb{C},\epsilon,\bar{\epsilon}=\pm 1
.
As a result we obtain the following classes of r-matrices

\displaystyle r_{1^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\beta(L_{1}+L_{-1}+2L_{0})\wedge(\bar{L}_{1}+\bar{L}_{-1}+2\bar{L}_{0})+a_{1}+\bar{a}_{1},

(235)

\displaystyle r_{2^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\beta L_{1}\wedge\bar{L}_{0}+a_{2},

(236)

\displaystyle r_{3^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\beta(L_{1}+\epsilon L_{0}+\epsilon^{\prime}L_{-1})\wedge(\bar{L}_{1}+\bar{L}_{-1}+2\bar{L}_{0})+\bar{a}_{1}+(1-\epsilon)(1-\epsilon^{\prime})a_{2},

(237)

\displaystyle r_{4^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\beta_{1}L_{1}\wedge\bar{L}_{1}+\beta_{2}(L_{1}+\bar{L}_{1})\wedge(L_{0}+\bar{L}_{0}),

(238)

\displaystyle r_{5^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\beta(L_{1}+L_{-1})\wedge(\bar{L}_{1}+\bar{L}_{0})+a_{1},

(239)

\displaystyle r_{6^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=(\beta L_{1}+\beta_{0}L_{0}+\epsilon\beta L_{-1})\wedge(\bar{\beta}\bar{L}_{1}+\bar{\beta}_{0}\bar{L}_{0}+\bar{\epsilon}\bar{\beta}\bar{L}_{-1}),

(240)

\displaystyle r_{7^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=L_{1}\wedge(\bar{\beta}\bar{L}_{1}+\bar{\beta}\bar{L}_{0}+\epsilon\bar{\beta}\bar{L}_{-1})+a_{2},

(241)

\displaystyle r_{8^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\beta L_{1}\wedge\bar{L}_{1}+a_{2}+\bar{a}_{2},

(242)

where
\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime},\bar{\epsilon}\in\{0,1\}
and

\displaystyle a_{1}

\displaystyle=\alpha(L_{1}\wedge L_{0}+L_{1}\wedge L_{-1}-L_{-1}\wedge L_{0}),\quad a_{2}=\alpha L_{1}\wedge L_{0},

(243)

\displaystyle\bar{a}_{1}

\displaystyle=\bar{\alpha}(\bar{L}_{1}\wedge\bar{L}_{0}+\bar{L}_{1}\wedge\bar{L}_{-1}-\bar{L}_{-1}\wedge\bar{L}_{0}),\quad\bar{a}_{2}=\bar{\alpha}\bar{L}_{1}\wedge\bar{L}_{0}.

(244)

In the complex
\mathfrak{W}\oplus\mathfrak{W}
all the parameters in
235
-
242
can take values in
\mathbb{C}
but for the real forms associated with the involutions
\dagger,\ddagger
(
202
), (
203
) constrains the choice of parameters. For
\dagger
in all classes except
r_{6^{\prime}}
this enforces
\beta,\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\alpha,\bar{\alpha}\in i\mathbb{R}
and in
r_{6^{\prime}}
one can restrict
\beta,\beta_{0},\bar{\beta},\bar{\beta}_{0}\in\sqrt{i}\mathbb{R}
without loss of generality.
In the case of positive
\Lambda
, i.e. the involution
\ddagger
, the reality condition is more restrictive. In particular,

\displaystyle r_{1^{\prime}}:

\displaystyle\quad\alpha=\bar{\alpha}\in i\mathbb{R},\beta\in\mathbb{R},\qquad r_{2^{\prime}}:\quad\beta=0,\alpha\in i\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle r_{3^{\prime}}:

\displaystyle\quad\bar{\alpha}=0,\epsilon=\epsilon^{\prime}=1,\beta\in\mathbb{R},\qquad r_{4^{\prime}}:\quad\beta_{1}\in\mathbb{R},\beta_{2}\in i\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle r_{5^{\prime}}:

\displaystyle\quad\text{excluded},\qquad r_{6^{\prime}}:\quad\epsilon=\bar{\epsilon},\beta_{0}\bar{\beta}=-\frac{\beta^{*}}{\beta_{0}^{*}}\text{ or }(\beta_{0}=\bar{\beta}_{0}=0,\beta,\bar{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}),

\displaystyle r_{7^{\prime}}:

\displaystyle\quad\text{excluded},\qquad r_{8^{\prime}}:\quad\beta\in\mathbb{R},\alpha=\bar{\alpha}\in i\mathbb{R}.

As the r-matrices from (
235
)-(
242
) that are not included in (
229
)-(
232
) are at least in
\mathfrak{o}(4)
automorphism orbits containing them, one can always use the inverse of the automorphisms to obtain the full twists. For example,
r=(L_{1}-L_{-1})\wedge(\bar{L}_{1}-\bar{L}_{-1})
(being automorphic to
r_{6^{\prime}}
with
\beta_{0}=\bar{\beta}_{0}=0,\epsilon=\bar{\epsilon}=1
) is automorphic to
L_{0}\wedge\bar{L}_{0}\hat{=}r_{III}
by

\displaystyle\varphi(L_{1})=-\frac{1}{2}(L_{1}+L_{-1})+L_{0},\quad\varphi(L_{-1})=-\frac{1}{2}(L_{1}+L_{-1})-L_{0},\quad\varphi(L_{0})=\frac{1}{2}(L_{1}-L_{-1}).

(245)

From the abelian twist for
r_{III}

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{III}=\exp(\eta L_{0}\wedge\bar{L}_{0})

(246)

one then gets the twist

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}=\left(\varphi^{-1}\otimes\varphi^{-1}\right)\mathcal{F}_{III}=\exp(\eta(L_{1}-L_{-1})\wedge(\bar{L}_{1}-\bar{L}_{-1})).

(247)

Full Deformations via Twist

From section
Drinfeld Twist Deformation
, it is known that all triangular LBA can be extended to a deformation in all orders by a twist satisfying the 2-cocycle condition (
72
). This does not necessarily entail an explicit method of constructing the twists but for (the real forms of)
\mathfrak{o}(4,\mathbb{C})
all of them are given in
[
87
]
. By the final remark in the previous section it follows that the twists corresponding to the r-matrices (
235
)-(
242
) can be obtained.

Abelian Twist

The first example we construct in detail is the abelian twist (see
[
93
]
) corresponding to the r-matrix
r_{III}
from (
232
). Its name is motivated by the fact that the two elements used for its construction are commuting. While there are several other r-matrices with this feature (like (
228
)),
r_{III}
is special in the sense that it is the only r-matrix from elements that are contained in all embeddings. Thus, the abelian twist deformation does not single out any particular embedding.

The full abelian twist can be expressed as

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{A}=\exp\left(\eta\bar{L}_{0}\wedge L_{0}\right)\exp\left(\eta(L_{0}\otimes L_{0}-\bar{L}_{0}\otimes\bar{L}_{0})\right),

(248)

which factorizes into the twist
\mathcal{F}_{3^{\prime\prime}}
from
[
87
]
and a factor that only contributes symmetric deformations of the coproduct. This particular construction is chosen to make contact with the deformations in the flat limit
(\Lambda\rightarrow 0)
. Explicitly, by making use of the Hadamard formula

\displaystyle e^{A}Be^{-A}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\underset{\text{n times}}{[\underbrace{A,[A,...[A,}}B]...]

(249)

for general elements of an algebra
A
and
B
one finds the deformed coproducts

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{3^{\prime\prime}}}(L_{m})=e^{-m\eta\bar{L}_{0}}\otimes L_{m}+L_{m}\otimes e^{m\eta\bar{L}_{0}},

(250)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{3^{\prime\prime}}}(\bar{L}_{m})=e^{m\eta L_{0}}\otimes\bar{L}_{m}+\bar{L}_{m}\otimes e^{-m\eta L_{0}}

(251)

and

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{A}}(L_{m})=e^{-m\eta(\bar{L}_{0}+L_{0})}\otimes L_{m}+L_{m}\otimes e^{m\eta(\bar{L}_{0}-L_{0})}

(252)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{A}}(\bar{L}_{m})=e^{m\eta(\bar{L}_{0}+L_{0})}\otimes\bar{L}_{m}+\bar{L}_{m}\otimes e^{-m\eta(L_{0}-\bar{L}_{0})}

(253)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{A}}(L_{0})=L_{0}\otimes 1+1\otimes L_{0},\quad\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{A}}(\bar{L}_{0})=\bar{L}_{0}\otimes 1+1\otimes\bar{L}_{0}.

(254)

The antipodes can also be inferred easily from

\displaystyle m\circ(S\otimes\text{id})\circ\Delta=1\circ\epsilon

(255)

and turn out to be

\displaystyle S_{\mathcal{F}_{3^{\prime\prime}}}(L_{m})=

\displaystyle-L_{m},

\displaystyle S_{\mathcal{F}_{3^{\prime\prime}}}(\bar{L}_{m})

\displaystyle=-\bar{L}_{m}

(256)

\displaystyle S_{\mathcal{F}_{A}}(L_{m})=

\displaystyle-L_{m}e^{-2m\eta L_{0}},

\displaystyle S_{\mathcal{F}_{A}}(\bar{L}_{m})

\displaystyle=-\bar{L}_{m}e^{2m\eta\bar{L}_{0}}.

(257)

The abelian twist thus induces non-cocommutative coproducts, and consequently the antipodes are non-trivial, except on
L_{0}
and
\bar{L}_{0}
. Before discussing further properties let us consider another example of a twist.

Jordanian Twist

One of the first known examples of twists besides of the abelian twist was the Jordanian twist. In a two dimensional Lie algebra with elements
X,Y
and (the only non-abelian) bracket
[X,Y]=\alpha Y
one has

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}=\exp\left(X\otimes\log\left(1+\alpha Y\right)\right).

(258)

Because the Lorentz sector ( containing (super)rotations and boosts) of all the relevant asymptotic symmetry algebras is semi-simple, there are always Cartan elements, e.g.
L_{0},\bar{L}_{0}
for
\mathfrak{W}\oplus\mathfrak{W}
, that diagonalize the adjoint action and thus have the structure of the two dimensional example. As a result, many of the consistent twists are of this prototypical Jordanian form or have it as a building block.

For the r-matrix
r_{I}
one has

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{J,n}=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{n}(L_{0}+\bar{L}_{0})\otimes\log\left(1+\tilde{a}(L_{n}-\bar{L}_{n})\right)\right),

(259)

so that

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{J,n}}(L_{0})

\displaystyle=L_{0}\otimes 1+1\otimes L_{0}-\frac{1}{n}(L_{0}+\bar{L}_{0})\otimes\frac{d\sigma_{n}}{d(L_{n}-\bar{L}_{n})}nL_{n}

\displaystyle=L_{0}\otimes 1+1\otimes L_{0}-\tilde{a}(L_{0}+\bar{L}_{0})\otimes L_{n}\Pi_{+n}^{-1},

(260)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{J,n}}(\bar{L}_{0})

\displaystyle=\bar{L}_{0}\otimes 1+1\otimes\bar{L}_{0}+\tilde{a}(L_{0}+\bar{L}_{0})\otimes\bar{L}_{n}\Pi_{+n}^{-1},

(261)

where

\displaystyle\sigma_{n}\equiv\log\left(1+\tilde{a}(L_{n}-\bar{L}_{n})\right),\quad\Pi_{+n}=e^{\sigma_{n}}.

(262)

Using (
255
) and the previous equations we find

\displaystyle S_{\mathcal{F}_{J,n}}(L_{0})=

\displaystyle-(L_{0}+\tilde{a}(\bar{L}_{0}L_{n}+L_{0}L_{n}))\frac{\Pi_{+n}^{-1}}{1-\Pi_{+n}^{-1}(L_{n}-\bar{L}_{n})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(L_{0}+\tilde{a}(\bar{L}_{0}L_{n}+L_{0}L_{n})),

(263)

\displaystyle S_{\mathcal{F}_{J,n}}(\bar{L}_{0})=

\displaystyle-(\bar{L}_{0}-\tilde{a}(\bar{L}_{0}\bar{L}_{n}+L_{0}\bar{L}_{n})).

(264)

For general generators we find

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{J,n}}(L_{m})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{J,n}(L_{m}\otimes 1+1\otimes L_{m})\mathcal{F}^{-1}_{J,n}

\displaystyle=L_{m}\otimes\Pi_{+n}^{\frac{m}{n}}+\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{l!}\left(\frac{L_{0}+\bar{L}_{0}}{n}\right)^{l}\otimes\big[\sigma_{n},\big[...,\big[\sigma_{n},L_{m}\big]...\big],

(265)

with

\displaystyle[\sigma_{n},L_{m}]=

\displaystyle\bigg[-\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-\tilde{a})^{j}}{j}(L_{n}-\bar{L}_{n})^{j},L_{m}\bigg]

(266)

\displaystyle\big[\left(L_{n}-\bar{L}_{n}\right)^{j},L_{m}\big]=

\displaystyle\sum_{s=0}^{j-1}(L_{n}-\bar{L}_{n})^{s}(n-m)L_{m+n}(L_{n}-\bar{L}_{n})^{j-s}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{j}\bigg(\sum_{s_{1}=k-1}^{j-1}...\sum_{s_{k}=0}^{s_{k-1}-1}L_{m+kn}(L_{n}-\bar{L}_{n})^{j-k}\prod_{p=0}^{k-1}(n-(m+pn))\bigg),

(267)

where in the last line we iteratively commuted the
s
terms in front of
L_{m+kn}
to the right.
From this formula, it can be seen that in general there is an infinite number of terms in the coproduct involving a tower of infinitely many different generators. In the h-adic topology this is well defined but in the light of the comments in section
Specialization and Classical Limit of the Standard Deformation
one could ask in which cases finite expressions are obtained. We find that this can be achieved when restricting to (two copies of) the one-sided Witt algebra (
\mathfrak{W}_{-}
) spanned by
\{L_{m},\bar{L}_{m};m\leq 1\}
. In that case there are only two possible embeddings with
n=\pm 1
and by choosing
n=1
the sum over
k
in (
266
) terminates after
min
\{1-m,j\}
terms, instead of when
k=j
which is not finite as we sum
j
to infinity. As a consequence also the sum over
l
terminates after
(1-m)
terms, only a finite number of generators appear and as we will see also only a finite number of terms.

In that case we proceed from (
265
) with the identity

\displaystyle\sum_{s_{1}=k-1}^{j-1}...\sum_{s_{k}=0}^{s_{k-1}-1}=\frac{j!}{(j-k)!k!}={j\choose k}.

(268)

It is straightforward to see that it holds for
k=1
. Now assume that it holds for
k=k^{\prime},1\leq k^{\prime}<j
and it follows that the lhs for
k=k^{\prime}+1
is

\displaystyle\sum_{s^{\prime}_{1}=k^{\prime}}^{j-1}\sum_{s^{\prime}_{2}=k^{\prime}-1}^{s^{\prime}_{1}-1}...\sum_{s^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}=0}^{s^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}-1}-1}=\sum_{s^{\prime}_{1}=k^{\prime}}^{j-1}{s^{\prime}_{1}\choose k^{\prime}}={j\choose k^{\prime}+1},

(269)

where in the last step the hockey stick identity was used. Thus, we proved (
268
) by induction.
Therefore,

\displaystyle[\sigma_{1},L_{m}]=

\displaystyle-\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-\tilde{a})^{j}}{j}\big[\left(L_{n}-\bar{L}_{n}\right)^{j},L_{m}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle L_{m}-\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{\text{min}\{j,1-m\}}\frac{(-\tilde{a})^{j}}{j}{j\choose k}L_{m+k}(L_{1}-\bar{L}_{1})^{j-k}\prod_{p=0}^{k-1}(1-(m+p))

(270)

and comparing with

\displaystyle\frac{d^{k}\sigma_{1}}{dL_{1}^{k}}=-\sum_{j=k}^{\infty}\frac{(-\tilde{a})^{j}}{j}\frac{j!}{(j-k)!}(L_{1}-\bar{L}_{1})^{j-k},

(271)

one finds that the summand in (
270
) and

\displaystyle L_{m}+\sum_{k=1}^{1-m}L_{m+k}\frac{d^{k}\sigma_{1}}{dL_{1}^{k}}\frac{1}{k!}\prod_{p=0}^{k-1}(1-(m+p))

(272)

are identical. Splitting the sums in (
270
) and (
272
) according to

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{j=1}^{1-m}\sum_{k=1}^{j}+\sum_{j=2-m}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{1-m}\right)...

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{k=1}^{1-m}\sum_{j=k+1}^{1-m}+\sum_{k=1}^{1-m}\sum_{j=2-m}^{\infty}\right)...

and using

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{1-m}\sum_{k=1}^{j}...=\sum_{k=1}^{1-m}\sum_{j=k}^{1-m}...,

(273)

it follows that (
266
) is indeed given by (
272
) if the one-sided Witt algebra with
n=1
is considered.
Plugging the result into (
265
) yields

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{J,1}}(L_{m})=

\displaystyle L_{m}\otimes\Pi_{+}^{m}+\sum_{l=1}^{1-m}\frac{1}{l!}\bigg(\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{1-m}\frac{1}{k!}\prod_{p_{1}=0}^{k_{1}-1}(1-(m+p_{1}))

\displaystyle\times\bigg(\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{1-(m+k_{1})}\frac{1}{k_{2}!}\prod_{p_{2}=0}^{k_{2}-1}(1-(m+k_{1}+p_{2}))

\displaystyle\times\bigg(...\bigg(\sum_{k_{l}=0}^{1-(m+k_{1}+...k_{l-1})}\frac{1}{k_{l}!}\prod_{p_{l}=0}^{k_{l}-1}(1-(m+k_{1}+...+k_{l-1}+p_{l}))A\otimes B\bigg)...\bigg),

(274)

where

\displaystyle A\otimes B=

\displaystyle\left(L_{0}+\bar{L}_{0}\right)^{l}\otimes L_{m+k_{1}+...+k_{l}}\frac{d^{k_{1}}\sigma_{1}}{dL_{1}^{k_{1}}}...\frac{d^{k_{l}}\sigma_{1}}{dL_{1}^{k_{l}}},

(275)

\displaystyle\frac{d^{k}\sigma_{1}}{dL_{1}^{k}}=

\displaystyle-(-\tilde{a})^{k}\Pi_{+}^{-k}k!

(276)

and all sums are finite for
m\leq 1
. Similarly for
\bar{L}_{m}
one finds

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{J,1}}(\bar{L}_{m})=

\displaystyle\bar{L}_{m}\otimes\Pi_{+}^{m}+\sum_{l=1}^{1-m}\frac{1}{l!}\bigg(\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{1-m}\frac{1}{k!}\prod_{p_{1}=0}^{k_{1}-1}(1-(m+p_{1}))

\displaystyle\times\bigg(\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{1-(m+k_{1})}\frac{1}{k_{2}!}\prod_{p_{2}=0}^{k_{2}-1}(1-(m+k_{1}+p_{2}))

\displaystyle\times\bigg(...\bigg(\sum_{k_{l}=0}^{1-(m+k_{1}+...k_{l-1})}\frac{1}{k_{l}!}\prod_{p_{l}=0}^{k_{l}-1}(1-(m+k_{1}+...+k_{l-1}+p_{l}))\bar{A}\otimes\bar{B}\bigg)...\bigg),

(277)

where

\displaystyle\bar{A}\otimes\bar{B}=\left(L_{0}+\bar{L}_{0}\right)^{l}\otimes\bar{L}_{m+k_{1}+...+k_{l}}\frac{d^{k_{1}}\sigma_{1}}{d\bar{L}_{1}^{k_{1}}}...\frac{d^{k_{l}}\sigma_{1}}{d\bar{L}_{1}^{k_{l}}}.

(278)

Then the antipodes follow from (
255
) and read

\displaystyle S_{\mathcal{F}_{J,1}}(L_{m})=

\displaystyle-\sum_{l=1}^{1-m}\frac{1}{l!}\bigg(\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{1-m}\frac{1}{k!}\prod_{p_{1}=0}^{k_{1}-1}(1-(m+p_{1}))

\displaystyle\times\bigg(\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{1-(m+k_{1})}\frac{1}{k_{2}!}\prod_{p_{2}=0}^{k_{2}-1}(1-(m+k_{1}+p_{2}))

\displaystyle\times\bigg(...\bigg(\sum_{k_{l}=0}^{1-(m+k_{1}+...k_{l-1})}\frac{1}{k_{l}!}\prod_{p_{l}=0}^{k_{l}-1}(1-(m+k_{1}+...+k_{l-1}+p_{l}))S(A)\otimes B\bigg)...\bigg)\Pi_{+}^{-m},

(279)

and
S(A)
can be inferred from (
263
)

\displaystyle S(A)=S((L_{0}+\bar{L}_{0})^{l})=(-(L_{0}+\bar{L}_{0})\Pi_{+})^{l}.

(280)

We summarize that for the full
\mathfrak{W}\oplus\mathfrak{W}
as well as the one-sided
\mathfrak{W}_{-}\oplus\mathfrak{W}_{-}
the coproducts deformed by the Jordanian twist are non-cocommutative for all the generators. In the latter case the algebra and coalgebra relations contain only a finite number of generators.

Specialization and One-sided Witt Algebra

In section
Specialization and Classical Limit of the Standard Deformation
it was argued that physical applications of a Hopf algebra
H
, entailing a numeric value for the deformation parameter, require the so-called specialization of
H
.

Let us study the specialization (or q-analog) on the examples of the abelian and the Jordanian twist respectively. For the abelian twist the formulas (
252
)-(
254
) show that only a finite number of generators appear in the coproducts but there are infinite power series in
a\equiv\frac{i}{\kappa}
. Thus, the full
\mathfrak{W}\oplus\mathfrak{W}
can be turned into the q-analog by adding the elements

\displaystyle e^{aL_{0}}\equiv K,\quad e^{-aL_{0}}\equiv K^{-1},\quad e^{a\bar{L}_{0}}\equiv\bar{K},\quad e^{-a\bar{L}_{0}}\equiv\bar{K}^{-1},

(281)

to the algebra. Furthermore, define
q=e^{a}
and the extra commutation relations become

\displaystyle e^{aL_{0}}L_{m}e^{-aL_{0}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{a^{j}}{j!}[L_{0},[...,[L_{0},L_{m}]...]=e^{-am}L_{m}=q^{-m}L_{m}

(282)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow[K,L_{m}]=

\displaystyle q^{-m}L_{m}e^{aL_{0}}-L_{m}e^{aL_{0}}=(q^{-m}-1)L_{m}K

(283)

and similarly

\displaystyle[K^{-1},L_{m}]=

\displaystyle(q^{m}-1)L_{m}K^{-1},\qquad[K,\bar{L}_{m}]=0,

(284)

\displaystyle[K^{-1},\bar{L}_{m}]=

\displaystyle 0,\qquad[\bar{K},\bar{L}_{m}]=(q^{-m}-1)\bar{L}_{m}\bar{K}

(285)

\displaystyle[\bar{K}^{-1},L_{m}]=

\displaystyle 0,\qquad[\bar{K}^{-1},\bar{L}_{m}]=(q^{m}-1)\bar{L}_{m}\bar{K}^{-1},

(286)

\displaystyle[K,K^{-1}]=

\displaystyle[K,\bar{K}]=[K,\bar{K}^{-1}]=0.

(287)

It is also easy to compute

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{A}}(K)=

\displaystyle K\otimes K,\qquad\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{A}}(K^{-1})=K^{-1}\otimes K^{-1},

(288)

\displaystyle S_{\mathcal{F}_{A}}(K)=

\displaystyle-K,\qquad S_{\mathcal{F}_{A}}(K^{-1})=-K^{-1}

(289)

and reexpressing (
252
)-(
254
) gives

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{A}}(L_{m})=

\displaystyle K^{m}\bar{K}^{m}\otimes L_{m}+L_{m}\otimes K^{-m}\bar{K}^{m},

(290)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{A}}(\bar{L}_{m})=

\displaystyle K^{-m}\bar{K}^{-m}\otimes\bar{L}_{m}+\bar{L}_{m}\otimes K^{m}\bar{K}^{-m}.

(291)

Endowed with this algebra and coalgebra structures the set of polynomials in the generators
\{L_{m},\bar{L}_{m},K,K^{-1},\bar{K},\bar{K}^{-1}\}
does indeed form a q-analog of the twisted Hopf algebra and it can be defined for any
q\in\mathbb{C}
. In particular, the classical limit
\kappa\rightarrow\infty\leftrightarrow q\rightarrow 1
gives simply the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{W}\oplus\mathfrak{W}
, but extended by the central elements
K,\bar{K}
.

In the case of the Jordanian twist the situation is different. We discovered in (
265
) that for
L_{m}
with
m\in\mathbb{Z}
the coproduct contains infinitely many different generators and thus it would be impossible to define a q-analog. However, by restricting to two copies of the one-sided Witt algebra
\mathfrak{W}_{-}
containing
L_{m}
with
m\leq 1
it was shown that all coproducts contain finitely many terms. Similarly, one could use the embedding corresponding to
n=-1
and restrict to
\mathfrak{W}_{+}
containing
L_{m},m\geq-1
. In order to express all algebra and coalgebra relations involving only finite powers of
1/\kappa
the elements
\Pi_{+}
, defined in (
262
), and its inverse
\Pi_{+}^{-1}
are used. The additional commutation relations then read

\displaystyle[\Pi_{+},L_{m}]=

\displaystyle\tilde{a}(1-m)L_{m+1},

(292)

\displaystyle[\Pi_{+}^{-1},L_{m}]=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{\underline{j}}}{j!}\tilde{a}^{j}[(L_{1}-\bar{L}_{1})^{j},L_{m}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{\text{min}\{1-m,j\}}\frac{(-1)^{\underline{j}}}{j!}{j\choose k}L_{m+k}\tilde{a}^{j}(L_{1}-\bar{L}_{1})^{j-k}\left(\prod_{r=0}^{k-1}(1-m-r)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{1-m}L_{m+k}\frac{d^{k}e^{-\sigma_{1}}}{dL_{1}^{k}}\left(\prod_{r=0}^{k-1}(1-m-r)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{1-m}\frac{(-1)^{\underline{k}}}{k!}\tilde{a}^{k}L_{m+k}\Pi_{+}^{k-1}\left(\prod_{r=0}^{k-1}(1-m-r)\right)

(293)

and similarly

\displaystyle[\Pi_{+},\bar{L}_{m}]=

\displaystyle-\tilde{a}(1-m)\bar{L}_{m+1},

(294)

\displaystyle[\Pi_{+}^{-1},\bar{L}_{m}]=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{1-m}\frac{(-1)^{\underline{k}}}{k!}(-\tilde{a})^{k}\bar{L}_{m+k}\Pi_{+}^{k-1}\left(\prod_{r=0}^{k-1}(1-m-r)\right).

(295)

From (
274
) one has in particular

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{J}}(L_{1})=L_{1}\otimes\Pi_{+}+\otimes L_{1},\quad\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{J}}(L_{1})=\bar{L}_{1}\otimes\Pi_{+}+\otimes\bar{L}_{1},

(296)

leading to

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{J}}(\Pi_{+})

\displaystyle=\Pi_{+}\otimes\Pi_{+},\qquad\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{J}}(\Pi_{+}^{-1})=\Pi_{+}^{-1}\otimes\Pi_{+}^{-1},

(297)

\displaystyle S_{\mathcal{F}_{J}}(\Pi_{+})

\displaystyle=-\Pi_{+},\qquad S_{\mathcal{F}_{J}}(\Pi_{+}^{-1})=-\Pi_{+}^{-1}.

(298)

All these formulas are well defined for
\tilde{a}\in\mathbb{C}
and for
\kappa\rightarrow\infty
the elements
\Pi_{+},\Pi_{+}^{-1}
become central. Thus, similar to the abelian twist, the classical limit is the centrally extended Lie algebra
\mathfrak{W}_{-}\oplus\mathfrak{W}_{-}
.

It turns out that all twist deformations except for the abelian twist do not have a q-analog on the full Witt algebra. However, these q-analogs can always be constructed on the one-sided Witt algebras similar to the Jordanian twist if and only if the corresponding twist (or r-matrix) does not contain both
L_{1}
and
L_{-1}
or
\bar{L}_{1}
and
\bar{L}_{-1}
simultaneously. Removing the r-matrices that do not admit a q-analog when quantized yields a set that is still bigger than (
229
)-(
232
). E.g. the r-matrix
r_{4^{\prime}}
can be obtained from
r_{I}
by an
\mathfrak{o}(4)
automorphism of the form

\displaystyle\varphi(L_{0})=\beta L_{1}+\beta^{\prime}L_{0},\quad\varphi(L_{1})=\beta^{\prime\prime}L_{1},\quad\varphi(\bar{L}_{m})=(-1)^{m}\bar{L}_{m}

(299)

but this is not an automorphism of
\mathfrak{W}\oplus\mathfrak{W}
, and also not of
\mathfrak{W}_{-}\oplus\mathfrak{W}_{-}
, as can be seen from the impossibility of solving

\displaystyle[\varphi(L_{0}),\varphi(L_{-2})]=2\varphi(L_{-2}).

(300)

It is not hard to prove (cf. Appendix
Proof of the Cohomology Theorems
), although it does not directly follow from theorem
2
, that also all LBA on
\mathfrak{W}_{+}\oplus\mathfrak{W}_{+}
are coboundary, i.e. we have
12
12

12

The same result obviously also holds for
\mathfrak{W}_{-}\oplus\mathfrak{W}_{-}
.

Theorem 3
.

The first cohomology group
H^{1}\left(\mathfrak{W}_{+}\oplus\mathfrak{W}_{+},\bigwedge^{2}\left(\mathfrak{W}_{+}\oplus\mathfrak{W}_{+}\right)\right)
vanishes.

Some physical consequences of considering one-sided subalgebras will be discussed in section
Decay of Cosmic Strings
.

Deformations of the Surface Charge Algebra

From a physical point of view we are interested in the conserved charges and their algebra is the central extension of
\mathfrak{W}\oplus\mathfrak{W}
given in section
Surface Charges
. A natural question is whether the central charges have any impact on the allowed deformations or on their precise form which will be investigated in the following.

We show in Appendix
Proof of the Cohomology Theorems
that the theorems stated at the beginning of the chapter generalize to the Virasoro algebra, i.e. one has
13
13

13

For one copy of the Virasoro algebra this was already shown in
[
92
]
independently.

Theorem 4
.

The first cohomology group
H^{1}\left(\mathfrak{Vir}\oplus\mathfrak{Vir},\bigwedge^{2}\left(\mathfrak{Vir}\oplus\mathfrak{Vir}\right)\right)
vanishes.

To see how the classification of the r-matrices is affected let us first note that on the entire
\mathfrak{Vir}\oplus\mathfrak{Vir}
there is no automorphism that mixes central elements and other generators. Furthermore, it is easy to see that any

\displaystyle r_{c}=c_{L}\wedge X,

(301)

where
X
is any linear combination in
\mathfrak{W}\oplus\mathfrak{W}
, is triangular. Then we only have to calculate the Schouten brackets of combinations of
r_{c}
with (
235
)-(
242
) and find triangular r-matrices

\displaystyle r=c_{L}\wedge L_{p}+L_{p}\wedge\left(\sum_{q}\bar{L}_{q}\right),\quad r=c_{L}\wedge L_{p}+\bar{r},\quad r=c_{L}\wedge L_{p}+\alpha L_{p}\wedge L_{0},

(302)

where
\bar{r}
is a triangular r-matrix generated by
\bar{L}_{q}
. The only exception is that
a_{1}
with an embedding (
L_{\pm 1}\rightarrow L_{\pm n},n\neq 1
) (and thus r-matrices containing it) is no longer triangular in
\mathfrak{Vir}\oplus\mathfrak{Vir}
, i.e. one has

\displaystyle[[a_{1},a_{1}]]=\frac{(n^{3}-n)}{6}(L_{n}\wedge c_{L}\wedge L_{-n}+L_{n}\wedge c_{L}\wedge L_{0}+L_{-n}\wedge c_{L}\wedge L_{0}).

(303)

The central extension thus can impact the LBAs. For example with
r=c_{L}\wedge L_{p}
we obtain

\displaystyle\delta_{r}(L_{m})=c_{L}\wedge(m-p)L_{m+p},\quad\delta_{r}(\bar{L}_{m})=0.

(304)

But also Lie bialgebras from r-matrices that contain no central element can contribute extra terms, e.g.
r=\chi L_{0}\wedge L_{n}
yields

\displaystyle\delta_{r}(L_{-n})=-n\chi L_{-n}\wedge L_{n}-\chi\frac{(n^{3}-n)}{12}L_{0}\wedge c_{L}.

(305)

For all r-matrices from (
302
) we can write down the twist. This is easy to see in the first two cases as everything is abelian. In the last case note that we can obtain the twist from a Jordanian twist by simply redefining
L_{0}\rightarrow L_{0}-\alpha c_{L}
which leaves
[L_{0},L_{p}]
invariant.

From the twists we can directly compute the coalgebra structures. In general these will contain infinite expressions which, as we did before, could be tried to remedy by considering one-sided algebras. Even though this works in the case of the Witt algebras, with a central extension the one-sided algebra is pointless to consider as the central elements do not appear in the algebra sector. Thus, out of (
302
) only
r=c_{L}\wedge(\chi L_{0}+\bar{\chi}\bar{L}_{0})
leads to a finite coalgebra sector. In particular the twist
\mathcal{F}=\exp(c_{L}\otimes(\chi L_{0}+\bar{\chi}\bar{L}_{0}))
yields

\displaystyle\Delta(L_{m})=L_{m}\otimes 1+\exp(-m\chi c_{L})\otimes L_{m},\quad\Delta(\bar{L}_{m})=\bar{L}_{m}\otimes 1+\exp(-m\bar{\chi}c_{L})\otimes\bar{L}_{m}

(306)

and the specialization is straightforward with adding
\Pi\equiv\exp(\chi c_{L}),\bar{\Pi}\equiv\exp(\bar{\chi}c_{L})
and the respective inverse elements.

Deformations of Asymptotically Flat Symmetries in 3D Gravity

Following the analysis of deformations on asymptotically (anti) de Sitter spacetimes, we will now focus on the flat limit. From a physical intuition one might expect that there is a smooth limit
\Lambda\rightarrow 0
implying that this case is qualitatively similar to
\Lambda\neq 0
. However, as addressed in the beginning of section
Asymptotically (Anti) de Sitter Spacetimes
, there are indeed conceptual differences in the asymptotic analysis of different values for
\Lambda
. Also, from a mathematical perspective the fact that the BMS algebra in three dimensions (
\mathfrak{B}_{3}
) is not semi-simple complicates some of the results from the previous sections.

Lie Bialgebra Structures on
\mathfrak{B}_{3}

As stated above the
\mathfrak{B}_{3}
is not semi-simple but it has the form of a semi-direct product of the semi-simple Witt algebra and the ideal of supertranslations.
Using this fact in combination with theorem
1
of section
Lie Bialgebra Structures on
\mathfrak{W}\oplus\mathfrak{W}
in Appendix
Proof of the Cohomology Theorems
, we prove the following

Theorem 5
.

The first cohomology group
H^{1}(\mathfrak{B}_{3},\bigwedge^{2}\mathfrak{B}_{3})
vanishes.

Additionally, all the results about ad-invariant elements carry over, i.e. one has

\displaystyle\left(\bigwedge^{3}\mathfrak{B}_{3}\right)_{\mathfrak{B}_{3}}=\left(\bigwedge^{2}\mathfrak{B}_{3}\right)_{\mathfrak{B}_{3}}=\{0\}.

(307)

We again conclude that the LBA structures on
\mathfrak{B}_{3}
are uniquely defined by triangular r-matrices classified up to automorphism.

Contraction Limit and Classification of Triangular r-matrices

In order to obtain r-matrices in
\mathfrak{B}_{3}
one can perform a contraction limit
\Lambda\rightarrow 0
on the r-matrices of
\mathfrak{W}\oplus\mathfrak{W}
.
For the finite dimensional embeddings this has been done in
[
94
]
and the results were compared to the classification of r-matrices in the three dimensional Poincaré algebra given by
[
88
]
.

It was claimed that the latter r-matrices can all be obtained from a contraction of
\mathfrak{o}(4,\mathbb{C})
r-matrices. It should be noted that the contraction is ambiguous, i.e. the contraction of a class of r-matrices can be performed in different non-equivalent ways (cf. below) and not injective, i.e. there are r-matrices in the 3D Poincaré that can be obtained as a contraction from distinct
\mathfrak{o}(4)
r-matrices. Whether the contraction is actually surjective is an interesting question because an affirmative answer could grant us a constructive recipe for quantization as we will see below.

Let us examine the previous points by explicitly contracting the r-matrices from (
235
)-(
242
) starting from
r_{1^{\prime}}
. To this end,
r_{1^{\prime}}
is expressed in terms of
l_{m},T_{m}
with the help of (
197
) and subsequently expanded in powers of
1/\sqrt{-\Lambda}

\displaystyle r_{1^{\prime}}=

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha+\bar{\alpha}}{-\Lambda}(T_{1}\wedge T_{0}-T_{1}\wedge T_{-1}-T_{-1}\wedge T_{0})

\displaystyle+\frac{\beta}{\sqrt{-\Lambda}}(l_{1}+l_{-1}+2l_{0})\wedge(T_{1}+T_{-1}+2T_{0})+\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{-\Lambda}}(...)+\beta(...)

(308)

In order to obtain a finite result we have to rescale
(\alpha+\bar{\alpha})\rightarrow(\hat{\alpha}+\hat{\bar{\alpha}})(-\Lambda)
and
\beta\rightarrow\hat{\beta}\sqrt{-\Lambda}
. Then taking the limit
\Lambda\rightarrow 0
yields

\displaystyle\hat{r}_{1^{\prime},a}=(\hat{\alpha}+\hat{\bar{\alpha}})(T_{1}\wedge T_{0}+T_{1}\wedge T_{-1}-T_{-1}\wedge T_{0})+\hat{\beta}(l_{1}+l_{-1}+2l_{0})\wedge(T_{1}+T_{-1}+2T_{0}).

(309)

This is not the only possibility to obtain a finite limit, in the case
\alpha=-\bar{\alpha}
one can also rescale
\alpha\rightarrow\hat{\alpha}\sqrt{-\Lambda}
to get

\displaystyle\hat{r}_{1^{\prime},b}=

\displaystyle\hat{\beta}(l_{1}\wedge T_{1}+l_{-1}\wedge T_{-1}+4l_{0}\wedge T_{0})+(2\hat{\beta}+\hat{\alpha})(l_{1}\wedge T_{0}+l_{0}\wedge T_{-1})

\displaystyle+(2\hat{\beta}-\hat{\alpha})(l_{0}\wedge T_{1}+l_{-1}\wedge T_{0})+(\hat{\beta}+\hat{\alpha})l_{1}\wedge T_{-1}+(\hat{\beta}-\hat{\alpha})l_{-1}\wedge T_{1}.

(310)

Similarly, one obtains the contraction limits for the rest of the r-matrices, i.e.

\displaystyle\hat{r}_{2^{\prime},a}

\displaystyle=(\hat{\alpha}-\hat{\beta})T_{1}\wedge T_{0},

(311)

\displaystyle\hat{r}_{2^{\prime},b}

\displaystyle=-2\hat{\alpha}l_{0}\wedge T_{1},

(312)

with
\alpha=\beta
for
\hat{r}_{2^{\prime},b}
,

\displaystyle\hat{r}_{3^{\prime},a}

\displaystyle=(\hat{\bar{\alpha}}+\hat{\alpha}(1-\epsilon)(1-\epsilon^{\prime})-2\hat{\beta})T_{1}\wedge T_{0}+(\hat{\bar{\alpha}}-\hat{\beta}(1-\epsilon^{\prime}))T_{1}\wedge T_{-1}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+(-\hat{\bar{\alpha}}+\hat{\beta}(\epsilon-2\epsilon^{\prime}))T_{-1}\wedge T_{0},

(313)

\displaystyle\hat{r}_{3^{\prime},b}

\displaystyle=\hat{r}_{1^{\prime},b}(\hat{\alpha}=0),

(314)

with
\bar{\alpha}=0
for
\hat{r}_{3^{\prime},b}
(if
\epsilon=\epsilon^{\prime}=1\Rightarrow\hat{r}_{3^{\prime},a}\subset\hat{r}_{1^{\prime},a}
),

\displaystyle\hat{r}_{4^{\prime},a}

\displaystyle=\hat{\beta}_{1}l_{1}\wedge T_{1}+\hat{\beta}_{2}l_{1}\wedge l_{0},

(315)

\displaystyle\hat{r}_{5^{\prime},a}

\displaystyle=(\hat{\alpha}-\hat{\beta})T_{1}\wedge T_{0}+(\hat{\alpha}+\hat{\beta})T_{1}\wedge T_{-1}-(\hat{\alpha}+\hat{\beta})T_{-1}\wedge T_{0},

(316)

\displaystyle\hat{r}_{6^{\prime},a}

\displaystyle=(\hat{\beta}T_{1}+\hat{\beta}_{0}T_{0}+\epsilon\hat{\beta}T_{-1})\wedge(\hat{\bar{\beta}}T_{1}+\hat{\bar{\beta}_{0}}T_{0}+\bar{\epsilon}\hat{\bar{\beta}}T_{-1}),

(317)

\displaystyle\hat{r}_{6^{\prime},b}

\displaystyle=(\hat{\beta}l_{1}+\hat{\beta}l_{-1}+\hat{\beta}_{0}l_{0})\wedge(\hat{\beta}T_{1}+\hat{\beta}T_{-1}+\hat{\beta}_{0}T_{0}),

(318)

with
\beta_{0}=\bar{\beta}_{0},\epsilon=\bar{\epsilon},\beta=\bar{\beta}
for
\hat{r}_{6^{\prime},b}
,

\displaystyle\hat{r}_{7^{\prime},a}

\displaystyle=(\hat{\beta}+\hat{\alpha})T_{1}\wedge T_{0}+\epsilon\hat{\beta}T_{1}\wedge T_{-1},

(319)

\displaystyle\hat{r}_{7^{\prime},b}

\displaystyle=\hat{\alpha}T_{1}\wedge(l_{1}+l_{0}),

(320)

with
\epsilon=0,\bar{\beta}=\alpha
for
\hat{r}_{7^{\prime},b}
,

\displaystyle\hat{r}_{8^{\prime},a}

\displaystyle=(\hat{\alpha}+\hat{\bar{\alpha}})T_{1}\wedge T_{0}-2\hat{\beta}l_{1}\wedge T_{1},

(321)

\displaystyle\hat{r}_{8^{\prime},b}

\displaystyle=-\hat{\beta}l_{1}\wedge T_{1}+\hat{\alpha}(l_{1}\wedge T_{0}-l_{0}\wedge T_{1}),

(322)

with
\alpha=-\bar{\alpha}
for
\hat{r}_{8^{\prime},b}
.
One easily checks that the reality condition is satisfied under the involution

\displaystyle l_{m}\mapsto-l_{m},\quad T_{m}\mapsto-T_{m},

(323)

if the parameter restrictions from
\dagger
are adopted but not necessarily for those from
\ddagger
. In particular, the r-matrices corresponding to the Jordanian and extended Jordanian twist (
\hat{r}_{2^{\prime},b},\hat{r}_{8^{\prime},b}(\hat{\beta}=0)
) are incompatible with the restrictions listed in section
Classification of Triangular r-matrices
for
\ddagger
.

The contraction results can be compared to the classification of r-matrices in
\mathfrak{P}_{3}
up to
\mathfrak{P}_{3}
automorphisms from
[
88
]
. Considering only the triangular parts these read
14
14

14

After removing the quasitriangular part, the r-matrices
r_{2},r_{3}
are
\mathfrak{P}_{3}
-automorphic but this does not extend to
\mathfrak{B}_{3}
.
,

\displaystyle r_{1}

\displaystyle=l_{0}\wedge(l_{1}-l_{-1})-l_{1}\wedge l_{-1},

(324)

\displaystyle r_{2}

\displaystyle=l_{0}\wedge T_{0}+T_{0}\wedge(\Theta_{1}T_{1}+\Theta_{-1}T_{-1}),

(325)

\displaystyle r_{3}

\displaystyle=(l_{1}-l_{-1})\wedge(T_{1}-T_{-1})+T_{0}\wedge(\Theta_{1}T_{1}+\Theta_{-1}T_{-1}),

(326)

\displaystyle r_{4}

\displaystyle=\frac{\chi}{2}(l_{1}\wedge T_{0}-l_{0}\wedge T_{1})-\frac{\zeta}{2}l_{1}\wedge T_{1}+T_{1}\wedge(\Theta_{1}T_{0}+\Theta_{0}T_{-1})+\delta_{\zeta,\frac{\chi}{2}}\Theta_{-1}T_{0}\wedge T_{-1},

(327)

\displaystyle r_{5}

\displaystyle=l_{0}\wedge T_{1}+T_{1}\wedge(\Theta_{1}T_{0}+\Theta_{0}T_{-1}).

(328)

Following the argumentation from section
Classification of Triangular r-matrices
we are interested in r-matrices up to
\text{Aut}(\mathfrak{B}_{3})
and we see that e.g. the r-matrix
\hat{r}_{1^{\prime},b}
is in fact not
\mathfrak{B}_{3}
-automorphic to any r-matrix from (
324
)-(
328
) as e.g. the term
l_{0}\wedge T_{0}
is not contained there. This term could only be obtained from
r_{3}
by a
\mathfrak{P}_{3}
automorphism.
As we will see in section
Four Dimensional Case
, terms in the r-matrices of the form
T\wedge T
,
l\wedge T
and
l\wedge l
correspond to canonical, Lie algebra type and quadratic non-commutativity, respectively.

Figure 3:
Schematic depiction of r-matrices in three dimensional asymptotic symmetry algebras. The red arrow represents the quotient
\text{Aut}(\mathfrak{o}(4))/\text{Aut}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{W}\oplus\mathfrak{W})
and the green arrow
\text{Aut}(\mathfrak{P}_{3})/\text{Aut}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{B}_{3})
. All the r-matrices inside the yellow and blue circle are triangular.

Surjectivity of the Contraction Limit

To address the question of surjectivity, i.e. whether all triangular
\mathfrak{B}_{3}
r-matrices can be obtained from a contraction limit of a triangular
\mathfrak{o}(4)
r-matrix, there are two more aspects that have to be taken into account. First, there might be quasitriangular r-matrices in
\mathfrak{o}(4)
that contract to triangular
\mathfrak{B}_{3}
r-matrices. As shown in
[
94
]
this is indeed happening but in this case there is always also a triangular
\mathfrak{o}(4)
r-matrix with the same limit.
Secondly, it is possible to perform the contraction limit along different axis as explained in the following.
The (anti) de Sitter algebra (
189
)-(
191
) is isomorphic to

\displaystyle[M_{AB},M_{CD}]=\delta_{AC}M_{BD}-\delta_{BC}M_{AD}+\delta_{BD}M_{AC}-\delta_{AD}M_{BC},

(329)

where the indices range from
1
to
4
and

\displaystyle M_{+-}=M_{13},\quad M_{+2}=M_{12}+M_{32},\quad M_{-2}=M_{12}-M_{32},

(330)

\displaystyle K_{\pm}=M_{14}\pm M_{34},\quad K_{2}=M_{24},

(331)

i.e. the fourth axis is chosen for contraction. With the isomorphism

\displaystyle H=

\displaystyle-\frac{i}{2}(M_{12}+M_{34}),\quad\bar{H}=\frac{i}{2}(M_{12}-M_{34}),

(332)

\displaystyle E_{\pm}=

\displaystyle-\frac{i}{2}(M_{23}+M_{14})\mp\frac{1}{2}(M_{24}-M_{13}),

(333)

\displaystyle\bar{E}_{\pm}=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{2}(M_{23}-M_{14})\mp\frac{1}{2}(-M_{24}-M_{13}),

(334)

one can express the r-matrices in terms of
M_{AB}
. Depending on which axis is chosen the result of the contraction differs, e.g. when choosing the second instead of the fourth axis

\displaystyle K_{i}=M_{i2},\quad J_{i}=\epsilon_{ijk}M_{jk}

(335)

one finds for

\displaystyle r=(E_{+}+\bar{E}_{+})\wedge(H+\bar{H}),

(336)

which is automorphic to
r_{I}
,

\displaystyle r=\frac{i}{2}(iJ_{3}+J_{4})\wedge J_{1},

(337)

where the contraction limit can be taken without rescaling. The
J_{i}
satisfy

\displaystyle[J_{1},J_{3}]=J_{4},\quad[J_{1},J_{3}]=J_{4},\quad[J_{3},J_{4}]=J_{1}

(338)

and can thus be related to the three dimensional Lorentz sector via

\displaystyle J_{3}=-l_{0},\quad J_{1}=\frac{l_{1}+l_{-1}}{2},\quad J_{4}=\frac{l_{1}-l_{-1}}{2},

(339)

leading to

\displaystyle\hat{r}=l_{1}\wedge l_{-1}+l_{0}\wedge(l_{1}+l_{-1}).

(340)

Note that this r-matrix of the
\mathfrak{P}_{3}
can not be obtained from a contraction of (
235
)-(
242
) which are associated with the fourth axis, so the consideration of different axis actually makes a difference.
But even after taking into account the possibility to contract along different axis it turns out that there are triangular r-matrices in
\mathfrak{P}_{3}
, e.g.

\displaystyle r=l_{0}\wedge T_{1}+\Theta_{1}T_{1}\wedge T_{0}+\Theta_{2}T_{1}\wedge T_{-1},

(341)

that can
not
be obtained in its general form from a contraction of a triangular r-matrix. It can, however, be obtained from the sum of two different contraction limits, a possibility also mentioned in
[
94
]
.

To summarize, in this weak sense (including sums of contractions and contractions along different axis) all r-matrices in
\bigwedge^{2}\mathfrak{P}_{3}
up to
\text{Aut}(\mathfrak{P}_{3}
) (the intersection of the green and dark-blue circle in figure
3
) can be obtained from a contraction of r-matrices from
\bigwedge^{2}\mathfrak{o}(4)
up to
\text{Aut}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{W}\oplus\mathfrak{W})
(the orange circle in figurefig4.1). In fact these contractions form a larger set as we saw with explicit examples. The full set represented by the light-blue circle in figure
3
could in principle be derived using the quotient of automorphisms
\text{Aut}(\mathfrak{P}_{3})/\text{Aut}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{B}_{3})
or alternatively by redoing the classification using only
\text{Aut}(\mathfrak{B}_{3}
. Since we did not explicitly carry out these procedures, it remains an open question whether the contraction of
r\in\bigwedge^{2}\mathfrak{o}(4)
up to
\text{Aut}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{W}\oplus\mathfrak{W})
is surjective.

Contraction of Twists

As stated above, the surjectivity might be relevant for obtaining the associated Hopf algebras. Namely there is the possibility in performing the contraction limit on the level of the full twist
[
96
]
. As an example let us consider the light-cone twist (the name will be justified later)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\text{LC}}=e^{L_{0}\otimes\log\left(1+aL_{n}\right)}e^{\bar{L}_{0}\otimes\log\left(1-a\bar{L}_{n}\right)}

(342)

corresponding to (
230
) with
a=\chi=-\chi,\zeta=0
. We express the
L_{m},\bar{L}_{m}
in terms of
l_{m},T_{m}

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\text{LC}}=

\displaystyle\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(l_{0}+\frac{T_{0}}{\sqrt{-\Lambda}}\right)\otimes\log\left(1+\frac{a}{2}\left(l_{n}+\frac{T_{n}}{\sqrt{-\Lambda}}\right)\right)\right)

\displaystyle\times\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(l_{0}-\frac{T_{0}}{\sqrt{-\Lambda}}\right)\otimes\log\left(1-\frac{a}{2}\left(l_{n}-\frac{T_{n}}{\sqrt{-\Lambda}}\right)\right)\right).

(343)

In order to obtain a finite contraction limit we have to rescale
a\rightarrow a^{\prime}=a/\sqrt{-\Lambda}
. Expanding the first exponent of the twist in powers of
\sqrt{-\Lambda}
and taking the limit then results in

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(l_{0}+\frac{T_{0}}{\sqrt{-\Lambda}}\right)

\displaystyle\otimes\sum_{j=1}\frac{-(-a^{\prime})^{j}}{j}\left(\sqrt{-\Lambda}l_{n}+T_{n}\right)^{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}l_{0}\otimes\log(1+a^{\prime}T_{n})+\frac{1}{2}T_{0}\otimes\sum_{j=1}\frac{-(-a^{\prime})^{j}}{j}jl_{n}T_{n}^{j-1}+\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{-\Lambda})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}l_{0}\otimes\log(1+a^{\prime}T_{n})+\frac{1}{2}T_{0}\otimes l_{n}a^{\prime}\left(1+a^{\prime}T_{n}\right)^{-1}.

(344)

After repeating this procedure for the second exponent the final twist is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\text{LC}}=\exp\left(l_{0}\otimes\log(1+a^{\prime}T_{n})+T_{0}\otimes a^{\prime}l_{n}\left(1+a^{\prime}T_{n}\right)\right).

(345)

It automatically satisfies the 2-cocycle condition in
\mathfrak{B}_{3}
since the twist (
343
) satisfies it in
\mathfrak{W}\oplus\mathfrak{W}
. The associated classical r-matrix turns out to be

\displaystyle r_{\text{LC}}=a^{\prime}(l_{0}\wedge T_{n}-l_{n}\wedge T_{0}),

(346)

which corresponds indeed to the three dimensional version of the light-cone
\kappa
-Poincaré (for some embedding).

But such a simple procedure is not always possible. As an example, we could try to use the same rescaling for the twist of
r_{2^{\prime}}
as for the r-matrix
\hat{r}_{2^{\prime},b}
. The
\mathfrak{o}(4)
twist for
r_{2^{\prime}}
reads
[
87
]

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}=\exp\left(-\frac{\beta}{\alpha}\bar{L}_{0}\wedge\log(1+\alpha L_{1})\right)\exp\left(-L_{0}\otimes\log(1+\alpha L_{1})\right)

(347)

and after rescaling we obtain in first order in the deformation parameters

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}=1\otimes 1+\frac{1}{\sqrt{-\Lambda}}(\hat{\beta}T_{0}\wedge T_{1}-\hat{\alpha}T_{0}\otimes T_{1})+\mathcal{O}(1),

(348)

i.e. the result diverges because the relevant terms only cancel in the antisymmetrized r-matrix but not in the twist. Even though we know the twist for
\hat{r}_{2^{\prime},b}
(being just a Jordanian twist), this illustrates the limitations of the contraction on the level of the twist.
In particular, we notice that for r-matrices of the form
l\wedge T+T\wedge T
this means, together with the fact that some r-matrices of this form can only be expressed as a sum of different contractions
15
15

15

Note that there is also no
\mathfrak{B}_{3}
automorphism that mixes superrotations and -translations after the contraction.
, that there is no simple recipe to obtain all the twists for the LBA’s on
\mathfrak{P}_{3}
up to
\mathfrak{B}_{3}
automorphisms. However, it is possible to explicitly write down the twists for r-matrices of the form
l\wedge T
. In the classification (
324
)-(
328
) this remains to be shown only for
r_{4}(\Theta_{1/0/-1}=0)\hat{=}r_{8^{\prime},b}
, other twists for r-matrices that are not equivalent in
\mathfrak{B}_{3}
can always be obtained from a
\mathfrak{P}_{3}
automorphism. For
r_{8^{\prime},b}
one can contract the twist of
r_{8^{\prime}}\hat{=}r_{II}
, which is given by
[
87
]

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}=e^{\frac{\beta}{-\alpha^{2}}\log\left(1+\alpha L_{1}\right)\wedge\log\left(1-\alpha\bar{L}_{1}\right)}e^{L_{0}\otimes\log\left(1+\alpha L_{1}\right)}e^{\bar{L}_{0}\otimes\log\left(1-\alpha\bar{L}_{1}\right)}.

(349)

Apart from the first exponent, this is just our example (
342
) and the contraction is done analogously. The first exponent becomes simply
\exp\left(2\hat{\beta}l_{1}\wedge T_{1}\right)
and thus

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{r_{8^{\prime},b}}=\exp\left(2\hat{\beta}l_{1}\wedge T_{1}\right)\exp\left(l_{0}\otimes\log(1+\hat{\alpha}T_{1})+T_{0}\otimes\hat{\alpha}l_{1}\left(1+\hat{\alpha}T_{1}\right)\right).

(350)

Uniqueness of Twist Deformation

In the previous subsection a twist for the light-cone r-matrix (
346
) was found via contraction. From
[
63
]
(cf. also
[
36
]
,
[
97
]
) it is known that the extended Jordanian twist of the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\text{eJ}}=\exp\left(\frac{a^{\prime\prime}}{2}l_{n}\otimes T_{0}\right)\exp\left(-\frac{l_{0}}{n}\otimes\log\left(1+a^{\prime\prime}nT_{n}\right)\right)

(351)

exists for the same r-matrix. However, the inequivalence is only superficial as we have to take into account automorphisms on the universal envelope. We find there exist invertible elements
\omega\in U\mathfrak{B}_{3}[[1/\kappa]]
inducing the automorphisms

\displaystyle f(l_{m})=\omega^{-1}l_{m}\omega,\quad f(T_{m})=\omega^{-1}T_{m}\omega

(352)

by a similarity transformation (see
[
99
]
and references therein for a detailed discussion). In general for every twist deformed Hopf algebra with
\mathcal{F}
one can obtain a “gauge equivalent” twist via
\mathcal{F}_{\omega}=\omega^{-1}\otimes\omega^{-1}\mathcal{F}\Delta(\omega)
. The new twist then satisifies the 2-cocycle condition because

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}_{\omega}}_{12}(\Delta\otimes 1)(\mathcal{F}_{\omega})

\displaystyle=(\omega^{-1}\otimes\omega^{-1})F_{12}(\Delta(\omega)\otimes\omega)\Delta(\omega^{-1})\otimes\omega^{-1})(\Delta\otimes 1)\mathcal{F}(\Delta(\omega)\otimes\omega)

\displaystyle=(\omega^{-1}\otimes\omega^{-1})\mathcal{F}_{23}(1\otimes\Delta)\mathcal{F}(\Delta(\omega)\otimes\omega)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{F}_{\omega}}_{23}(1\otimes\Delta)\mathcal{F}_{\omega}

(353)

and
f(x)\equiv\omega x\omega^{-1}
establishes the isomorphism between the twisted Hopf algebras

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}}\circ f=(f\otimes f)\circ\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{\omega}}.

(354)

If the untwisted Hopf algebra admits a
*
-structure, the twist has to satisfy

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{*\otimes*}=\mathcal{F}^{-1},

(355)

i.e. be unitary in order to preserve the
*
-structure. On the invertible element
\omega
this enforces the unitarity condition

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\omega}^{*\otimes*}

\displaystyle=\Delta(\omega^{*})\mathcal{F}^{-1}({\omega^{*}}^{-1}\otimes{\omega^{*}}^{-1})\overset{!}{=}\mathcal{F}_{\omega}^{-1}=\Delta(\omega^{-1})\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\omega\otimes\omega)

(356)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\omega^{*}

\displaystyle=\omega^{-1}.

(357)

In our particular example we find that in first order of
1/\kappa
the isomorphism induced from the element

\displaystyle\omega=e^{-\frac{a^{\prime}}{4}(l_{n}T_{0}+T_{0}l_{n})}

(358)

relates the extended Jordanian and the contraction limit of the light-cone twist for
a^{\prime\prime}=-a^{\prime}
. It is easy to see that it is hermitian with respect to the reality condition
l_{m}^{*}=-l_{m},T_{m}^{*}=-T_{m}
and
a^{\prime}\in i\mathbb{R}
.

Such a resolution as a “gauge equivalence” of the apparent ambiguity is in line with the statement that the full deformation is already determined by the first order quoted at the end of section
Drinfeld Twist Deformation
.

Full Deformations via Twist

Even though we did not find a general recipe for all twists, let us construct and examine the full quantum group structures in some example cases where it is possible.
A special focus will again be put on the possibility of finding a q-analog. We also noted in section
\kappa
-Poincaré and Non-commutative Geometry
that the fate of the light-like
\kappa
-Poincaré is of interest as it has been studied in the finite dimensional Poincaré algebra.

Abelian Twist

There is again a unique abelian r-matrix, i.e.
r_{2}(\Theta=0)
from eq.(
325
