), that does not single out any embedding as its constituents are present in each of the embeddings. It is associated with the twist

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{A}=\exp\left(\eta l_{0}\otimes T_{0}\right),

(359)

which does not come from a contraction of a
\mathfrak{o}(4)
twist but the construction is straightforward since it is abelian. The twisting element (
359
) induces the coalgebra relations

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{A}}(l_{m})

\displaystyle=1\otimes l_{m}-m\eta l_{0}\otimes T_{m}+l_{m}\otimes\exp\left(-m\eta T_{0}\right),

(360)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{A}}(T_{m})

\displaystyle=1\otimes T_{m}+T_{m}\otimes\exp\left(-m\eta T_{0}\right).

(361)

Because the coproduct is cocommutative (only for)
l_{0},T_{0}
we infer

\displaystyle S_{\mathcal{F}_{A}}(l_{0})=-l_{0},\quad S_{\mathcal{F}_{A}}(T_{0})=-T_{0}

(362)

and then

\displaystyle S_{\mathcal{F}_{A}}(l_{m})=-(l_{m}+m\eta l_{0}T_{m})e^{m\eta T_{0}},\quad S_{\mathcal{F}_{A}}(T_{m})=-T_{m}e^{m\eta T_{0}}.

(363)

One can see that the qualitative behavior is very similar to the abelian twist in the
\mathfrak{W}\oplus\mathfrak{W}
algebra (eq.(
248
) and following).

Jordanian and Extended Jordanian Twist

In the following, we will first construct a Jordanian twist in
\mathfrak{B}_{3}
since a lot of the consistent twists are either of this form or have it as a building block, which we already noted in section
Full Deformations via Twist
. Furthermore, the extended Jordanian twist will be investigated as it will turn out to exhibit qualitatively different behavior and also because of its relevance in the context of
\kappa
-Poincaré (cf. section
\kappa
-Poincaré and Non-commutative Geometry
and
Dual Lie Bialgebras and Non-commutative Spacetime
).

For the r-matrix
r_{5}
from (
328
) with
\Theta_{1/0}=0
one obtains the Jordanian twist

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{J}=\exp\left(-\frac{l_{0}}{n}\otimes\log\left(1+\chi T_{n}\right)\right),

(364)

leading to the coproducts

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{J}}(l_{m})

\displaystyle=1\otimes l_{m}-\frac{n-m}{n}\chi l_{0}\otimes T_{m+n}\Pi_{+n}^{-1}+l_{m}\otimes\Pi_{+n}^{\frac{m}{n}},

(365)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{J}}(T_{m})

\displaystyle=1\otimes T_{m}+T_{m}\otimes\Pi_{+n}^{\frac{m}{n}},

(366)

where

\displaystyle\Pi_{+n}=\left(1+\chi T_{n}\right).

(367)

From

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{J}}(l_{0})=1\otimes l_{0}+l_{0}\otimes(1-\chi T_{n})\Pi_{+n}^{-1},\quad\rightarrow S_{\mathcal{F}_{J}}(l_{0})=-l_{0}(1-\chi T_{n})\Pi_{+n}^{-1},

(368)

we infer

\displaystyle S_{\mathcal{F}_{J}}(l_{m})

\displaystyle=-(l_{m}-\frac{n-m}{n}\chi(1-\chi T_{n})T_{m+n}\Pi_{+n}^{-2})\Pi_{+n}^{-\frac{m}{n}},

(369)

\displaystyle S_{\mathcal{F}_{J}}(T_{m})

\displaystyle=-T_{m}\Pi_{+n}^{-\frac{m}{n}}.

(370)

We can already note that, in contrast to the asymptotically (A)dS symmetry, the coalgebra structures of the Jordanian deformation only involve a finite number of generators for the entire algebra. As a consequence there is no necessity to restrict to a subset of the algebra and thereby to a specific embedding.

For the light-cone deformation described by the r-matrix (
346
) it was already noted that there are two possibilities for a twist. To perform the calculations we choose the extended Jordanian twist

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{eJ}=\exp\left(\frac{\chi}{n}l_{n}\otimes T_{0}\right)\mathcal{F}_{J}

(371)

which, as the name suggests, also contains the Jordanian twist. Using again the Hadamard formula we calculate

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{eJ}}(l_{m})=

\displaystyle\exp\left(\frac{\chi}{n}l_{n}\otimes T_{0}\right)\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{J}}(l_{m})\exp\left(-\frac{\chi}{n}l_{n}\otimes T_{0}\right),

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{J}}(l_{m})-\frac{m}{n}\chi l_{n}\otimes T_{m}+\frac{n-m}{n}\chi^{2}l_{n}\otimes T_{0}T_{m+n}\Pi_{+n}^{-1}

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\chi^{k}}{n^{k}k!}\prod_{p=0}^{k-1}(n-(m+pn))l_{m+kn}\otimes T_{0}^{k}\Pi_{+n}^{\frac{m}{n}},

(372)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{eJ}}(T_{m})=

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{J}}(T_{m})+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\chi^{k}}{n^{k}k!}\prod_{p=0}^{k-1}(n-(m+pn))T_{m+kn}\otimes T_{0}^{k}\Pi_{+n}^{\frac{m}{n}}.

(373)

If there is no
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
n=m+kn
there appear infinite power series that also contain infinitely many different generators. Excluding either
m>1
and choosing
n=1
or
m<-1
with
n=-1
solves the latter problem as the sum over
k
in (
372
)-(
373
) terminates. Choosing the former then results in finite coproducts, i.e. the sums in (
372
) and (
373
) terminate at
k=1-m
(for
m=1
the sum is set to zero) which also allows to iteratively compute the antipodes from

\displaystyle S_{eJ}(l_{m})=

\displaystyle-\left(l_{m}+(1-m)\chi l_{0}T_{m+1}\Pi_{+}^{-1}+m\chi l_{1}T_{m}-(1-m)\chi^{2}l_{1}T_{0}T_{m+1}\Pi_{+}^{-1}\right)\Pi_{+}^{-m}

\displaystyle-\sum_{k=1}^{1-m}\frac{\chi^{k}}{k!}\prod_{p=0}^{k-1}(1-(m+p))S(l_{m+k})T_{0}^{k},

(374)

\displaystyle S_{eJ}(T_{m})=

\displaystyle-T_{m}\Pi_{+}^{-m}-\sum_{k=1}^{1-m}\frac{\chi^{k}}{k!}\prod_{p=0}^{k-1}(1-(m+p))S(T_{m+k})T_{0}^{k}.

(375)

Because the
T_{i}
form an abelian ideal all the formulas for the Jordanian twist remain finite and all embeddings are possible. The extended Jordanian twist on the other hand behaves more like the Jordanian twist in
\mathfrak{W}\oplus\mathfrak{W}
as the one-sided algebra has to be chosen to obtain finite coalgebra structures.

As in section
Specialization and One-sided Witt Algebra
, the theorem
5
has a one-sided analogon proven in Appendix
Proof of the Cohomology Theorems
.

Theorem 6
.

The first cohomology group
H^{1}\left(\mathfrak{B}_{3+},\bigwedge^{2}\mathfrak{B}_{3+}\right)
vanishes.

Specialization

The specialization of the Hopf algebra deformed by the abelian twist can be performed analogously to section
Specialization and One-sided Witt Algebra
. This means we add the elements

\displaystyle K\equiv e^{\eta T_{0}},\quad K^{-1}\equiv e^{-\eta T_{0}}

(376)

to the algebra and then the coalgebra structures can be reformulated as

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{A}}(l_{m})

\displaystyle=1\otimes l_{m}-m\eta l_{0}\otimes T_{m}+l_{m}\otimes K^{-m},

(377)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{A}}(T_{m})

\displaystyle=1\otimes T_{m}+T_{m}\otimes K^{-m},

(378)

\displaystyle S_{\mathcal{F}_{A}}(l_{m})

\displaystyle=-(l_{m}+m\eta l_{0}T_{m})K^{m},\quad S_{\mathcal{F}_{A}}(T_{m})=-T_{m}K^{m}

(379)

and

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{A}}(K)=K\otimes K,\quad\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{A}}(K^{-1})=K^{-1}\otimes K^{-1}.

(380)

In the algebraic sector the extra relations read

\displaystyle[l_{m},K]=-m\eta T_{m}K,\quad[l_{m},K^{-1}]=m\eta T_{m}K^{-1},

(381)

\displaystyle[T_{m},K]=[T_{m},K^{-1}]=[K,K^{-1}]=0

(382)

and everything is well defined for all complex
\eta
so we can set
\eta=\frac{i}{\kappa}
. In the classical limit
\kappa\rightarrow\infty\leftrightarrow\eta\rightarrow 0
the elements
K,K^{-1}
become central.

When deforming the primitive Hopf algebra with a Jordanian twist only a finite number of generators appear. However, the coproducts (
365
)-(
366
) involve fractional powers in
\Pi_{+n}
. Thus, after singling out an embedding for the twist we add the elements

\displaystyle\Pi\equiv\Pi_{+n}^{\frac{1}{n}},\qquad\Pi^{-1}\equiv\Pi_{+n}^{-\frac{1}{n}}

(383)

to the algebra and the coalgebra sector can be expressed using only finite powers

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{J}}(l_{m})

\displaystyle=1\otimes l_{m}-\frac{n-m}{n}\chi l_{0}\otimes T_{m+n}\Pi^{-n}+l_{m}\otimes\Pi^{m},

(384)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{J}}(T_{m})

\displaystyle=1\otimes T_{m}+T_{m}\otimes\Pi^{m}.

(385)

Furthermore we have

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{J}}(\Pi)=\Pi\otimes\Pi,\qquad\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{J}}(\Pi^{-1})=\Pi^{-1}\otimes\Pi^{-1}.

(386)

Adding the fractional powers of
\Pi_{+n}
comes at the cost of slightly more complicated algebraic relations

\displaystyle[\Pi^{\frac{1}{n}}_{+n},l_{m}]=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(1/n)^{\underline{j}}}{j!}\chi^{j}[T_{n}^{j},l_{m}]=(n-m)T_{m+n}\frac{d\Pi_{+n}^{\frac{1}{n}}}{dT_{n}}=\chi\frac{(n-m)}{n}T_{m+n}\Pi^{\frac{1}{n}-1}_{+n},

(387)

\displaystyle[\Pi_{+n}^{-\frac{1}{n}},l_{m}]=

\displaystyle-\chi\frac{(n-m)}{n}T_{n+m}\Pi_{+n}^{-\frac{1}{n}-1},

(388)

\displaystyle[\Pi^{\frac{1}{n}}_{+n},T_{m}]=

\displaystyle[\Pi_{+n}^{-\frac{1}{n}},T_{m}]=0,

(389)

but all formulas are well defined for general complex
\chi
. Again, the classical limit
\chi\rightarrow 0
results in central
\Pi,\Pi^{-1}
.

We thus find that the q-analog can be constructed on the full algebra for this particular twist deformation which contains elements with non-zero index in contrast to the situation in
\mathfrak{W}\oplus\mathfrak{W}
where this was true only for the deformation defined by the abelian
r\sim L_{0}\wedge\bar{L}_{0}
. In section
Contraction Limit and Classification of Triangular r-matrices
we saw that while the r-matrix corresponding to
\mathcal{F}_{J}
can be obtained as a contraction limit from the triangular
r_{2^{\prime}}\in\bigwedge^{2}\left(\mathfrak{W}\oplus\mathfrak{W}\right)
, the same limit does not work on the level of the twist. Such a result is compatible with the different behavior in the specialization of the Jordanian twist and the twist for
r_{2^{\prime}}
. Again, we conclude that the flat case is more than just the contraction of (A)dS but in this example the difference appeared only at the level of the full quantum group.

For the extended Jordanian twist , implementing the light-like
\kappa
-Poincaré, it is obvious that one has to restrict to the one-sided BMS algebra (
\mathfrak{B}_{3,-}
), generated by
\{l_{m},T_{p}|m,p\leq 1\}
(or
\mathfrak{B}_{3,+}
), to avoid infinite series in the deformed coproducts. Adding the elements from (
383
) to the algebra one can reexpress (
372
)-(
373
) so that it contains finite powers in the deformation parameter. As we added the same elements as for the Jordanian twist, the extra relation for the algebra sector are again (
387
)-(
389
).

Deformations of the Surface Charge Algebra

When considering the algebra of the surface charge generators in asymptotically flat three dimensional spacetime
\mathfrak{B}_{3,c}
we, somewhat surprisingly, find (proved in Appendix
Proof of the Cohomology Theorems
)

Theorem 7
.

The first cohomology group
H^{1}(\mathfrak{B}_{3,c},\bigwedge^{2}\mathfrak{B}_{3,c})
contains only the cocycle
\delta_{\alpha}
.

Here,

\displaystyle\delta_{\alpha}:l_{m}\mapsto 0,\;T_{m}\mapsto\alpha c^{\prime}_{L}\wedge T_{m},

(390)

and this cocycle clearly defines a Lie bialgebra as the co-Jacobi identity is trivially satisfied. This result pinpoints a clear situation where the flat case can not be seen as just a limit from the (A)dS case.

Besides this single possibility of a non-coboundary LBA let us investigate how the classification of r-matrices is affected by the central element. First, we calculate the Schouten brackets of (
324
)-(
328
) in
\mathfrak{B}_{3c}
and find that all of them vanish with the exception that if we consider a different embedding (
l_{1},T_{1}\rightarrow l_{n},T_{n}
) then we have to demand
\Theta_{1}=-\Theta_{-1}
for
r_{3}
and
\Theta_{-1}=2\Theta_{0}
for
r_{4}
. As the
\mathfrak{P}_{3}
automorphisms do not necessarily extend to
\text{Aut}(\mathfrak{B}_{3c})
it is not surprising that there exist r-matrices classified up to
\text{Aut}(\mathfrak{B}_{3})
that are not triangular in
\mathfrak{B}_{3c}
. This is the case for
\hat{r}_{1^{\prime},b}
, which is a contraction of
r_{1^{\prime}}
and we already noted in section
Deformations of the Surface Charge Algebra
that the
a_{1}
part of
r_{1^{\prime}}
is not triangular in
\mathfrak{Vir}\oplus\mathfrak{Vir}
. However, the other possible contraction
\hat{r}_{1^{\prime},a}
is triangular in
\mathfrak{B}_{3c}
.

Furthermore, the addition of the central element makes it possible to define triangular r-matrices which are any linear combination of

\displaystyle r=c^{\prime}_{L}\wedge l_{m},\quad r=c^{\prime}_{L}\wedge T_{m}.

(391)

A tedious but straightforward computation reveals that also the following combinations with the r-matrices we discussed in
Contraction Limit and Classification of Triangular r-matrices
define coboundary LBA

\displaystyle r_{1^{\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle=c^{\prime}_{L}\wedge(\alpha T_{0}+\beta l_{0})+l_{0}\wedge T_{0},\quad r_{2^{\prime\prime}}=c^{\prime}_{L}\wedge T_{0}+\alpha l_{n}\wedge T_{n}+\beta T_{0}\wedge T_{n},

(392)

\displaystyle r_{3^{\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle=c^{\prime}_{L}\wedge(\alpha T_{n}+\beta T_{0}+\gamma T_{-n})+T_{0}\wedge(\Theta_{1}T_{n}+\Theta_{-1}T_{-n})+\Theta_{0}T_{n}\wedge T_{-n},

(393)

\displaystyle r_{4^{\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle=c^{\prime}_{L}\wedge(\alpha T_{n}+\beta l_{n})+\chi(l_{n}\wedge T_{0}-l_{0}\wedge T_{n})-\zeta l_{n}\wedge T_{n}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\Theta_{1}T_{n}\wedge T_{0}+\delta_{\beta,0}\delta_{\zeta,\chi/2}\Theta_{-1}T_{0}\wedge T_{-n},

(394)

\displaystyle r_{5^{\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle=c^{\prime}_{L}\wedge(\alpha T_{0}+\beta T_{n}+\gamma l_{n})+\chi l_{0}\wedge T_{n}+\Theta_{1}T_{0}\wedge T_{n}+\delta_{\gamma,0}\Theta_{0}T_{n}\wedge T_{-n},

(395)

\displaystyle r_{6^{\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle=c^{\prime}_{L}\wedge(l_{n}+\alpha l_{0})+\chi_{1}l_{n}\wedge T_{n}+\delta_{\alpha,0}\chi_{2}l_{n}\wedge l_{0},

(396)

\displaystyle r_{7^{\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle=c^{\prime}_{L}\wedge(\alpha T_{n}+\beta T_{0})+T_{n}\wedge(\chi_{1}l_{n}+\chi_{2}l_{0}).

(397)

As in the asymptotically (A)dS setting also LBA from r-matrices that do not contain central elements can receive extra contributions, e.g. with
r=\chi l_{0}\wedge T_{n}
we get

\displaystyle\delta_{r}(l_{-n})=\chi\left((-n)l_{-n}\wedge T_{n}-2nl_{0}\wedge T_{0}-l_{0}\wedge(n^{3}-n)c^{\prime}_{L}\right).

(398)

Quantization

Here we want to qualitatively analyze how the Lie bialgebras discussed in the previous section can be quantized to quantum groups in all orders of the deformation parameter(s) and highlight this with several examples.

We already established that there is one LBA, defined by the cobracket (
390
) that is not coboundary and consequently the associated Hopf algebra can not be obtained by a twist. However, we simply make an educated guess, namely

\displaystyle\Delta_{\alpha}(l_{m})=l_{m}\otimes 1+1\otimes l_{m},\quad\Delta_{\alpha}(c^{\prime}_{L})=c^{\prime}_{L}\otimes 1+1\otimes c^{\prime}_{L},

(399)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\alpha}(T_{m})=T_{m}\otimes 1+e^{\alpha c^{\prime}_{L}}\otimes T_{m},

(400)

\displaystyle S_{\alpha}(l_{m})=-l_{m},\quad S_{\alpha}(c^{\prime}_{L})=-c^{\prime}_{L},

(401)

\displaystyle S_{\alpha}(T_{m})=-e^{-\alpha c^{\prime}_{L}}T_{m}.

(402)

This indeed defines a Hopf algebra with a coassociative coalgebra and the correct limit. When trying to find quantum groups for LBA with a cobracket
\delta=\delta_{r}+\delta_{\alpha}
it is in general not clear that there exists a constructive method even if we know the twist associated with
\delta_{r}
. For
r=-\frac{\chi}{n}l_{0}\wedge T_{n}+\frac{\alpha}{n}c_{L}\wedge l_{0}
, containing our standard example, we are in luck though; the situation exactly resembles method 5 from
[
100
]
if we identify
-\frac{l_{0}}{n}=H,e^{\alpha c^{\prime}_{L}}=C,T_{n}=D
. Then the coalgebra (
399
)-(
402
) can be deformed with the twist

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}=\exp\left(-\frac{l_{0}}{n}\otimes\log\left(e^{\alpha c^{\prime}_{L}}+\chi T_{n}\right)\right).

(403)

For the supertranslations this then yields

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F},\alpha}(T_{m})=\mathcal{F}\Delta_{\alpha}(T_{m})\mathcal{F}^{-1}=T_{m}\otimes\left(e^{\alpha c^{\prime}_{L}}+\chi T_{n}\right)^{\frac{m}{n}}+e^{\alpha c^{\prime}_{L}}\otimes T_{m},

(404)

which has indeed
\delta=\delta_{r}+\delta_{\alpha}
as a classical limit.

While the twist implementing the deformation defined by (linear combinations of) (
391
) is just an abelian twist, it is not easy to find explicit twists for the combined r-matrices in (
392
)-(
397
) in general. In some cases we can use automorphisms on the subalgebra spanned by the elements appearing in the r-matrix. E.g. by redefining
l_{0}\rightarrow l^{\prime}_{0}=l_{0}+\frac{\beta}{\chi}c^{\prime}_{L}
we can bring
r_{5^{\prime\prime}}(\gamma=0=\Theta_{1}=\Theta_{0})
in the form
r=\chi l^{\prime}_{0}\wedge T_{n}+\alpha c^{\prime}_{L}\wedge T_{0}
consisting of a Jordanian part and a second term commuting with everything else. Thus, the twist is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}=\exp\left(\left(l_{0}+\frac{\beta}{\chi}c^{\prime}_{L}\right)\otimes\log\left(1+\chi T_{n}\right)\right)\exp(\alpha c^{\prime}_{L}\otimes T_{0}).

(405)

r
-matrices that do not contain
c^{\prime}_{L}
explicitly admit the same twist as in the
\mathcal{B}_{3}
algebra, however, there can be extra contributions to the coalgebra sector. We illustrate this fact with the standard example (
364
) leading to (
365
)-(
366
) except for
l_{-n}
where we obtain

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{J}}(l_{-n})=1\otimes l_{-n}-2\chi l_{0}\otimes\Pi_{+n}^{-1}\left(T_{0}+c^{\prime}_{L}(n^{2}-1)\right)+l_{-n}\otimes\Pi_{+n}^{-1}.

(406)

Specialization

The process of specialization is not much different in the centrally extended BMS algebra. For example, adding the simple redefinition
\Pi_{c}=e^{\alpha c^{\prime}_{L}}
to the Hopf algebra with coalgebra sector (
399
)-(
402
) allows the expression in finite powers.

Still, the general comment applies that any Hopf algebra derived from an r-matrix containing
l_{m},m\neq 0
will not admit a q-analog on the whole algebra. It is noteworthy though that the restriction to the one-sided algebra does not make much sense as then no central charge appears in the algebra sector.

Deformations of Asymptotically Flat Symmetries in 4D Gravity

After the investigation of quantum groups in the three dimensional toy model we will now try to carry the insights over to the realistic four dimensional spacetime. Again, first the “infinitesimal deformations”, i.e. the Lie bialgebras, are studied. Then, full quantum groups will be constructed and analyzed, focusing on the comparison between three and four dimensions.

Lie Bialgebras and Classification of Triangular r-matrices

As expected, it is fairly straightforward to prove the following theorem similar to theorem
5
for the three dimensional case.

Theorem 8
.

The first cohomology group
H^{1}(\mathfrak{B}_{4},\bigwedge^{2}\mathfrak{B}_{4})
vanishes.

Furthermore, all Lie bialgebras have to be triangular and are uniquely determined by equivalence classes of r-matrices since

\displaystyle\left(\bigwedge^{3}\mathfrak{B}_{4}\right)_{\mathfrak{B}_{4}}=\left(\bigwedge^{2}\mathfrak{B}_{4}\right)_{\mathfrak{B}_{4}}=\{0\}.

(407)

For the four dimensional Poincaré algebra a classification of the r-matrices was carried out in
[
98
]
(see Appendix
Classification of
r
-matrices
). Even though it is not complete, the only missing parts are some quasi-triangular solutions. By calculating the Schouten brackets of the r-matrices we can filter out their triangular parts that are listed in Appendix
Classification of
r
-matrices
(in the basis (
144
)-(
149
)).
We will mostly focus on the
l\wedge T
part for the sake of simplicity and because it corresponds to the Lie algebra type non-commutativity. Then the distinct r-matrices of this form are

\displaystyle r_{2}

\displaystyle=S_{11}\wedge\bar{k}_{0},

\displaystyle r_{7}

\displaystyle=S_{11}\wedge k_{0}+S_{01}\wedge k_{1}+A_{01}\wedge\bar{k}_{1}+\beta S_{11}\wedge k_{1},

(408)

\displaystyle r_{9}

\displaystyle=S_{01}\wedge\bar{k}_{1}+S_{11}\wedge k_{1},

\displaystyle r_{10}

\displaystyle=A_{01}\wedge k_{1},

(409)

\displaystyle r_{13}

\displaystyle=(S_{11}-S_{00})\wedge\bar{k}_{0},

\displaystyle r_{14}

\displaystyle=S_{11}\wedge\bar{k}_{0},

(410)

\displaystyle r_{17}

\displaystyle=S_{11}\wedge(k_{0}+\beta\bar{k}_{0}).

(411)

In their general form all of these are not part of the
\mathfrak{B}_{3}
subalgebra. It is easy to find a twist for
r_{2},r_{10},r_{12},r_{13},r_{14}
since they are abelian and
r_{17}
corresponds to a Jordanian twist. Furthermore,
r_{7}
is the four dimensional light-cone r-matrix and thus the deformation can be performed with an extended Jordanian twist. Only for
r_{9}
no twist is explicitly known
16
16

16

One can show that finding a twist for
r_{9}
is equivalent to finding one for a matrix of th form
l\wedge T+T\wedge T
in three dimensions.
.

Another difference to the three dimensional setting is that the only deformation that does not single out a particular embedding is given by the abelian r-matrix
k_{0}\wedge\bar{k}_{0}
which does not lead to a non-commutativity of Lie algebra type. The simple reason for this is that in four dimensions the embeddings do not “overlap” in the supertranslation sector.

Full Deformations via Twist

Quantum groups in all orders of the deformation parameter are constructed again by using the twist deformation of the coalgebra sector. The abelian twist and the Jordanian twist are also twists in
\mathfrak{B}_{4}
and the corresponding deformations from section
Full Deformations via Twist
define Hopf subalgebras of four dimensional Hopf algebras. For example, the Jordanian twist

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{J}=\exp\left(-\frac{k_{0}}{n}\otimes\log\left(1+\chi S_{1-m,1-m}\right)\right)

(412)

leads to

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{J}}(S_{pq})=S_{pq}\otimes\Pi_{+,1-m}^{-\frac{1-p-q}{n}}+1\otimes S_{pq},

(413)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{J}}(A_{pq})=A_{pq}\otimes\Pi_{+,1-m}^{-\frac{1-p-q}{n}}+1\otimes A_{pq},

(414)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{J}}(k_{p})=k_{p}\otimes\Pi_{+,1-m}^{\frac{p}{n}}+1\otimes k_{p}-\chi\frac{k_{0}}{n}\otimes(n-p)S_{1-m+p,1-m}\Pi_{+,1-m}^{-1},

(415)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{J}}(\bar{k}_{p})=\bar{k}_{p}\otimes\Pi_{+,1-m}^{\frac{p}{n}}+1\otimes\bar{k}_{p}-\chi\frac{k_{0}}{n}\otimes(n-p)A_{1-m+p,1-m}\Pi_{+,1-m}^{-1},

(416)

with

\displaystyle\Pi_{+,n}\equiv(1+\chi S_{nn}),\quad 1-2m=n.

(417)

The antipodes are given by

\displaystyle S_{\mathcal{F}_{J}}(S_{pq})

\displaystyle=-S_{pq}\Pi_{+,1-m}^{\frac{1-p-q}{n}},\quad S_{\mathcal{F}_{J}}(A_{pq})=-A_{pq}\Pi_{+,1-m}^{\frac{1-p-q}{n}},

(418)

\displaystyle S_{\mathcal{F}_{J}}(k_{p})

\displaystyle=-\left(k_{p}+\frac{\chi}{n}(n-p)S_{\mathcal{F}_{J}}(k_{0})S_{1-m-p,1-m}\Pi_{+,1-m}^{-1}\right)\Pi_{+,1-m}^{-\frac{p}{n}},

(419)

\displaystyle S_{\mathcal{F}_{J}}(\bar{k}_{p})

\displaystyle=-\left(\bar{k}_{p}+\frac{\chi}{n}(n-p)S_{\mathcal{F}_{J}}(k_{0})A_{1-m-p,1-m}\Pi_{+,1-m}^{-1}\right)\Pi_{+,1-m}^{-\frac{p}{n}},

(420)

where

\displaystyle S_{\mathcal{F}_{J}}(k_{0})=-k_{0}(1-\chi S_{1-m,1-m})\Pi_{+,1-m}^{-1}.

(421)

The extended Jordanian twist on the other hand includes the momentum generators from the extra spatial dimension but the general form is analogous to that of section
Full Deformations via Twist

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{eJ}=\exp\left(\frac{\chi}{n}(k_{n}\otimes S_{1-m,m}-\bar{k}_{n}\otimes A_{1-m,m}\right)\mathcal{F}_{J}.

(422)

This complicates the calculations of the deformed coproducts and we find

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{eJ}}(S_{pq})=

\displaystyle\exp\left(\frac{\chi}{n}(k_{n}\otimes S_{1-m,m}-\bar{k}_{n}\otimes A_{1.m,m}\right)\left(\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{J}}(S_{pq})\right)

\displaystyle\times\exp\left(-\frac{\chi}{n}(k_{n}\otimes S_{1-m,m}-\bar{k}_{n}\otimes A_{1.m,m}\right)

(423)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1\otimes S_{pq}+\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{l!}\left(\frac{\chi}{n}\right)^{l}\sum_{r+s=l}{r\choose l}\Bigg(A_{p+rn,q+sn}\otimes g^{(pq)}_{rs}\Pi_{+,1-m}^{\frac{p+q-1}{n}}

\displaystyle+S_{p+rn,q+sn}\otimes f^{(pq)}_{rs}\Pi_{+,1-m}^{\frac{p+q-1}{n}}\Bigg),

(424)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{eJ}}(A_{pq})=

\displaystyle 1\otimes A_{pq}+\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{l!}\left(\frac{\chi}{n}\right)^{l}\sum_{r+s=l}{r\choose l}\Bigg(A_{p+rn,q+sn}\otimes h^{(pq)}_{rs}\Pi_{+,1-m}^{\frac{p+q-1}{n}}

\displaystyle+S_{p+rn,q+sn}\otimes j^{(pq)}_{rs}\Pi_{+,1-m}^{\frac{p+q-1}{n}}\Bigg),

(425)

with the recursively defined coefficients

\displaystyle g^{(pq)}_{00}=0,\quad f^{(pq)}_{00}=1,

(426)

\displaystyle g^{(pq)}_{10}=-(1-m-pn)A_{1-m,m},\quad g^{(pq)}_{01}=(1-m-qn)A_{1-m,m},

(427)

\displaystyle f^{(pq)}_{10}=(1-m-pn)S_{1-m,m},\quad f^{(pq)}_{01}=(1-m-qn)S_{1-m,m},

(428)

\displaystyle g^{(pq)}_{r+1,s}=(1-m-(p+r)n)S_{1-m,m}g^{(pq)}_{rs}-(1-m-(p+r)n)A_{1-m,m}f^{(pq)}_{rs},

(429)

\displaystyle g^{(pq)}_{r,s+1}=(1-m-(q+s)n)S_{1-m,m}g^{(pq)}_{rs}+(1-m-(q+s)n)A_{1-m,m}f^{(pq)}_{rs},

(430)

\displaystyle f^{(pq)}_{r+1,s}=(1-m-(p+r)n)S_{1-m,m}f^{(pq)}_{rs}+(1-m-(p+r)n)A_{1-m,m}g^{(pq)}_{rs},

(431)

\displaystyle f^{(pq)}_{r,s+1}=(1-m-(q+s)n)S_{1-m,m}f^{(pq)}_{rs}-(1-m-(q+s)n)A_{1-m,m}g^{(pq)}_{rs},

(432)

\displaystyle h^{(pq)}_{00}=1,\quad j^{(pq)}_{00}=0,

(433)

\displaystyle h^{(pq)}_{10}=(1-m-pn)S_{1-m,m},\quad h^{(pq)}_{01}=(1-m-qn)S_{1-m,m},

(434)

\displaystyle j^{(pq)}_{10}=-(1-m-pn)A_{1-m,m},\quad j^{(pq)}_{01}=(1-m-qn)A_{1-m,m},

(435)

\displaystyle h^{(pq)}_{r+1,s}=(1-m-(p+r)n)S_{1-m,m}h^{(pq)}_{rs}-(1-m-(p+r)n)A_{1-m,m}j^{(pq)}_{rs},

(436)

\displaystyle h^{(pq)}_{r,s+1}=(1-m-(q+s)n)S_{1-m,m}h^{(pq)}_{rs}+(1-m-(q+s)n)A_{1-m,m}j^{(pq)}_{rs},

(437)

\displaystyle j^{(pq)}_{r+1,s}=(1-m-(p+r)n)S_{1-m,m}j^{(pq)}_{rs}+(1-m-(p+r)n)A_{1-m,m}h^{(pq)}_{rs},

(438)

\displaystyle j^{(pq)}_{r,s+1}=(1-m-(q+s)n)S_{1-m,m}j^{(pq)}_{rs}-(1-m-(q+s)n)A_{1-m,m}h^{(pq)}_{rs},

(439)

and similarly

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{eJ}}(k_{p})=

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{J}}(k_{p})-\frac{\chi}{n}k_{n}\otimes\left(\left(\frac{p+1}{2}-(1-m)\right)S_{1-m+p,m}+\left(\frac{p+1}{2}-m)\right)S_{1-m,m+p}\right)

\displaystyle-\frac{\chi}{n}\bar{k}_{n}\otimes\left(-\left(\frac{p+1}{2}-(1-m)\right)A_{1-m+p,m}-\left(\frac{p+1}{2}-m)\right)A_{1-m,m+p}\right)

\displaystyle-\chi(n-p)\left(k_{n}\otimes S_{1-m,m}-\bar{k}_{n}\otimes A_{1-m,m}\right)\left(1\otimes S_{1-m+p,1-m}\Pi_{+,1-m}^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{l!}\left(\frac{\chi}{n}\right)^{l}\sum_{r+s=l}{r\choose l}\Bigg(k_{p+rn}\otimes\tilde{g}^{(p)}_{r}\Pi_{+,1-m}^{\frac{p}{n}}+\bar{k}_{p+sn}\otimes\tilde{f}^{(p)}_{s}\Pi_{+,1-m}^{\frac{p}{n}}\Bigg),

(440)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{eJ}}(\bar{k}_{p})=

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{J}}(\bar{k}_{p})-\frac{\chi}{n}k_{n}\otimes\left(\left(\frac{p+1}{2}-(1-m)\right)A_{1-m+p,m}-\left(\frac{p+1}{2}-m)\right)A_{1-m,m+p}\right)

\displaystyle-\frac{\chi}{n}\bar{k}_{n}\otimes\left(\left(\frac{p+1}{2}-(1-m)\right)S_{1-m+p,m}-\left(\frac{p+1}{2}-m)\right)S_{1-m,m+p}\right)

\displaystyle-\chi(n-p)\left(k_{n}\otimes S_{1-m,m}-\bar{k}_{n}\otimes A_{1-m,m}\right)\left(1\otimes A_{1-m+p,1-m}\Pi_{+,1-m}^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{l!}\left(\frac{\chi}{n}\right)^{l}\sum_{r+s=l}{r\choose l}\Bigg(k_{p+rn}\otimes\tilde{h}^{(p)}_{r}\Pi_{+,1-m}^{\frac{p}{n}}+\bar{k}_{p+sn}\otimes\tilde{j}^{(p)}_{s}\Pi_{+,1-m}^{\frac{p}{n}}\Bigg),

(441)

with

\displaystyle\tilde{g}^{(p)}_{1}=(n-p)S_{1-m,m},\quad\tilde{f}^{(p)}_{1}=-(n-p)A_{1-m,m},

(442)

\displaystyle\tilde{g}^{(p)}_{r+1}=(n-(p+r)n)S_{1-m,m}\tilde{g}^{(p)}_{r}-(n-(p+r)n)A_{1-m,m}\tilde{f}^{(p)}_{r},

(443)

\displaystyle\tilde{f}^{(p)}_{s+1}=(n-(p+s)n)S_{1-m,m}\tilde{f}^{(p)}_{r}+(n-(p+s)n)A_{1-m,m}\tilde{g}^{(p)}_{r},

(444)

\displaystyle\tilde{h}^{(p)}_{1}=(n-p)A_{1-m,m},\quad\tilde{j}^{(p)}_{1}=(n-p)S_{1-m,m},

(445)

\displaystyle\tilde{h}^{(p)}_{r+1}=(n-(p+r)n)S_{1-m,m}\tilde{h}^{(p)}_{r}+(n-(p+r)n)A_{1-m,m}\tilde{j}^{(p)}_{r},

(446)

\displaystyle\tilde{j}^{(p)}_{s+1}=(n-(p+s)n)S_{1-m,m}\tilde{j}^{(p)}_{r}-(n-(p+s)n)A_{1-m,m}\tilde{h}^{(p)}_{r}.

(447)

Despite the more involved relations the extended Jordanian twist on
\mathfrak{B}_{3}
and
\mathfrak{B}_{4}
share most conceptual properties. E.g. also for (
424
),(
425
),(
440
), (
441
) the expressions admit only a finite number of generators if the one-sided
\mathfrak{B}_{4-}
(spanned by
\{k_{p},\bar{k}_{p},S_{pq},A_{pq}|p,q\leq 1\}
) is considered, implying the embedding with
m=0\hat{=}n=1
. In that case one can also calculate the antipodes recursively from

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle S(S_{pq})+\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{l!}\left(\chi\right)^{l}\sum_{r+s=l}{r\choose l}\Bigg(A_{p+r,q+s}S\left(g^{(pq)}_{rs}\Pi_{+}^{p+q-1n}\right)

\displaystyle+S_{p+r,q+s}S\left(f^{(pq)}_{rs}\Pi_{+}^{p+q-1}\right)\Bigg),

(448)

starting from

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{eJ}}(S_{11})=\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{J}}(S_{11})=S_{11}\otimes\Pi_{+}+1\otimes S_{11},

(449)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{eJ}}(k_{1})=\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{J}}(k_{1})=k_{1}\otimes\Pi_{+}+1\otimes k_{1},\quad\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{eJ}}(\bar{k}_{1})=\bar{k}_{1}\otimes\Pi_{+}+1\otimes\bar{k}_{1}

(450)

\displaystyle\rightarrow S_{\mathcal{F}_{eJ}}(S_{11})=-S_{11}\Pi_{+}^{-1},\quad S_{\mathcal{F}_{eJ}}(k_{1})=-k_{1}\Pi_{+}^{-1},\quad S_{\mathcal{F}_{eJ}}(\bar{k}_{1})=-\bar{k}_{1}\Pi_{+}^{-1}.

(451)

Thus we were able to implement the unique deformed Hopf algebra that contains the light-like
\kappa
-Poincaré as a sub Hopf algebra in the symmetry algebra of a three and four dimensional asymptotically flat spacetime. In both cases the restriction to the respective one-sided algebras is necessary to assign a numerical value to the deformation parameter
1/\kappa
.

Comparison with 3D Deformations

Since, as exemplified by the extended Jordanian twist discussed above, most aspects of the deformation are similar in three and four dimensions even though the formulas tend to be more bloated, we will mostly discuss key differences and similarities in this section.

A first distinction, that there is no deformation leading to Lie algebra type non-commutativity which does not single out an embedding, was already noted in section
Lie Bialgebras and Classification of Triangular r-matrices
. This implies, in contrast to the three dimensional case, that any deformation qualitatively changes the picture of the asymptotic symmetries as presented in chapter
Asymptotic Symmetries of Spacetime
. Namely, no choice of vacuum is preferred a priori but the deformation would violate this ambiguity.

Specialization

We already saw that the formulas for the coproducts deformed by an extended Jordanian twist contain a finite amount of generators under the same conditions as in three dimensions and it is straightforward to show that these also allow for a specialization. Similarly one finds that the Jordanian twist deformation admits a specialization on the entire
\mathfrak{B}_{4}
by extending it with the elements (cf. (
417
))

\displaystyle\Pi_{+,1-m}^{\frac{1}{n}},\quad\Pi_{+,1-m}^{-\frac{1}{n}}.

(452)

Even though we excluded r-matrices that combine different embeddings for physical reasons (cf. section
Classification of Triangular r-matrices
) we can make some general statements about all mathematically possible deformations. In the three dimensional case every r-matrix containing
l_{p},p\neq 0
will not have a quantization with q-analog.
Thus, the only allowed (in the sense of the specialization) terms are either of the form
T\wedge T
or the Jordanian
l_{0}\wedge T_{n}
. Focusing only on the latter we find that these r-matrices can not contain multiple embeddings since e.g.
l_{0}\wedge(T_{n}+T_{n^{\prime}})
is not triangular for
n\neq n^{\prime}
.
In
\mathfrak{B}_{4}
, however, there are triangular r-matrices of the form

\displaystyle r=\bar{k}_{0}\wedge(S_{pp}+S_{p^{\prime}p^{\prime}})

(453)

which admit a q-analog for arbitrary
p,p^{\prime}
(cf. section
Decoupling of Soft and Hard Modes
). The right tensor leg in (
453
) can also contain more generators
S_{p^{\prime\prime}p^{\prime\prime}}
but otherwise this is the only possibility as can be checked by directly computing the Schouten brackets of general r-matrices containing
k_{0},\bar{k}_{0}
.

Surface Charge Algebra

Despite the different algebraic structure of the algebra of surface charges in four dimensional asymptotically flat spacetime
\mathfrak{B}_{4,c}
we obtain a similar result in Appendix
Classification of
r
-matrices

Theorem 9
.

The first cohomology group
H^{1}(\mathfrak{B}_{4,c},\bigwedge^{2}\mathfrak{B}_{4,c})
contains only
\delta_{\alpha,\bar{\alpha}}
.

Here the cocycle

\displaystyle\delta_{\alpha,\bar{\alpha}}:l_{m}\mapsto 0,\quad\bar{l}_{m}\mapsto 0,\quad T_{pq}\mapsto(\alpha c_{l}+\bar{\alpha}c_{\bar{l}})\wedge T_{pq}

(454)

defines a LBA since the co-Jacobi identity is also satisfied.

The rest of the LBA are derived from classical r-matrices and in contrast to the three dimensional BMS all of the r-matrices in
\mathfrak{B}_{4}
of the form
l\wedge T
are triangular in
\mathfrak{B}_{4c}
as well due to the different form of the central extension.
Furthermore, any linear combination of

\displaystyle r=(\alpha c_{l}+\bar{\alpha}c_{\bar{l}})\wedge T_{pq},\quad r=(\alpha c_{l}+\bar{\alpha}c_{\bar{l}})\wedge l_{p},\quad r=(\alpha c_{l}+\bar{\alpha}c_{\bar{l}})\wedge\bar{l}_{p}

(455)

defines a LBA. We refrain from classifying all possible combinations with (
408
)-(
411
) except for our standard example, the Jordanian
r=\chi k_{0}\wedge S_{1-m,1-m}
, where we find

\displaystyle r=

\displaystyle\chi k_{0}\wedge S_{1-m,1-m}+(\alpha c_{l}+\bar{\alpha}c_{\bar{l}})\wedge(\beta_{1}S_{m,1-m}+\beta_{2}A_{m,1-m}

\displaystyle+\beta_{3}S_{1-m,1-m}+\beta_{4}\bar{k}_{n}+\beta_{5}\bar{k}_{-n}+\beta_{6}\bar{k}_{0}).

(456)

While it is still true that some LBA defined by r-matrices in
\bigwedge^{2}\mathfrak{B}_{4}
receive extra terms with central charges in the cobrackets, these do not extend to Hopf algebras which admit a q-analog on the entire algebra because they necessarily contain
k_{n}
or
\bar{k}_{n}
with
n\neq 0
.

The rest of the discussion of the coalgebr sector of full quantum groups in section
Deformations of the Surface Charge Algebra
carries over to the four dimensional case similarly. In particular, the Hopf algebra associated with
\delta_{\alpha,\bar{\alpha}}
only has the non-primitive coproducts

\displaystyle\Delta_{\alpha,\bar{\alpha}}(T_{pq})=T_{pq}\otimes 1+e^{\alpha c_{l}+\bar{\alpha}c_{\bar{l}}}\otimes T_{pq}

(457)

and the example with the twist (
403
) can be defined in four dimensions if we replace
l_{0}\rightarrow k_{0},T_{n}\rightarrow S_{1-m,1-m},\alpha c^{\prime}_{L}\rightarrow\alpha c_{l}+\bar{\alpha}c_{\bar{l}}
. The same redefinition also works to define the twist (
405
) in
\mathfrak{B}_{4c}
.

Applications and Phenomenology

In the last chapter all Lie bialgebra structures and (almost all) Hopf algebras that are compatible with the asymptotic symmetry algebras in three and four dimensions were constructed. Now, let us examine applications and consequences for these deformations some of which are motivated from the finite dimensional symmetry algebras and the
\kappa
-Poincaré in particular (cf. also chapter
Quantum Groups and Non-commutative Geometry
). Of special interest is the interplay of constraints on the deformations and the gravitational aspects of the asymptotic symmetries. Furthermore, novel applications that require the existence of the infinite dimensional symmetry algebra are investigated in the context of the black hole information loss paradox.
Even though the three dimensional spacetime does not fully capture the universe we live in it will again be a useful toy model that allows for many explicit calculations.

Dual Lie Bialgebras and Non-commutative Spacetime

In section
Non-commutative Geometry
it was shown that the coordinates dual to a non-cocommutative Hopf algebra of momenta satisfy non-vanishing commutation relations. A direct interpretation of the dual of an infinite dimensional “momentum algebra” is of course hard to justify and the symmetry only holds asymptotically so we demand that a local observer that picks a vacuum (embedding) should find just the (deformed) spacetime dual to the finite embedding. This requirement will turn out to be non-trivial which is not surprising from a mathematical point of view, i.e. in general the set which is dual to a subalgebra is not necessarily a closed subalgebra itself.

Recall from section
Real Forms of Complex Lie Algebras and
*
-Structures
that for a finite dimensional Lie bialgeba
(\mathfrak{g},[,],\delta)
a dual can be constructed on the dual vector space by simply dualizing the bracket and cobracket. The dual Lie bialgebra is not necessarily coboundary also if
(\mathfrak{g},\delta)
is.
In the infinite dimensional case we will encounter two types of problems with performing this dualization. First, a general problem is that, given an infinite dimensional vector space
V
, the tensor product of its dual is not necessarily the dual of the tensorproduct, i.e.
V^{*}\otimes V^{*}\overset{?}{=}(V\otimes V)^{*}
. Thus the cobracket
\delta^{\circ}\equiv[,]^{*}:\mathfrak{g}^{*}\rightarrow(\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{g})^{*}
might not be closed. To fix this, one can define a subspace
\mathfrak{g}^{\circ}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}^{*}
as a sum of all so-called good subspaces, i.e. subspaces
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
such that
\delta^{\circ}(\mathcal{S})\subseteq\mathcal{S}\otimes\mathcal{S}
(see e.g.
[
101
,
102
,
103
]
).
The second problem will be discussed in a concrete setting in the next section.

Looking at the examples in section
Non-commutative Geometry
, one can see that the first order deformation of the coproduct is enough to determine the dual brackets for the Lie algebra type non-commutativity and so we will be content with studying only the LBA and its duals in order to spare ourselves the burden of constructing full Hopf algebras for the dual spaces.

Three Dimensional Case

Consider a Lie bialgebra consisting of the BMS algebra in three dimension and a cobracket defined by a classical r-matrix from (
324
)-(
328
). The generators of
\mathfrak{B}_{3}
can be expressed in terms of monomials in a formal commuting parameter
a
and its formal inverse (cf. e.g.
[
104
]
), i.e. as a Laurent polynomials algebra from
\mathbb{C}[a^{\pm 1}]

\displaystyle l_{m}\equiv(a^{m+1},0),\quad T_{m}\equiv(0,a^{m+1}).

(458)

For the complex, associative and commutative algebra
A\equiv\mathbb{C}[a^{\pm 1}]
with basis
\{x^{j}|j\in\mathbb{Z}\}
the algebraic dual
A^{*}
admits a pseudo basis
\{\varepsilon^{j}|j\in\mathbb{Z}\}

[
102
,
105
]
. This means that while each element
f\in A
can be decomposed into
f=\sum_{n}f_{n}a^{n}
with only a finite number of non-zero coefficients, the decomposition
A^{*}\ni h=\sum_{n}h_{n}\varepsilon^{n}
has no restrictions on the coefficients.
From
[
108
]
we have the following theorem.

Theorem 10
.

For the dual
A^{*}
of
A\equiv\mathbb{C}[a^{\pm 1}]
the good subspace
A^{\circ}
is the subspace of all sequences
(h_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
satisfying linearly recursive relations of the form
h_{n}=\sum_{j=1}^{r}c_{j}h_{n-j}
for some
1\leq r\in\mathbb{N}
and some coefficients
c_{1},...,c_{r}\in\mathbb{C},c_{r}\neq 0
.

Since for a direct sum
B=B_{1}\oplus B_{2}
the good subspace is given by
B^{\circ}=B^{\circ}_{1}\oplus B^{\circ}_{2}
and
\mathfrak{B}_{3}\sim A\oplus A
we have
\mathfrak{B}^{\circ}_{3}\sim A^{\circ}\oplus A^{\circ}
. Dual to the pseudo basis of
\mathfrak{B}_{3}
we set

\displaystyle\zeta^{m}\equiv(\varepsilon^{m+1},0),\quad\chi^{m}\equiv(0,\varepsilon^{m+1}),

(459)

which is technically only valid in the one-sided BMS (or Witt) algebra. This is due to the second problem, namely that the dual to the Witt algebra bracket is given by

\displaystyle\delta^{\circ}(\varepsilon^{m})=\sum_{i+j=m}(j-i)\varepsilon^{i}\otimes\varepsilon^{j},

(460)

which is only an expression with finitely many terms if the indices are restricted to e.g.
i,j\leq 1
. Upon closer inspection we find that it is only necessary to consider the one-sided part of the superrotation sector and the supertranslations and their duals could have indices from
\mathbb{Z}
. This is in contrast with the requirements from the specialization for certain deformations but it still would single out the
n=1
embedding.
In the following we shall only focus on the supertranslations and calculate the dual algebra for specific deformations based on the Lie bialgebras discussed in chapter
Quantum Groups on Asymptotic Symmetry Algebras
.

Abelian Twist

The cobracket associated with the abelian twist (defined by eq.(
359
) with
\eta=\frac{i}{n\kappa}
)

\displaystyle\delta_{A}(l_{m})=m\frac{i}{n\kappa}(l_{m}\wedge T_{0}+l_{0}\wedge T_{m}),\quad\delta_{A}(T_{m})=m\frac{i}{n\kappa}T_{m}\wedge T_{0},

(461)

leads to

\displaystyle\braket{\chi^{k}\otimes\chi^{j},\delta_{A}(T_{m})}

\displaystyle=\braket{[\chi^{k},\chi^{j}]_{A},T_{m}},

(462)

\displaystyle\rightarrow[\chi^{k},\chi^{j}]_{A}

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{\kappa}(k\delta^{j}_{0}\chi^{k}-j\delta^{k}_{0}\chi^{j}).

(463)

Considering an embedding with the dual coordinates

\displaystyle x^{\pm}\equiv-i\sqrt{2}\chi^{\pm n},\quad x^{2}\equiv i\chi^{0},

(464)

results in

\displaystyle[x^{+},x^{2}]_{A}=\frac{1}{\kappa}x^{+},\quad[x^{-},x^{2}]=-\frac{1}{\kappa}x^{-},

(465)

with all other commutators vanishing. Thus, the three dimensional (deformed) Minkowski subalgebra closes as required.

Jordanian Twist

Similarly for the Jordanian twist (defined by eq. (
364
) with
\chi=\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}\kappa}
)

\displaystyle\delta_{J}(l_{m})=\frac{i}{n\sqrt{2}\kappa}(ml_{m}\wedge T_{n}+(m-n)l_{0}\wedge T_{n+m},\quad\delta_{J}(T_{m})=\frac{i}{n\sqrt{2}\kappa}mT_{m}\wedge T_{n},

(466)

is dual to

\displaystyle[\chi^{k},\chi^{j}]_{J}=\frac{i}{n\sqrt{2}\kappa}(k\delta^{j}_{n}\chi^{k}-j\delta^{k}_{n}\chi^{j})

(467)

and the deformed Minkowski algebra closes again as

\displaystyle[x^{+},x^{-}]_{J}=-\frac{1}{\kappa}x^{-}.

(468)

Extended Jordanian Twist

In the case of the extended Jordanian (or light-cone) twist (defined by eq.(
371
) with
\chi=\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}\kappa}
) one has

\displaystyle\delta_{eJ}(l_{m})

\displaystyle=\delta_{J}(l_{m})-\frac{i}{n\sqrt{2}\kappa}((m-n)l_{m+n}\wedge T_{0}+ml_{n}\wedge T_{m}),

(469)

\displaystyle\delta_{eJ}(T_{m})

\displaystyle=\delta_{J}(T_{m})-\frac{i}{n\sqrt{2}\kappa}(m-n)T_{m+n}\wedge T_{0},

(470)

\displaystyle[\chi^{k},\chi^{j}]_{eJ}

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{n\sqrt{2}\kappa}(k\delta^{j}_{n}\chi^{k}-j\delta^{k}_{n}\chi^{j}-(k-2n)\delta^{j}_{0}\chi^{k-n}+(j-2n)\delta^{k}_{0}\chi^{j-n},

(471)

yielding

\displaystyle[x^{+},x^{-}]_{eJ}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\kappa}x^{-},\quad[x^{+},x^{2}]_{eJ}=-\frac{1}{\kappa}x^{2},

(472)

\displaystyle[x^{-},x^{2}]_{eJ}

\displaystyle=-\frac{3i}{\kappa}\chi^{-2n}.

(473)

This subalgebra does not close and even the restriction to the one-sided
\mathfrak{B}_{3}
can not remedy this fact. Note that the other one-sided subalgebra with indices
\geq-1
would close the algebra but then the construction of the q-analog were not possible.

When we compare (
472
)-(
473
) with the three dimensional light-cone
\kappa
Minkowski algebra from section
Non-commutative Geometry
(i.e. with
\tau^{2}=0
), it can be seen that they are identical up to the extra term
\sim\chi^{-2n}
that leads to the algebra being not closed. Thus, one of our main motivations, to generalize the
\kappa
-Poincaré to the infinite dimensional asymptotic symmetry algebras can not be realized in such a way that it remains a sub (Hopf) algebra in momentum space and its dual.

Furthermore, it is noteworthy that all other deformations (from r-matrices that correspond to Lie algebra type non-commutativity (NC)) which do not admit a q-analog on the full algebra also do not close the dual Minkowski subalgebra.

Automorphisms on the Dual Algebra

A potential caveat could arrise by using duals of automorphisms. In general one has

\displaystyle\braket{\delta_{\tilde{\varphi}(r)}\varphi(T),\chi\otimes\chi^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\braket{\tilde{\varphi}(\delta_{r}(T)),\chi\otimes\chi^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\braket{T,[\varphi^{\dagger}(\chi),\varphi^{\dagger}(\chi^{\prime})]_{r}},

(474)

where
\tilde{\varphi}=\varphi\otimes\varphi
,
\varphi^{\dagger}
is the adjoint of
\varphi
and the lhs is also equal to

\displaystyle\braket{\delta_{\tilde{\varphi}(r)}\varphi(T),\chi\otimes\chi^{\prime}}=\braket{T,\varphi^{\dagger}([\chi,\chi^{\prime}]_{\tilde{\varphi}(r)})},

(475)

so that

\displaystyle[\chi,\chi^{\prime}]_{\tilde{\varphi}(r)}=\left(\varphi^{\dagger}\right)^{-1}[\varphi^{\dagger}(\chi),\varphi^{\dagger}(\chi^{\prime})]_{r}.

(476)

For the r-matrix
r=(l_{1}-l_{-1})\wedge(T_{1}-T_{-1})\subset r_{3}
with

\displaystyle\delta_{r}(T_{m})

\displaystyle=(m-1)T_{1+m}\wedge(T_{1}-T_{-1})-(m+1)T_{m-1}\wedge(T_{1}-T_{-1}),

(477)

\displaystyle[\chi^{k},\chi^{j}]_{r}

\displaystyle=(k-2)\chi^{k-1}(\delta^{j}_{1}-\delta^{j}_{-1})-(k+2)\chi^{k+1}(\delta^{j}_{1}-\delta^{j}_{-1})-(k\leftrightarrow j)

(478)

one could apply the
\mathfrak{P}_{3}
automorphism

\displaystyle\varphi(l_{\pm 1})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}(l_{1}+l_{-1})\pm l_{0},\quad\varphi(l_{0})=\frac{1}{2}(l_{1}-l_{-1}),

(479)

\displaystyle\varphi(T_{\pm 1})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}(T_{1}+T_{-1})\pm T_{0},\quad\varphi(T_{0})=\frac{1}{2}(T_{1}-T_{-1}),

(480)

so that
\varphi(r)=l_{0}\wedge T_{0}
. From the dual automorphism

\displaystyle\varphi^{\dagger}(\chi^{\pm 1})=-\frac{1}{2}(\chi^{1}+\chi^{-1})\pm\chi^{0}

(481)

and (
476
), (
463
)

\displaystyle[\chi^{1},\chi^{-1}]_{r}

\displaystyle=2\chi^{0},\quad[\chi^{1},\chi^{0}]_{r}=2(\chi^{1}+\chi^{-1}),

(482)

\displaystyle[\chi^{-1},\chi^{0}]_{r}

\displaystyle=-2(\chi^{1}+\chi^{-1})

(483)

follows. Thus, the deformed Minkowski algebra closes in contrast to (
478
) leading e.g. to

\displaystyle[\chi^{1},\chi^{-1}]_{r}=2\chi^{0}+3(\chi^{2}+\chi^{-2}).

(484)

However,
\varphi
does not extend to an automorphism of
\mathfrak{B}_{3}
and consequently the closing subalgebra (
482
) is not dual to the whole three dimensional BMS.

Four Dimensional Case

In a completely analogous way one can construct the dual algebra to the supertranslation sector of
\mathfrak{B}_{4}
. We first define the dual pseudo basis as

\displaystyle\chi^{pq}_{S},\quad\chi^{pq}_{A},

(485)

\displaystyle\braket{S_{kj},\chi^{pq}_{S}}=\delta^{p}_{k}\delta^{q}_{j},\quad\braket{A_{kj},\chi^{pq}_{A}}=\delta^{p}_{k}\delta^{q}_{j},

(486)

with
p\leq q
.

A deformation with the Jordanian twist (defined in eq.(
412
) with
\chi=\frac{-i\sqrt{2}}{\kappa}
) leads to the cobrackets

\displaystyle\delta_{J}(k_{p})

\displaystyle=-ipk_{p}\wedge S_{1-m,1-m}-i(p-n)S_{1-m+p,1-m},

(487)

\displaystyle\delta_{J}(\bar{k}_{p})

\displaystyle=-ip\bar{k}_{p}\wedge S_{1-m,1-m}-i(p-n)A_{1-m+p,1-m},

(488)

\displaystyle\delta_{J}(S_{pq})

\displaystyle=i(1-p-q)S_{pq}\wedge S_{1-m,1-m},\quad\delta_{J}(A_{pq})=0,

(489)

which in turn imply

\displaystyle[\chi^{kj}_{S},\chi^{k^{\prime}j^{\prime}}_{S}]_{J}=-i(1-k-j)\chi^{kj}_{S}\delta^{k^{\prime}}_{1-m}\delta^{j^{\prime}}_{1-m}-((kj)\leftrightarrow(k^{\prime}j^{\prime})).

(490)

With

\displaystyle x^{+}\equiv\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}}\chi^{1-m,1-m}_{S},\quad x^{-}\equiv-\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}}\chi^{mm}_{S},\quad x^{1}=i\chi^{m,1-m}_{S},\quad x^{2}=i\chi^{m,1-m}_{A}

(491)

we finally obtain the closed deformed Minkowski algebra

\displaystyle[x^{+},x^{-}]_{J}=-\frac{1}{\kappa}x^{-}.

(492)

The cobracekts for the extended Jordanian twist (defined by eq.(
422
) with
\chi=\frac{-i\sqrt{2}}{\kappa}
) reads

\displaystyle\delta_{eJ}(S_{pq})=

\displaystyle\delta_{J}(S_{pq})-i\left(\frac{n+1}{2}-p\right)S_{p+n,q}\wedge S_{1-m,m}-i\left(\frac{n+1}{2}-q\right)S_{p,q+n}\wedge S_{1-m,m}

\displaystyle+i\left(\frac{n+1}{2}-p\right)A_{p+n,q}\wedge A_{1-m,m}-i\left(\frac{n+1}{2}-q\right)A_{p,q+n}\wedge A_{1-m,m},

(493)

\displaystyle\delta_{eJ}(A_{pq})=

\displaystyle-i\left(\frac{n+1}{2}-p\right)A_{p+n,q}\wedge S_{1-m,m}-i\left(\frac{n+1}{2}-q\right)A_{p,q+n}\wedge S_{1-m,m}

\displaystyle-i\left(\frac{n+1}{2}-p\right)S_{p+n,q}\wedge A_{1-m,m}-i\left(\frac{n+1}{2}-q\right)S_{p,q+n}\wedge A_{1-m,m}.

(494)

This is dual to

\displaystyle[\chi^{kj}_{S},\chi^{k^{\prime}j^{\prime}}_{S}]_{eJ}=

\displaystyle[\chi^{kj}_{S},\chi^{k^{\prime}j^{\prime}}_{S}]_{J}+i\bigg(\left(\frac{n+1}{2}-(k-n)\right)\chi^{k-n,j}_{S}\delta^{k^{\prime}}_{1-m}\delta^{j^{\prime}}_{m}

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{n+1}{2}-(j-n)\right)\chi^{k,j-n}_{S}\delta^{k^{\prime}}_{1-m}\delta^{j^{\prime}}_{m}-((kj)\leftrightarrow(k^{\prime}j^{\prime}))\bigg),

(495)

\displaystyle[\chi^{kj}_{A},\chi^{k^{\prime}j^{\prime}}_{A}]_{eJ}=

\displaystyle i\bigg(-\left(\frac{n+1}{2}-(k-n)\right)\chi^{k-n,j}_{S}\delta^{k^{\prime}}_{1-m}\delta^{j^{\prime}}_{m}

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{n+1}{2}-(j-n)\right)\chi^{k,j-n}_{S}\delta^{k^{\prime}}_{1-m}\delta^{j^{\prime}}_{m}-((kj)\leftrightarrow(k^{\prime}j^{\prime}))\bigg),

(496)

\displaystyle[\chi^{kj}_{A},\chi^{k^{\prime}j^{\prime}}_{S}]_{eJ}=

\displaystyle i\bigg(\left(\frac{n+1}{2}-(k-n)\right)\chi^{k-n,j}_{A}\delta^{k^{\prime}}_{1-m}\delta^{j^{\prime}}_{m}

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{n+1}{2}-(j-n)\right)\chi^{k,j-n}_{A}\delta^{k^{\prime}}_{1-m}\delta^{j^{\prime}}_{m}-((kj)\leftrightarrow(k^{\prime}j^{\prime}))\bigg),

(497)

and thus we obtain

\displaystyle[x^{+},x^{-}]_{eJ}=\frac{1}{\kappa}x^{-},\quad[x^{+},x^{1}]_{eJ}=\frac{1}{\kappa}x^{1},

(498)

\displaystyle[x^{+},x^{2}]_{eJ}=\frac{1}{\kappa}x^{2},\quad[x^{-},x^{1}]_{eJ}=\frac{2i}{\kappa}\chi_{S}^{3m-1,m},

(499)

\displaystyle[x^{-},x^{2}]_{eJ}=\frac{2i}{\kappa}\chi_{A}^{3m-1,m},

(500)

so the Minkowski algebra does not close as in the three dimensional case.

In the previous section we remarked that deformations that do not admit a specialization on the full algebra do not close the dual Minkowski subalgebra which is also true in four dimensions. Furthermore, the converse is only true if we insist on the requirement that only one embedding is present in the r-matrix. Otherwise there is the counterexample (
453
) which admits a q-analog on
\mathfrak{B}_{4}
but does not close the Minkowski sector for
p\neq p^{\prime}
.

Thus, we can conclude that imposing the constraint that the sector dual to the Poincaré subalgebra has to be a closed subalgebra as well would, in combination with the specialization requirement, rule out all (but in four dimensions not only) deformations which can be specialized only on the one-sided BMS algebra.

Other Types of Non-commutativity

Recall from section
Broken and Deformed Symmetries
that also other types of non-commutativity are possible. In particular, r-matrices of the form
T\wedge T
lead to canonical NC. For example from
r=\Theta T_{0}\wedge T_{n}
with

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{-1}_{r}\equiv\bar{f}^{\alpha}\otimes\bar{f}_{\alpha}=\exp\left(-\frac{\Theta}{2}T_{0}\wedge T_{n}\right)

(501)

one obtains

\displaystyle[\chi^{p},\chi^{q}]

\displaystyle=\left(\bar{f}^{\alpha}\triangleright\chi^{p}\right)\left(\bar{f}_{\alpha}\triangleright\chi^{q}\right)-(p\leftrightarrow q)=-\Theta(\delta^{p}_{0}\delta^{q}_{n}-\delta^{p}_{n}\delta^{q}_{0}),

(502)

\displaystyle\rightarrow[x^{2},x^{+}]

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{2}\Theta,\quad[x^{+},x^{-}]=[x^{2},x^{-}]=0.

(503)

Here we have used the definition (
120
) and (
464
). In this specific example the deformed Minkowski algebra closes which is not necessarily true for arbitrary r-matrices. Since the supertranslations commute all r-matrices are mathematically allowed. Only in combination with terms of the form
l\wedge T
there are restrictions for the canonical NC (zeroth order in (
122
)). Unfortunately, already in the three dimensional case we do not know the explicit form of the twist for those r-matrices.

Similarly, the classifications (
324
)-(
328
) and (
753
)-(
766
) allow us to infer constraints on mixed Lie algebra type NC and quadratic NC
17
17

17

Cf.
[
51
]
for detailed construction in the Poincaré algbra.
. From the former it is easy to see that in the three dimensional case there are no r-matrices that would result in such mixed NC, whereas in the four dimensional asymptotic symmetry algebras they are possible but much more restricted than each of the NC individually. The same is also true for combinations of canonical and quadratic NC and all three types of NC are not allowed simultaneously in three and four dimensions.
Note that some of these constraints are direct consequences of the triangularity requirements. For example, in the finite dimensional
\mathfrak{P}_{3}
where also quasitriangular r-matrices exist combinations of quadratic and Lie algebra NC are allowed (cf. the classification of
[
88
]
). Similarly, it is known that combinations of all three types of NC are possible in
\mathfrak{P}_{4}
with quasitriangular r-matrices (cf.
[
98
]
).

Concerning the centrally extended algebra of the surface charges, it is easy to see that contributions to the cobrackets containing central charges do not alter the dual brackets as we have
\braket{c_{l},\chi^{p}}=0
.

In principle, non-commutative spacetime coordinates could have directly observable consequences like uncertainty relations (implying a minimal unvertainty) (see e.g.
[
106
]
or
[
107
]
) but these would be out of reach for experiments in the near future. From a conceptional point of view the idea also adopted in this thesis is that the entire concept of spacetime breaks down at the smallest scales and is only valid as an emergent phenomenon. The dual momentum quantum group is the more fundamental and directly accessible object and potentially also allows to test deformations in current experiments as we explore in the following sections.

Quantum Lie Algebra and Deformed Dispersion Relations

Recall from section
Star Product
that the property (
89
) of the deformed coproduct in the so-called quantum Lie algebra basis is crucial to define a deformed differential calculus and a deformed wave operator expressed in the formulation admitting said property. As all the deformations we studied in chapter
Quantum Groups on Asymptotic Symmetry Algebras
are given by a twist, we can readily construct the isomorphism
D
and calculate the wave operator of any given Poincaré subalgebra, i.e.

\displaystyle\Box^{\mathcal{F}}=D(\Box)=P_{\mu}^{\mathcal{F}}P^{\mu\,\mathcal{F}}.

(504)

A scalar field with mass
m
in the non-commutative spacetime described by a wave function
\phi(p)
in momentum space then satisfies

\displaystyle\left(\Box^{\mathcal{F}}+m^{2}\right)\phi(p)=0,

(505)

leading to a potentially deformed vacuum dispersion relation. If the non-commutativity is seen as an effective phenomenon from QG one could interpret these effects on the propagation in vacuum as arising from interactions with the non-trivial structure (at the QG scale) of spacetime itself.

For all generators of the Poincaré algebra we have

\displaystyle[P_{\mu}^{\mathcal{F}},\Box^{\mathcal{F}}]_{\star}

\displaystyle\equiv P_{\mu\,(1^{\mathcal{F}})}^{\mathcal{F}}\Box^{\mathcal{F}}S_{\mathcal{F}}(P_{\mu\,(2^{\mathcal{F}})})=0,

(506)

\displaystyle[M_{\mu\nu}^{\mathcal{F}},\Box^{\mathcal{F}}]_{\star}

\displaystyle=0,

(507)

i.e. the wave operator is the quadratic Casimir of the quantum Lie algebra.
Since (
506
)-(
507
) obviously also holds for the undeformed wave operator and the undeformed adjoint action, it follows that
\Box^{\mathcal{F}}
is always undeformed if the
n=1
embedding is used for the twist.
Only if we allow for deformations that include different embeddings there are potentially differences in the wave operator involving generators of soft charges and thus the wave equation can be influenced by soft charges.

Three Dimensional Case

The most trivial example is the abelian twist deformation in three dimensions (cf. section
Full Deformations via Twist
). Since it does not single out an embedding the quadratic Casimir has to be undeformed by the previous remarks. Indeed, expressing it in terms of the algebra generators
18
18

18

Summation convention applies to “relativistic indices” (greek letters) but not to “BMS indices” (latin letters).

\displaystyle P_{\mu}P^{\mu}=T_{n}T_{-n}-T_{0}^{2}

(508)

and calculating

\displaystyle D_{A}(T_{m})=T_{m}e^{m\eta T_{0}},

(509)

we find

\displaystyle D_{A}(\Box)=T_{n}T_{-n}-T_{0}^{2}=\Box.

(510)

For the Jordanian twist deformation we know that one could consistently use different embeddings but from

\displaystyle D_{J}(T_{m})=T_{m}\Pi_{+,n^{\prime}}^{\frac{m}{n^{\prime}}}

(511)

where
\Pi_{+,n^{\prime}}=(1+\chi T_{n^{\prime}})
, we find that

\displaystyle D_{J}(\Box)=\Box

(512)

again, i.e. the wave operator is undeformed independently from the embedding.

Our third standard example, the light-cone
\kappa
-Poincaré defined on the BMS with an extended Jordanian twist, requires the restriction to the one-sided
\mathfrak{B}_{3-}
for the specialization of the deformation parameter (cf. section
Full Deformations via Twist
) which only admits one Poincaré subalgebra resulting in an undeformed
\Box^{\mathcal{F}}
. In general the quantum Lie algebra generators can be calculated from

\displaystyle D_{eJ}(T_{m})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,k^{\prime}=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!k^{\prime}!}(-\chi)^{k^{\prime}}(l_{0}^{k}l_{n^{\prime}}^{k^{\prime}})\triangleright(T_{m})(\log(1+\chi T_{n^{\prime}}))^{k}T_{0}^{k^{\prime}},

(513)

\displaystyle l_{n^{\prime}}^{k^{\prime}}\triangleright T_{m}

\displaystyle=\prod_{r=1}^{k^{\prime}-1}(n^{\prime}-(m+rn^{\prime}))T_{m+k^{\prime}},

(514)

which also contains infinitely many generators for unrestricted
m
. Ignoring the technical constraints for a moment and treating the deformation as an effective theory, one has in first order of the deformation parameter

\displaystyle D_{eJ}(T_{m})=T_{m}-\chi(n^{\prime}-m)T_{n^{\prime}+m}T_{0}-\chi mT_{m}T_{n^{\prime}}+\mathcal{O}(\chi^{2})

(515)

resulting in

\displaystyle D_{eJ}(\Box)=\Box-\chi\left((n^{\prime}-n)T_{n+n^{\prime}}T_{0}T_{-n}+(n^{\prime}+n)T_{-n+n^{\prime}}T_{0}T_{n}+2n^{\prime}T_{n^{\prime}}T_{0}^{2}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\chi^{2}).

(516)

For
n^{\prime}=n=1
this is just the undeformed
\Box
as it should be but otherwise the wave operator is deformed. Unlike the scenario in
[
43
]
, where a dilation operator is included in the algebra and used for deformation,
\Box^{\mathcal{F}}
is not proportional to
\Box
and thus the wave equation would be also altered for massless fields.

Including the central charges does not change the conclusions considerably. In that case only the
n=1
embedding has a quadratic Casimir operator and if we only permit deformations which allow for q-analog on the full
\mathfrak{B}_{3c}
algebra
\Box^{\mathfrak{F}}
is undeformed. For the non-coboundary LBA and the corresponding Hopf algebra it is not even clear how to define a deformed Casimir operator.

Four Dimensional Case

In the four dimensional spacetime there is no abelian twist that does not single out an embedding and corresponds to non-commutative geometry of Lie algebra type (see section
Comparison with 3D Deformations
). For the Jordanian and extended Jordanian twist the results are qualitatively similar to the three dimensional scenario. In particular, for the Jordanian twist from (
412
) we find

\displaystyle D_{J}(S_{pq})=S_{pq}\Pi_{+,1-m}^{\frac{p+q-1}{n^{\prime}}}

(517)

and the quadratic Casimir for a given embedding

\displaystyle\Box\equiv P_{\mu}P^{\mu}=S_{1-m,1-m}S_{mm}-S_{1-m,m}^{2}-A_{1-m,m}^{2}

(518)

is undeformed, i.e.

\displaystyle D_{J}(\Box)=\Box.

(519)

The light-cone
\kappa
-Poincaré in four dimensions from the twist (
422
) is not well defined in all orders for general elements
S_{pq},A_{pq}
but in a first order approximation one obtains

\displaystyle D_{eJ}(S_{pq})=

\displaystyle D_{J}(S_{pq})+\chi\left(\left(\frac{n^{\prime}+1}{2}-p\right)S_{p+n^{\prime},q}+\left(\frac{`n+1}{2}-q\right)S_{p,,q+n^{\prime}}\right)S_{1-m^{\prime},m^{\prime}}

\displaystyle-\chi\left(\left(\frac{n^{\prime}+1}{2}-p\right)A_{p+n^{\prime},q}-\left(\frac{n^{\prime}+1}{2}-q\right)A_{p,,q+n^{\prime}}\right)A_{1-m^{\prime},m^{\prime}},

(520)

\displaystyle D_{eJ}(A_{pq})=

\displaystyle D_{J}(A_{pq})+\chi\left(\left(\frac{n^{\prime}+1}{2}-p\right)A_{p+n^{\prime},q}+\left(\frac{n^{\prime}+1}{2}-q\right)A_{p,,q+n^{\prime}}\right)S_{1-m^{\prime},m^{\prime}}

\displaystyle-\chi\left(-\left(\frac{n^{\prime}+1}{2}-p\right)S_{p+n^{\prime},q}+\left(\frac{n^{\prime}+1}{2}-q\right)S_{p,,q+n^{\prime}}\right)A_{1-m^{\prime},m^{\prime}},

(521)

leading to the deformed wave operator

\displaystyle D_{eJ}(\Box)=

\displaystyle\Box+\chi((n^{\prime}-n)S_{1-m+n^{\prime},1-m}S_{1-m^{\prime},m^{\prime}}-(n^{\prime}-n)A_{1-m+n^{\prime},1-m}A_{1-m^{\prime},m^{\prime}})S_{m,m}

\displaystyle+\chi((n^{\prime}+n)S_{m+n^{\prime},m}S_{1-m^{\prime},m^{\prime}}-(n^{\prime}+n)A_{m+n^{\prime},m}A_{1-m^{\prime},m^{\prime}})S_{1-m,1-m}

\displaystyle-\chi(((n^{\prime}-n)S_{1-m+n^{\prime},m}+(n^{\prime}+n)S_{1-m,m+n^{\prime}})S_{1-m^{\prime},m^{\prime}}

\displaystyle-((n^{\prime}-n)A_{1-m+n^{\prime},m}-(n^{\prime}+n)A_{1-m,m+n^{\prime}})A_{1-m^{\prime},m^{\prime}})S_{1-m,m}

\displaystyle-\chi(((n^{\prime}-n)A_{1-m+n^{\prime},m}+(n^{\prime}+n)A_{1-m,m+n^{\prime}})S_{1-m^{\prime},m^{\prime}}

\displaystyle-(-(n^{\prime}-n)S_{1-m+n^{\prime},m}+(n^{\prime}+n)S_{1-m,m+n^{\prime}})A_{1-m^{\prime},m^{\prime}})A_{1-m,m}.

(522)

For
n^{\prime}=n
this expression vanishes and otherwise the deformation is not purely multiplicative as in three dimensions.

Without explicitly presenting the calculations
19
19

19

In four dimensions only twists from r-matrices of the form (
453
) have to be checked additionally.
we note that for all deformations associated to Lie algebra type non-commutativity the wave operator is either undeformed for all embeddings or the deformation can not be consistently performed in all orders if we insist on the specialization.

Phenomenology of Deformed Dispersion Relations

Deformed dispersion relations are one of the most studied consequences of various versions of non-commutativity as a QG effect because of the possibility to offset the suppression of the effect size by the smallness of the QG length scale
l_{\text{{QG}}}=1/\kappa
with large distances over which e.g. cosmic rays can travel as we will see below. In the context of
\kappa
-Poincaré deformed dispersion relations can arise for example when a basis is chosen such that the deformation affects also the algebra (cf. section
\kappa
-Poincaré and Non-commutative Geometry
) and consequently the Casimir operator of that algebra is deformed. Note that this is different from the choices made in this thesis where the algebra of symmetries itself is undeformed.

Also other frameworks like Loop Quantum Gravity or theories involving Lorentz invariance violation appear to predict deformed dispersion relations and usually (but not always, as we shall see) this results in an energy dependent speed of light
v(E)\equiv dE/dp

[
110
]
. A model independent ansatz (in this limited sense)
[
109
]

\displaystyle{\it{p}}^{2}=E^{2}\left(1+\xi(l_{\text{{QG}}}E)^{\alpha}+\mathcal{O}(l_{\text{{QG}}}E)^{\alpha+1}\right),

(523)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow v(E)\approx 1+\xi\frac{1-\alpha}{2}(l_{\text{{QG}}}E)^{\alpha},

(524)

with the parameters
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
and
\xi=\pm 1
leads to the time delay at the detector of photons of different energy emitted simultaneously from a source at distance
L

\displaystyle|\Delta t|=L\left|\frac{1}{v(E)}-\frac{1}{v(E+\Delta E)}\right|\approx L\left|\frac{(1-\alpha)\alpha l_{\text{{QG}}}^{\alpha}E^{\alpha-1}}{2}\right|\Delta E.

(525)

From this formula, one can see that a larger effect size can be achieved either by observing very high energy photons or having a large distance to the source. The former is very hard to obtain as it is currently not possible to produce them terrestrially. Even though high energy photons from astronomical sources are rare, they have the advantage that also the distance they traveled can be gigantic.

Some data has been collected for example on high-energy Gamma-ray bursts
[
111
]
,
[
112
]
and analyzed for signatures of deformed dispersion relations
[
113
]
. While the raw accuracy of the experiments should already be high enough to detect such signatures, a big conceptual problem remains, namely that the emission process in astronomical sources is not understood very well (see
[
114
]
and references therein). This makes it currently impossible to estimate whether photons were emitted sufficiently simultaneously but an improved understanding or larger effect sizes in the near future could make it possible to test deformed dispersion relations of the form (
523
).

In the previous section it was already noted that the twist performed in
[
43
]
causes a multiplicative deformation of
\Box
and thus massless fields have to satisfy an undeformed equation of motion. Only massive fields would obey a deformed dispersion relation in such a scenario.

The only scenarios from all deformations we discussed in chapter
Quantum Groups on Asymptotic Symmetry Algebras
that predict an effect in the dispersion relation (which is only possible if the specialization requirement is relaxed) would not just cause a multiplicative change in
\Box
. However, the deformed wave operator (e.g. (
516
), (
522
)) contains generators of supertranslations and in analogy to the calculations in (
523
) we might compute
v(E,T)
. For example from the dispersion of a massless field with (
516
) which moves in the
P_{2}
direction
(P_{1}=0)
it follows that

\displaystyle v_{2}\equiv\frac{dE}{dP_{2}}\approx\pm 1\pm\chi 4n^{\prime}T_{n^{\prime}}+\frac{\chi}{2}[(n^{\prime}+n)T_{n^{\prime}-n}+(n^{\prime}+n)T_{n^{\prime}+n}]+\mathcal{O}\left(\chi^{2}\right).

(526)

Thus, any deviations in the time of flight would not be depending on the energy of the quanta but on soft charges which makes this effect practically undetectable.

Deformed Leibniz Rule and Black Hole Information

An important application of a (deformed) coproduct on momentum space is that it induces a recipe for the addition of the total momentum of multi-particle states. This aspect will be explored in the following by applying it not only to the classical “hard” momenta but also to supertranslation and superrotation charges introduced in section
Asymptotic Flatness
. We then investigate a potential connection to the proposal by Hawking, Perry and Strominger
[
115
]
that superrotation charges can (at least partially) solve the black hole information loss paradox.

Addition of Momenta, Supertranslation/-rotation Charges

With the additional structure of a Hopf algebra with primitive coproduct on the Poincaré algebra one can write the usual simple addition of momenta as the consequence of eq.(
43
). To be more explicit, let us denote a single-particle state
\ket{p_{\mu}}
by its momentum eigenvalue (it may also have other discrete quantum numbers which we ignore for the moment) and in the momentum space representation the momentum operator
P_{\mu}
acts via

\displaystyle P_{\mu}\triangleright\ket{p_{\nu}}=p_{\mu}\ket{p_{\nu}}

(527)

and

\displaystyle P_{\mu}\triangleright\left(\ket{p_{1,\nu}}\otimes\ket{p_{2,\nu}}\right)=P_{\mu(1)}\triangleright\ket{p_{1,\nu}}\otimes P_{\mu(2)}\triangleright\ket{p_{2,\nu}}=(p_{1,\mu}+p_{2,\mu})\left(\ket{p_{1,\nu}}\otimes\ket{p_{2,\nu}}\right)

(528)

for two-particles states, where the primitivity of the coproduct was used in the last equality. In other words, the total momentum of a multi-particle state is just the sum of the single-particle states. However, the addition might involve other, non-linear terms if the coproduct is deformed. This feature, sometimes referred to as deformed Leibniz rule, was discussed for example in
[
116
]
,
[
117
]
and more recently also in
[
43
]
. In some scenarios the addition rules lead to a saturation at the QG scale (a maximal momentum) as discussed e.g. in
[
117
]
and ideas to use this feature for automatic UV regularization were early motivations to study deformed symmetries
[
118
]
.

Another peculiarity about the deformed Leibniz rule is the apparent lack of symmetry under the exchange of particles (via the flip of the tensorproduct) resulting from a non-symmetric coproduct. But recall from section
Quasitriangular Hopf Algebras and Quantum
R
-Matrices
that in the deformed setting the representation of the braid group is not given by the flip but instead dictated by a quantum R-matrix
[
43
]

\displaystyle\ket{p_{1}}\otimes\ket{p_{2}}\mapsto\tilde{R}^{-1}(\ket{p_{1}}\otimes\ket{p_{2}})=\bar{R}^{\alpha}\triangleright\ket{p_{2}}\otimes\bar{R}_{\alpha}\triangleright\ket{p_{1}},

(529)

restoring some form of symmetry.
Here
R^{-1}\equiv\bar{R}^{\alpha}\otimes\bar{R}_{\alpha}=\mathcal{F}\mathcal{F}^{-1}_{21}
is known from the twist of the deformation.

When using the standard Leibniz rule to add up momenta, one does not need to care whether the individual momenta already belong to composite systems or elementary particles. Such a prescription applied to the deformed Leibniz rule would immediately lead to a contradiction with experiment because the momenta of macroscopic objects (as e.g. a soccer ball) can easily be bigger than the mass scale
\kappa\sim m_{\text{Pl}}
that might appear for a
\kappa
-deformed coproduct in (
528
) leading to noticeable differences. For elementary particles on the other hand momenta of this scale are unobtainable in current experiments by orders of magnitude and the deviations for the resulting total momentum would be minuscule. The necessity to distinguish between elementary and composite states is, however, a big conceptual issue discussed in the literature under the term soccer-ball problem (see
[
119
]
,
[
120
]
,
[
121
]
and also
[
122
]
).

All the deformations discussed in this thesis imply a deformed Leibniz rule (see section
Decoupling of Soft and Hard Modes
for two examples) which (depending on the embedding for the deformation) persists also if only the Poincaré sector is considered.

Black Hole Information Loss Paradox and Soft Hair Solution

Black holes, astronomical objects so dense that not even light rays can escape their gravitational pull beyond the event horizon, are a prime example of a phenomenon where the necessity of a theory combining gravitational as well as quantum mechanical features becomes apparent. For once, this is due to the singularity of physical quantities like the curvature at the center of a black hole as predicted by GR. Another, more subtle point is that Hawking showed in 1974
[
123
]
that black holes, contrary to what was previously believed, lose mass in the form of thermal radiation called Hawking radiation. Hawking used a semi-classical approach, meaning that the background geometry of the black hole is described by classical GR and the matter/radiation fields in the vicinity of the horizon by a quantum field theory. This simplification seems to be justified as the gravitational tidal forces are weak at the horizon for large black holes. In the original calculation the spectrum of the Hawking radiation is completely thermal, i.e. it is described by a mixed state. Since we can form a black hole out of matter from which we have perfect information, i.e. a pure state, the decay into thermal radiation and a unique vacuum is incompatible with the unitary (therefore reversible) evolution in quantum mechanics of the matter/black hole/radiation that would evolve pure states to pure states and this tension is called the black hole information loss paradox. A central argument for the paradox to persist is the so-called no-hair theorem stating that a black hole is characterized solely by the Poincaré momenta and gauge charges. In particular, multipole moments of infalling matter settle into a completely spherical black hole configuration long before the process of Hawking radiation starts if the black hole is large enough because the power radiated away is inversely proportional to the cube of the mass
[
124
]
,
[
125
]
.

Several proposed solutions of the black hole information loss paradox have been put forward as e.g. recently in
[
126
]
but the debate remains, so far, inconclusive. As stated above, here we will only consider the proposal by Strominger et. al.

Poincaré Hair

The existence of the ten Poincaré charges does introduce correlations between early and late radiation but it is by far not enough to purify the state completely. These correlations are not included in Hawkings calculation and lead to a slight departure from a completely thermal spectrum. As a warm-up for the soft charges, we explicitly describe the hard Poincaré charges as follows
[
127
]
. We start with a black hole with mass
M
described by
\ket{M}
from which some early quanta with total mass
m
were emitted (or alternatively in which an object of mass
m
was dropped) and that decays into the configuration
\ket{X}
, i.e.

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}\ket{M}=\ket{X}=\sum_{p}\mathcal{S}_{M\rightarrow p}\ket{p},

(530)

where
\mathcal{S}
is a unitary S-matrix and
\ket{p}=\prod_{i}a^{\dagger}_{p_{i}}\ket{0}
are radiation modes with measurable momentum
p_{i}
created by
a^{\dagger}_{p_{i}}
acting on the vacuum. Boosting the state
\ket{M}
with a Lorentz transformation
U(\Lambda)
such that the energy differs by
m
then leads to

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}U(\Lambda)\ket{M}=U(\Lambda)\ket{X}=\sum_{p}\mathcal{S}_{M\rightarrow p}\ket{\Lambda p}

(531)

as Lorentz invariance implies the commutation of the S-matrix with
U
. The radiation gets boosted and the vacuum remains invariant, i.e.
U(\Lambda)\ket{0}=\ket{0}
and thus an observer measuring the late radiation can distinguish between the black hole states with and without the early radiation (or the infalling object) corresponding to
\ket{M}
and
U(\Lambda)\ket{M}
respectively. In other words, the existence of the ten Poincaré charges (“hair”) is measurable by the observer.

Soft Hair Proposal

The question is now whether charges related to the supertranslations and -rotations allow to retrieve some information in the same way.
In
[
115
]
(cf. also
[
61
]
) Strominger et.al. argue that this is indeed the case. The crucial caveat in the argumentation leading to the paradox, they argue, is that while the no-hair theorem (correctly) implies that all stationary black hole solutions are diffeomorphic to the Kerr spacetime (a black hole described by angular momentum, mass and charge), the diffeomorphisms do not necessarily act trivial. We saw in section
Asymptotic Flatness
that the onset of a supertranslation changes the Bondi news
N_{AB}
and superrotations similarly lead to physically inequivalent states.
Corresponding to these transformation there exist conserved surface charges (cf. section
Surface Charges
and
[
61
]
) of the form

\displaystyle Q^{+}[f]=\frac{1}{4\pi G}\int_{\mathcal{I}^{+}_{-}}d^{2}z\gamma_{z\bar{z}}fm_{B}=\frac{1}{4\pi G}\int_{\mathcal{I}^{+}}dud^{2}z\gamma_{z\bar{z}}f\left[T_{uu}-\frac{1}{4}\left(D^{2}_{z}N^{zz}+D^{2}_{\bar{z}}N^{\bar{z}\bar{z}}\right)\right],

(532)

where
T_{uu}=1/4N_{AB}N^{AB}+4\pi G\underset{r\rightarrow\infty}{\text{lim}}\left[r^{2}T_{uu}^{M}\right]
is the
uu
component of the stress energy tensor containing contributions from the matter stress energy tensor
T^{M}
. From (
532
) one observes that the charges are split into a “hard” and a “soft” part (the first and second term on the rhs). The soft part in particular is unchanged by Poincaré (
\hat{=}n=1
embedding) transformations but can receive contributions from supertranslations and -rotations by the same arguments that were used in section
Asymptotic Flatness
. In analogy to the electromagnetic case discussed in section 2.5 of
[
61
]
the soft charge here promoted to an operator creates a zero energy graviton mode.

So far the surface charges have been defined on future/past null infinity
\mathcal{I}^{\pm}
. However, in the presence of e.g. a Schwarzschild black hole
\mathcal{I}^{+}
is no longer a Cauchy surface (a submanifold of the spacetime at a specific time for which the evolution equations of GR can be solved uniquely) and one has to include the future event horizon of the black hole
\mathcal{H}^{+}
on which surface charges are defined analogously.
Strominger et. al. proceed to show that dropping matter into a black hole by e.g. sending in a shockwave induces a supertranslation which does change the superrotation horizon charges, which means soft (zero energy) gravitons are created at the horizon. While these soft modes are basically by definition not directly observable (or the detection would take longer and longer time as the energy goes to zero), they are related to the supertransformations which (as mentioned in section
Asymptotic Flatness
) can be observed in principal via memory effects in finite time
[
61
]
. Thus, after the entire black hole is evaporated information might be stored in the degenerate vacuum.

Decoupling of Soft and Hard Modes

The soft hair proposal has been met with a series of papers (
[
127
]
,
[
128
]
,
[
129
]
) refuting that the soft hair have any bearing on the information loss paradox. A central argument, which will be detailed below, is that the soft and hard modes decouple completely and thus the change of the soft charges altered e.g. by infalling matter have no effect on late, measurable (i.e. hard) radiation.

We start our review of this critique (mostly following
[
127
]
) by noting that the splitting of the charges in a hard and a soft part also implies the factorization of the in- and outgoing Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}^{\pm}
according to

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\pm}=\mathcal{H}^{\pm}_{h}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\pm}_{s},

(533)

where
\mathcal{H}^{\pm}_{h/s}
contain hard and soft modes respectively. An ingoing state can then be written as
\ket{\varphi_{in}}=\ket{a}\ket{\alpha}
and a generic process is described by

\displaystyle\bra{\beta}\bra{b}\mathcal{S}\ket{a}\ket{\alpha}\equiv\mathcal{S}_{b,\beta;a,\alpha}.

(534)

Weinbergs soft theorem
20
20

20

This theorem is a gravitational generalization of soft theorems originally developed to tame infrared divergences in quantum electrodynamics by
[
131
]
.

[
130
]
in the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{b,\beta;a,\alpha}=\frac{\bra{\beta}\Omega(b)\Omega^{\dagger}(a)\ket{\alpha}}{\braket{0|\Omega(b)\Omega^{\dagger}(a)|0}}\mathcal{S}_{b,0;a,0}

(535)

implies

\displaystyle\bra{\beta}\bra{b}\mathcal{S}\ket{a}\ket{\alpha}=\braket{b|\hat{\mathcal{S}}|a}\braket{\beta|\Omega(a)|\alpha},

(536)

with the soft graviton operators
\Omega
and
\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{b,a}\equiv\frac{\mathcal{S}_{b,0;a,0}}{\braket{0|\Omega(b)\Omega^{\dagger}(a)|0}}
. The Weinberg theorem and the independence of
\hat{\mathcal{S}}
in particular of the soft operators is a direct consequence of the conservation of the surface charges
Q
. For an extensive discussion of the relation between asymptotic symmetries and soft theorems also in the electromagnetic case see
[
61
]
.
Using the “dressing”
[
127
]

\displaystyle\ket{\ket{a,\alpha}}\equiv\Omega(a)\ket{a}\ket{\alpha},

(537)

the decoupling of soft and hard part of the scattering amplitude

\displaystyle\bra{\bra{b,\beta}}\mathcal{S}\ket{\ket{a,\alpha}}=\braket{b|\hat{\mathcal{S}}|a}\braket{\beta|\alpha}

(538)

follows directly from (
536
) using the unitarity of
\Omega
.

In analogy to the case of the Poincaré hair discussed above let us consider a black hole of mass
M
from which some early soft radiation has been emitted which is described by the action of a generator of supertranslations
\ket{\tilde{M}}=Q\ket{M}
that creates a soft graviton with the associated charge
\beta
. But, using the dressed states with decoupled dynamics of soft and hard modes derived above, infalling hard matter could not be responsible for this change. Suppose now
\ket{M}
decays into
\ket{X}
, i.e.

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}\ket{\tilde{M}}=Q\ket{X}=\sum_{p}\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{M\rightarrow p}Q\ket{\ket{p,0}}=\sum_{p}\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{M\rightarrow p}\ket{\ket{p,\beta}},

(539)

where the commutation of
\hat{\mathcal{S}}
with
Q
is a consequence of the BMS invariance. While the state
\ket{\beta}
is in principle distinguishable for different
\beta
