,
\delta(l_{2})=0
. Then
\delta(l_{m})=0,m>0
follows easily and

\displaystyle 0=\delta([l_{-1},l_{m}])=-[l_{m}\otimes 1+1\otimes l_{m},\delta(l_{-1})]

(675)

for all positive
m
yields
\delta(l_{-1})=0
. Showing that
\delta(l_{-2})=0
and subsequently for all negative
m
is again obtained in an analogous way and thus
Z(\mathfrak{W},\bigwedge^{2}V_{4}))=B(\mathfrak{W},\bigwedge^{2}V_{4}))
.

In the second step it will be shown that
\bm{Z(V_{4},\bigwedge^{2}\mathfrak{B}_{4})\cap\text{{Mor}}_{\mathfrak{W}\oplus\overline{\mathfrak{W}}}(V_{4},\bigwedge^{2}\mathfrak{B}_{4})=\{0\}}
. Using a decomposition as in (
657
) we start with
\bm{\text{{Mor}}_{\mathfrak{W}\oplus\overline{\mathfrak{W}}}(V_{4},\bigwedge^{2}\mathfrak{W})}
. The general ansatz

\displaystyle\delta(T_{mk})=\sum_{i,j}l_{i}\wedge l_{j}

(676)

and the morphism condition

\displaystyle l_{0}\triangleright\delta(T_{mk})=\delta(l_{0}\triangleright T_{mk})

(677)

yield

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j}\left(\frac{1}{2}-m\right)l_{i}\wedge l_{j}=\sum_{i,j}(-i-j)l_{i}\wedge l_{j}

(678)

and thus
\text{Mor}_{\mathfrak{W}\oplus\overline{\mathfrak{W}}}(V_{4},\bigwedge^{2}\mathfrak{W})=\{0\}
.
Similarly, one has
\bm{\text{{Mor}}_{\mathfrak{W}\oplus\overline{\mathfrak{W}}}(V_{4},\bigwedge^{2}\overline{\mathfrak{W}})=\{0\}}
and
\bm{\text{{Mor}}_{\mathfrak{W}\oplus\overline{\mathfrak{W}}}(V_{4},\mathfrak{W}\wedge\overline{\mathfrak{W}})=\{0\}}
.

For a cocycle
\bm{\delta\in\text{{Mor}}_{\mathfrak{W}\oplus\overline{\mathfrak{W}}}(V_{4},\bigwedge^{2}V_{4})}
(
677
) results in

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{mk},j_{mk},k_{mk},s_{mk}}\alpha^{mk}_{i_{mk},j_{mk},k_{mk},s_{mk}}\left(\frac{1}{2}-m\right)T_{i_{mk},j_{mk}}\wedge T_{k_{mk},s_{mk}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i_{mk},j_{mk},k_{mk},s_{mk}}\alpha^{mk}_{i_{mk},j_{mk},k_{mk},s_{mk}}(1-i_{mk}-k_{mk})T_{i_{mk},j_{mk}}\wedge T_{k_{mk},s_{mk}},

(679)

with the ansatz

\displaystyle\delta(T_{mk})=\sum_{i_{mk},j_{mk},k_{mk},s_{mk}}\alpha^{mk}_{i_{mk},j_{mk},k_{mk},s_{mk}}T_{i_{mk},j_{mk}}\wedge T_{k_{mk},s_{mk}}

(680)

and therefore
\text{Mor}_{\mathfrak{W}\oplus\overline{\mathfrak{W}}}(V_{4},\bigwedge^{2}V_{4})=\{0\}
.

Finally, for a cocycle
\bm{\delta\in\text{{Mor}}_{\mathfrak{W}\wedge\overline{\mathfrak{W}}}(V_{4},V_{4}\oplus\mathfrak{W})}
we define the degree such that

\displaystyle\delta(T_{mk})=\sum_{i_{mk},j_{mk},k_{mk}}\alpha^{mk}_{i_{mk},j_{mk},k_{mk}}l_{i_{mk}}\wedge T_{j_{mk},k_{mk}}

(681)

has degree
d=i_{mk}+j_{mk}-m
. If
d\neq 0
the equation

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{2}-m\right)\sum_{i_{mk},j_{mk},k_{mk}}\alpha^{mk}_{i_{mk},j_{mk},k_{mk}}l_{i_{mk}}\wedge T_{j_{mk},k_{mk}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i_{mk},j_{mk},k_{mk}}\alpha^{mk}_{i_{mk},j_{mk},k_{mk}}\left(\frac{1}{2}-i_{mk}-j_{mk}\right)l_{i_{mk}}\wedge T_{j_{mk},k_{mk}}

(682)

from (
677
) implies
\delta=0
.
Otherwise, i.e. for a degree zero cocycle, we set

\displaystyle\delta(T_{mk})=\sum_{i_{mk},s_{mk}}\alpha^{mk}_{i_{mk},s_{mk}}l_{m-i_{mk}}\wedge T_{i_{mk},s_{mk}}

(683)

and the cocycle condition

\displaystyle 0=\delta([T_{0k},T_{mk^{\prime}}])=[T_{0k}\otimes 1+1\otimes T_{0k},\delta(T_{mk^{\prime}})][T_{mk^{\prime}}\otimes 1+1\otimes T_{mk^{\prime}},\delta(T_{0k})]

(684)

results in

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{mk^{\prime}},s_{mk^{\prime}}}\alpha^{mk^{\prime}}_{i_{mk^{\prime}},s_{mk^{\prime}}}\left(-\frac{m-i_{mk}+1}{2}T_{m-i_{mk^{\prime}}}\wedge T_{i_{mk^{\prime}},s_{mk^{\prime}}}\right)

\displaystyle-\sum_{i_{0k},s_{0k}}\alpha^{0k}_{i_{0k},s_{0k}}\left(\left(-\frac{1-i_{0k}}{2}-m\right)T_{m-i_{0k},k}\wedge T_{i_{0k},s_{ok}}\right),

(685)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\alpha^{mk}_{i^{\prime}_{0k},s_{0k}}\frac{m-i^{\prime}_{0k}+1}{2}=

\displaystyle\alpha^{0k}_{i^{\prime}_{0k},s_{0k}}\left(\frac{1-i^{\prime}_{0k}}{2}-m\right)

(686)

for the smallest index
i^{\prime}_{0k}
and sufficiently large
m
. Additionally, the morphism condition yields

\displaystyle l_{m}\triangleright\delta(T_{0k})=

\displaystyle\delta(l_{m}\triangleright T_{0k}),

(687)

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{0k},s_{0k}}\alpha^{0k}_{i_{0k},s_{0k}}\left((m+i_{0k})l_{m-i_{0k}}\wedge T_{i_{0k},s_{0k}}+\left(\frac{m+1}{2}-i_{0k}\right)l_{-i_{0k}}\wedge T_{i_{0k}+m,s_{0k}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i_{mk},s_{mk}}\alpha^{mk}_{i_{mk},s_{mk}}\left(\frac{m+1}{2}l_{m-i_{mk}}\wedge T_{i_{mk},s_{mk}}\right),

(688)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\alpha^{0k}_{i^{\prime}_{0k},s_{0k}}(m+i^{\prime}_{0k})=\alpha^{mk}_{i^{\prime}_{0k},s_{0k}}\frac{m+1}{2}

(689)

under the same conditions. Since the equations (
686
) and (
689
) can not be satisfied simultaneously for all
m
we have
\text{Mor}_{\mathfrak{W}\wedge\overline{\mathfrak{W}}}(V_{4},V_{4}\wedge\mathfrak{W})=\{0\}
and analogously
\text{Mor}_{\mathfrak{W}\wedge\overline{\mathfrak{W}}}(V_{4},V_{4}\wedge\overline{\mathfrak{W}})=\{0\}
. This completes the proof.

Proof of Theorem
9

The proof is structured similarly as for theorem
7
. A generic cocycle in
Z\left(\mathfrak{B}_{4c},\bigwedge^{2}\mathfrak{B}_{4c}\right)
can be split into the parts
\delta=\delta_{\mathfrak{B}}+\delta_{c}+\delta_{\bar{c}}+\delta_{c,\bar{c}}
mapping to
\bigwedge^{2}\mathfrak{B}_{4}
,
c_{l}\bigwedge\mathfrak{B}_{4}
,
c_{\bar{l}}\bigwedge\mathfrak{B}_{4}
and
c_{l}\bigwedge c_{\bar{l}}\mathbb{C}
respectively. The cocycle condition with
X,Y\in\mathfrak{B}_{4}

\displaystyle\delta([X,Y])=[\delta(X),Y\otimes 1+1\otimes Y]-(X\leftrightarrow Y)

(690)

splits into

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathfrak{B}}([X,Y]_{\mathfrak{B}})

\displaystyle=[\delta_{\mathfrak{B}}(X),Y\otimes 1+1\otimes Y]_{\mathfrak{B}}-(X\leftrightarrow Y),

(691)

\displaystyle\delta_{c}([X,Y]_{\mathfrak{B}})

\displaystyle=[\delta_{\mathfrak{B}}(X),Y\otimes 1+1\otimes Y]_{c}+[\delta_{c}(X),Y\otimes 1+1\otimes Y]_{\mathfrak{B}}-(X\leftrightarrow Y),

(692)

\displaystyle\delta_{\bar{c}}([X,Y]_{\mathfrak{B}})

\displaystyle=[\delta_{\mathfrak{B}}(X),Y\otimes 1+1\otimes Y]_{\bar{c}}+[\delta_{\bar{c}}(X),Y\otimes 1+1\otimes Y]_{\mathfrak{B}}-(X\leftrightarrow Y),

(693)

\displaystyle\delta_{c,\bar{c}}([X,Y]_{\mathfrak{B}})

\displaystyle=[\delta_{c}(X),Y\otimes 1+1\otimes Y]_{\bar{c}}+[\delta_{\bar{c}}(X),Y\otimes 1+1\otimes Y]_{c}-(X\leftrightarrow Y),

(694)

where
[,]_{\mathfrak{B}}
,
[,]_{c}
and
[,]_{\bar{c}}
conserves or respectively increases the number of central charges in the input. Equation (
691
) implies with theorem
8
that
\bm{\delta_{\mathfrak{B}}}
is a coboundary with a cochain
r\in\bigwedge^{2}\mathfrak{B}_{4}
. After redefining
\delta\rightarrow\delta-\delta_{r}
we next turn to the semi-simple “factor”
\mathfrak{W}\oplus\mathfrak{W}
of the semi-direct product
\mathfrak{B}
using equations (
692
)-(
693
) where the first term on the rhs vanishes. We further decompose

\displaystyle Z\left(\mathfrak{W}\oplus\mathfrak{W},c_{l}\bigwedge\left(\mathfrak{W}\oplus\mathfrak{W}\right)\right)=

\displaystyle Z\left(\mathfrak{W},c_{l}\bigwedge\mathfrak{W}\right)\oplus Z\left(\mathfrak{W},c_{l}\bigwedge\overline{\mathfrak{W}}\right)

\displaystyle\oplus Z\left(\overline{\mathfrak{W}},c_{l}\bigwedge\overline{\mathfrak{W}}\right)\oplus Z\left(\overline{\mathfrak{W}},c_{l}\bigwedge\mathfrak{W}\right)

(695)

and (
692
) for
\bm{\delta_{c}\in Z\left(\mathfrak{W},c_{l}\bigwedge\mathfrak{W}\right)}
can be used as in the last part of the proof of theorem
2
by replacing
L_{m}\rightarrow l_{m},\bar{L}_{m}\rightarrow c_{l}
to show that
\delta_{c}
has to be a coboundary from a cochain
r\in c_{l}\bigwedge\mathfrak{W}
. Similarly
\bm{Z\left(\overline{\mathfrak{W}},c_{l}\bigwedge\overline{\mathfrak{W}}\right)=B\left(\overline{\mathfrak{W}},c_{l}\bigwedge\overline{\mathfrak{W}}\right)}
and the remaining parts have to vanish because e.g. the rhs of (
692
) is alwas zero for
\delta_{c}\in Z\left(\mathfrak{W},c_{l}\bigwedge\overline{\mathfrak{W}}\right)
. Equation (
693
) completely analogously asserts the same statements for
c_{l}\rightarrow c_{\bar{l}}
.

Next we consider cocycles
\bm{\delta_{c}\in Z\left(\mathfrak{W}\oplus\mathfrak{W},c_{l}\bigwedge V_{4}\right)}
. With the general ansatz

\displaystyle\delta_{c}(l_{m})=\sum_{p_{m},q_{m}}\alpha^{m}_{p_{m},q_{m}}c_{l}\wedge T_{p_{m},q_{m}}

(696)

the cochain
r=\sum_{p_{0},q_{0}}\alpha^{0}_{p_{0},q_{0}}\frac{1}{1/2-p_{0}}c_{l}\wedge T_{p_{0},q_{0}}
can be used so that
\delta_{c}\rightarrow\delta_{c}-\delta_{r}
vanishes on
l_{0}
. Then the cocycle condition (
692
) with
X=l_{m},Y=l_{0}
gives

\displaystyle m\delta_{c}(l_{m})=\left(p-\frac{1}{2}\right)\delta_{c}(l_{m})

(697)

and thus
\delta_{c}(l_{m})=0
and similarly also
\delta_{c}(\bar{l}_{m})=0=\delta_{\bar{c}}(l_{m})=\delta_{\bar{c}}(\bar{l}_{m})
.

Equation (
692
) for
\bm{\delta_{c}\in Z\left(V_{4},c_{l}\bigwedge\mathfrak{W}\right)}
(and similarly for
\mathfrak{W}\rightarrow\overline{\mathfrak{W}}
) with
X=l_{0},Y=T_{pq}
is just a morphism condition and with the ansatz

\displaystyle\delta_{c}(T_{pq})=\sum_{i_{pq}}\alpha^{pq}_{i_{pq}}c_{l}\wedge l_{i_{pq}}

(698)

it yields

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{2}-p\right)\delta_{c}(T_{pq})=\delta_{c}(T_{pq})i_{pq}

(699)

which thus has to vanish.

This leaves us with
\bm{\delta_{c}\in Z\left(V_{4},c_{l}\bigwedge V_{4}\right)}
where the morphism condition with
X=l_{0},Y=T_{pq}
and the ansatz

\displaystyle\delta_{c}(T_{pq})=\sum_{i_{pq},j_{pq}}\alpha^{pq}_{i_{pq},j_{pq}}c_{l}\wedge T_{i_{pq},j_{pq}}

(700)

results in

\displaystyle\left(p-\frac{1}{2}\right)\delta_{c}(T_{pq})=\delta_{c}(T_{pq})\left(i_{pq}-\frac{1}{q}\right),

(701)

which implies
\delta_{c}(T_{pq})=0
unless the left degree
d_{L}\equiv i_{pq}-m
is zero. Similarly one finds that any cocycle with right degree
d_{R}\equiv j_{pq}-m\neq 0
has to vanish, leaving only the possibility

\displaystyle\delta_{\alpha,\bar{\alpha}}:l_{m}\mapsto 0,\quad\bar{l}_{m}\mapsto 0,\quad T_{pq}\mapsto(\alpha c_{l}+\bar{\alpha}c_{\bar{l}})\wedge T_{pq},

(702)

which satisfies all the cocyle conditions and can not be written as a coboundary because the only r-matrices with the correct grading (
r\sim c_{l/\bar{l}}\wedge l_{0},\;r\sim c_{l/\bar{l}}\wedge\bar{l}_{0}
) do not reproduce the coefficients.

Finally, we have to consider cocycles mapping to
\bm{c_{l}\bigwedge c_{\bar{l}}\mathbb{C}}
. To this end we simply use the cocycle condition (
694
) with
X=l_{m},Y=l_{n}
(respectively
X=\bar{l}_{m},Y=\bar{l}_{n}
) and that
\delta_{c}
(
\delta_{\bar{c}}
) is cohomolog to zero to show that
\delta_{c,\bar{c}}(l_{m})
(
\delta_{c,\bar{c}}(\bar{l}_{m})
) has to vanish. The same equations with
X=l_{m},Y=T_{pq}
imply
\delta_{c,\bar{c}}(T_{pq})=0
if we take into account that on the rhs
\delta_{c}(T_{pq})
(or
\delta_{\bar{c}}(T_{pq})
) is cohomolog to at most
\delta_{\alpha,\bar{\alpha}}
and thus the brackets
[,]_{c},[,]_{\bar{c}}
vanish.

From cocycle conditions with
X=c_{l}
and
Y\in\mathfrak{B}_{4}
we again conclude that
\delta(c_{l})
(or
\delta(c_{\bar{l}})
) has to be ad-invariant. Contrary to the proof of theorems
4
and
7
this at first sight leaves open the possibility that
\delta(c_{l})\sim c_{l}\wedge c_{\bar{l}}
. However, from

\displaystyle\delta([l_{n},l_{-n}]_{c})=[\delta(l_{n}),l_{-n}\otimes 1+1\otimes l_{-n}]_{c}-(n\leftrightarrow-n)

(703)

and the previous results that
\delta
is cohomolog to zero on
\mathfrak{W}
we see that
\delta(c_{l})=\delta(c_{\bar{l}})=0
concluding the proof.

Classification of
r
-matrices

Classification of Triangular r-matrices in
\mathfrak{o}(4)

First, note that since
\mathfrak{o}(4,\mathbb{C})=\mathfrak{sl}(2)\oplus\bar{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
and
\bigwedge^{2}\mathfrak{o}(4,\mathbb{C})=\mathfrak{sl}(2)\wedge{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)\oplus\mathfrak{sl}(2)\wedge\bar{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)\oplus\bar{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)\wedge\bar{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
each r-matrix can be split according to

\displaystyle r=a+\bar{a}+b,\quad a\in\mathfrak{sl}(2)\wedge{\mathfrak{sl}}(2),\,\bar{a}\in\bar{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)\wedge\bar{\mathfrak{sl}}(2),\,b\in\mathfrak{sl}(2)\wedge\bar{\mathfrak{sl}}(2).

(704)

Starting with a generic

\displaystyle a=\alpha_{+}L_{1}\wedge L_{0}+\alpha_{0}L_{1}\wedge L_{-1}+\alpha_{-}L_{-1}\wedge L_{0},

(705)

triangularity
[[a,a]]=0
enforces

\displaystyle\alpha_{0}^{2}=-\alpha_{+}\alpha_{-}.

(706)

Using the automorphism (
233
) with
\gamma=\sqrt{-\frac{\alpha_{-}}{\alpha_{+}}},\epsilon=1
in the case
\alpha_{0}\neq 0
and with
\epsilon=-1(+1)
if
\alpha_{-}=0(\alpha_{+}=0)
we find that there are two one-parameter r-matrices in
\mathfrak{sl}(2)\wedge{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)

\displaystyle a_{1}=\alpha(L_{1}\wedge L_{0}+L_{1}\wedge L_{-1}-L_{-1}\wedge L_{0}),

(707)

\displaystyle a_{2}=\alpha L_{1}\wedge L_{0}

(708)

and similar for
\bar{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)\wedge\bar{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)

\displaystyle\bar{a}_{1}=\bar{\alpha}(\bar{L}_{1}\wedge\bar{L}_{0}+\bar{L}_{1}\wedge\bar{L}_{-1}-\bar{L}_{-1}\wedge\bar{L}_{0}),

(709)

\displaystyle\bar{a}_{2}=\bar{\alpha}\bar{L}_{1}\wedge\bar{L}_{0}.

(710)

For r-matrices that only contain terms of type
b
one has to demand
[[b,b]]=0
and the general result (before applying any automorphisms) as obtained in
[
95
]
reads

\displaystyle(\beta_{+}L_{1}+\beta_{0}L_{0}+\beta_{-}L_{-1})\wedge(\bar{\beta}_{+}\bar{L}_{1}+\bar{\beta}_{0}\bar{L}_{0}+\bar{\beta}_{-}\bar{L}_{-1}).

(711)

Taking into account the automorphisms (
233
), (
234
) one can represent this as eleven r-matrices with up to four parameters

\displaystyle b_{1}=

\displaystyle(\beta L_{1}+\beta_{0}L_{0}+\beta L_{-1})\wedge(\bar{\beta}\bar{L}_{1}+\bar{\beta}_{0}\bar{L}_{0}+\bar{\beta}\bar{L}_{-1}),

(712)

\displaystyle b_{2}=

\displaystyle(L_{1}+L_{0})\wedge(\bar{\beta}\bar{L}_{1}+\bar{\beta}_{0}\bar{L}_{0}+\bar{\beta}\bar{L}_{-1}),

(713)

\displaystyle b_{3}=

\displaystyle(L_{1}+L_{-1})\wedge(\bar{\beta}\bar{L}_{1}+\bar{\beta}_{0}\bar{L}_{0}+\bar{\beta}\bar{L}_{-1}),

(714)

\displaystyle b_{4}=

\displaystyle\beta(L_{1}+L_{0})\wedge(\bar{L}_{1}+\bar{L}_{0}),\quad b_{5}=\beta(L_{1}+L_{-1})\wedge(\bar{L}_{1}+\bar{L}_{-1}),

(715)

\displaystyle b_{6}=

\displaystyle\beta(L_{1}+L_{-1})\wedge(\bar{L}_{1}+\bar{L}_{0}),\quad b_{7}=L_{1}\wedge(\bar{\beta}\bar{L}_{1}+\bar{\beta}_{0}\bar{L}_{0}+\bar{\beta}\bar{L}_{-1}),

(716)

\displaystyle b_{8}=

\displaystyle L_{1}\wedge\bar{\beta}(\bar{L}_{1}+\bar{L}_{0}),\quad b_{9}=L_{1}\wedge\bar{\beta}(\bar{L}_{1}+\bar{L}_{-1}),

(717)

\displaystyle b_{10}=

\displaystyle L_{1}\wedge\bar{L}_{1},\quad b_{11}=L_{1}\wedge\bar{L}_{0}.

(718)

When combining
a
,
\bar{a}
and
b
terms there are two different cases,
[[b,b]]=0
and
[[b,b]]=-2[[b,a]]-2[[b,\bar{a}]]\neq 0
, that will be analyzed separately.
In the first case (for the moment considering only
a
terms) one infers
[[b,a]]=0
and
[[a,a]]=0
. With the general ansatz (
705
) for
a
and

\displaystyle b=

\displaystyle\beta_{1}L_{1}\wedge\bar{L}_{1}+\beta_{2}L_{1}\wedge\bar{L}_{0}+\beta_{3}L_{1}\wedge\bar{L}_{-1}

\displaystyle+\beta_{4}L_{0}\wedge\bar{L}_{1}+\beta_{5}L_{0}\wedge\bar{L}_{0}+\beta_{6}L_{0}\wedge\bar{L}_{-1}

\displaystyle+\beta_{7}L_{-1}\wedge\bar{L}_{1}+\beta_{8}L_{-1}\wedge\bar{L}_{0}\beta_{9}L_{-1}\wedge\bar{L}_{-1}

(719)

we extract the equations

\displaystyle-2\beta_{1}\alpha_{0}+\beta_{4}\alpha_{1}

\displaystyle=0,\quad-\beta_{4}\alpha_{-1}-2\beta_{7}\alpha_{0}=0,

(720)

\displaystyle\beta_{1}\alpha_{-1}+\beta_{7}\alpha_{1}

\displaystyle=0,\quad-2\beta_{2}\alpha_{0}+\beta_{5}\alpha_{1}=0,

(721)

\displaystyle-\beta_{5}\alpha_{-1}-2\beta_{8}\alpha_{0}

\displaystyle=0,\quad\beta_{2}\alpha_{-1}+\beta_{8}\alpha_{1}=0,

(722)

\displaystyle-2\beta_{3}\alpha_{0}+\beta_{6}\alpha_{1}

\displaystyle=0,\quad-\beta_{6}\alpha_{-1}-2\beta_{9}\alpha_{0}=0,

(723)

\displaystyle\beta_{3}\alpha_{-1}+\beta_{9}\alpha_{1}

\displaystyle=0

(724)

from
[[b,a]]=0
. For the coefficients of
a
triangularity entails (
706
) and for
b
we additionally use the automorphisms to bring them in the form (
712
)-(
718
). For
b=b_{1}
, implying

\beta_{1}=\beta_{3}=\beta_{7}=\beta_{9},\quad\beta_{2}=\beta_{8},\quad\beta_{4}=\beta_{6},

the equations (
720
)-(
724
) yield

\alpha_{-1}=-\alpha_{1},\quad\beta_{4}=2\beta_{1},\quad\beta_{5}=2\beta_{2},

resulting in

\displaystyle r=(L_{1}+L_{-1}+2L_{0})\wedge(\beta_{1}(\bar{L}_{1}+\bar{L}_{-1})+\beta_{2}\bar{L}_{0})+a_{1}.

(725)

Similarly, for the other r-matrix components of type
b
one has

\displaystyle r\equiv b_{2}+a=

\displaystyle L_{1}\wedge(\beta_{1}(\bar{L}_{1}+\bar{L}_{-1})+\beta_{2}\bar{L}_{0})+a_{2},

(726)

\displaystyle r\equiv b_{3}+a=

\displaystyle b_{3}+\alpha(L_{1}-L_{-1})\wedge L_{0},

(727)

\displaystyle r\equiv b_{4}+a=

\displaystyle\beta L_{1}\wedge(\bar{L}_{1}+\bar{L}_{0})+a_{2},

(728)

\displaystyle r\equiv b_{4}+a=

\displaystyle\beta(L_{1}+L_{0})\wedge\bar{L}_{1}+a_{2},

(729)

\displaystyle r\equiv b_{5}+a=

\displaystyle b_{5}+\alpha(L_{1}-L_{-1})\wedge L_{0},

(730)

\displaystyle r\equiv b_{6}+a=

\displaystyle b_{6}+a_{1},

(731)

\displaystyle r\equiv b_{7}+a=

\displaystyle L_{1}\wedge\bar{L}_{0}+a_{2},

(732)

\displaystyle r\equiv b_{8}+a=

\displaystyle L_{1}\wedge(\bar{L}_{1}+\bar{L}_{0})+a_{2},

(733)

\displaystyle r\equiv b_{9}+a=

\displaystyle L_{1}\wedge(\bar{L}_{1}+\bar{L}_{-1})+a_{2},

(734)

\displaystyle r\equiv b_{10}+a=

\displaystyle L_{1}\wedge\bar{L}_{1}+a_{2}.

(735)

To classify r-matrices of the form
b+\bar{a}
one can use (
234
) and that the coefficients of
\bar{b}
with (
719
) are just the transposed coefficients (if they are represented by a
3\times 3
matrix) of
b
and a global minus sign. In the symmetric cases
b_{1},b_{4},b_{5},b_{10}
the results are automorphic to (
726
)-(
731
) with
\varphi^{\prime}
and for the rest one has

\displaystyle r=-\bar{b}_{2}+a=

\displaystyle\beta(L_{1}+L_{-1}+2L_{0})\wedge(\bar{L}_{1}+\bar{L}_{0})+a_{1},

(736)

\displaystyle r=-\bar{b}_{4}+a=

\displaystyle\beta(L_{1}+L_{-1}+2L_{0})\wedge(\bar{L}_{1}+\bar{L}_{-1})+a_{1},

(737)

\displaystyle r=-\bar{b}_{6}+a=

\displaystyle b_{6}+\alpha L_{1}\wedge(L_{0}+2L_{-1}),

(738)

\displaystyle r=-\bar{b}_{7}+a=

\displaystyle L_{1}\wedge(\bar{L}_{1}+\bar{L}_{-1}+2\bar{L}_{0})+\bar{a}_{1}.

(739)

While the
r
in (
727
) and (
738
) are solutions of
[[b,a]]=0
, the
a
part is not triangular so they have to be discarded.
Combining the previous results (and explicitly calculating some ”overlaps” of the form
[[b,\bar{a}]]
), we find for
b+a+\bar{a}
the following possibilities

\displaystyle r\equiv

\displaystyle b_{1}+a+\bar{a}=(L_{1}+L_{-1}+2L_{0})\wedge(\bar{L}_{1}+\bar{L}_{1}+2\bar{L}_{0})+a_{1}+\bar{a}_{1},

(740)

\displaystyle r\equiv

\displaystyle b_{2}+a+\bar{a}=\beta L_{1}\wedge(\bar{L}_{1}+\bar{L}_{-1}+2\bar{L}_{0})+a_{2}+\bar{a}_{1},

(741)

\displaystyle r\equiv

\displaystyle b_{4}+a+\bar{a}=L_{1}\wedge(\bar{L}_{1}+\bar{L}_{0})+a_{2}+\bar{a}_{2},

(742)

\displaystyle r\equiv

\displaystyle b_{10}+a+\bar{a}=L_{1}\wedge\bar{L}_{1}+a_{2}+\bar{a}_{2}.

(743)

In the case
[[b,b]]\neq 0
we again make use of the results found in
[
95
]
. In particular, the general solution for the equation

\displaystyle 0\neq[[b,b]]=-2[[b,a]]-2[[b,\bar{a}]]

(744)

up to
\text{Aut}(\mathfrak{o}(4,\mathbb{C}))
has the form

\displaystyle\alpha L_{1}\wedge L_{-1}-\alpha\bar{L}_{1}\wedge\bar{L}_{-1}+b,\quad\alpha L_{1}\wedge L_{0}+\alpha\bar{L}_{1}\wedge\bar{L}_{0}+b^{\prime},

(745)

with specific
b,b^{\prime}
that are not of interest for now. The first r-matrix in (
745
) is quasitriangular with ad-invariant (in
\mathfrak{o}(4,\mathbb{C})
) element containing
\Omega=4\alpha^{2}L_{1}\wedge L_{0}\wedge L_{-1}+...
and since the solutions of (
744
) up to
\text{Aut}(\mathfrak{W}\oplus\mathfrak{W})
are in the orbits of
\mathfrak{o}(4,\mathbb{C})
automorphisms
\varphi
containing (
745
) we would need

\displaystyle\varphi(\Omega)=4\alpha^{2}\varphi(L_{1}\wedge L_{0}\wedge L_{-1})+...=0

(746)

to obtain a triangular solution. This, however, would entail that the matrix of the coefficients of
\varphi
has determinant zero but then it would not be invertible and thus
\varphi
no automorphism. Furthermore, there can be no
\mathfrak{o}(4,\mathbb{C})
automorphism that maps the
a
terms of the second solution of (
745
) to
a_{1}
because
a_{1}
can not be written in the form
(\alpha_{1}L_{1}+\alpha_{2}L_{0}+\alpha_{3}L_{-1})\wedge(\alpha^{\prime}_{1}L_{1}+\alpha^{\prime}_{2}L_{0}+\alpha^{\prime}_{3}L_{-1})
. We conclude that only r-matrices of the form
b+a_{2}+\bar{a}_{2}
have to be considered. To this end we extract the equations

\displaystyle\beta_{1}\beta_{5}-\beta_{2}\beta_{4}+\beta_{4}2\alpha

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle-\beta_{4}\beta_{8}+\beta_{7}\beta_{5}

\displaystyle=0,

(747)

\displaystyle\beta_{1}\beta_{8}-\beta_{2}\beta_{7}+\beta_{7}2\alpha

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle 2\beta_{1}\beta_{6}-2\beta_{3}\beta_{4}+\beta_{5}2\alpha

\displaystyle=0,

(748)

\displaystyle-2\beta_{4}\beta_{9}+2\beta_{6}\beta_{7}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle 2\beta_{1}\beta_{9}-2\beta_{3}\beta_{7}+\beta_{8}2\alpha

\displaystyle=0,

(749)

\displaystyle-\beta_{3}\beta_{5}+\beta_{2}\beta_{6}+\beta_{6}2\alpha

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle-\beta_{5}\beta_{9}+\beta_{6}\beta_{8}

\displaystyle=0,

(750)

\displaystyle-\beta_{3}\beta_{8}+\beta_{2}\beta_{9}+\beta_{9}2\alpha

\displaystyle=0

(751)

from (
744
). Additionally, we also get the same equations with
\alpha\rightarrow-\bar{\alpha}
and in the terms proportional to
\bar{\alpha}
the coefficients of
b
are transposed. Solving these equations yields only the solution

\displaystyle\beta_{1}L_{1}\wedge\bar{L}_{1}+\beta_{2}(L_{1}\wedge\bar{L}_{0}+\bar{L}_{1}\wedge L_{0})+\beta_{2}L_{1}\wedge L_{0}+\beta_{2}\bar{L}_{1}\wedge\bar{L}_{0},

(752)

i.e. the same as in
[
95
]
.
After removing duplicates, all the r-matrices we found can be casted into the classes (
235
)-(
242
).

Triangular r-matrices in
\mathfrak{P}_{4}

In table 1 in
[
98
]
the classification of r-matrices in the four dimensional Poincaré algebra is given. It is not quite complete, however, only certain quasitriangular r-matrices are missing. After calculating the Schouten brackets we obtain the following triangular part of this classification.

\displaystyle r_{1}

\displaystyle=\bar{k}_{0}\wedge k_{0}+\alpha S_{11}\wedge S_{00},\qquad r_{2}=\bar{k}_{1}\wedge k_{1}+\beta S_{11}\wedge\bar{k}_{0},

(753)

\displaystyle r_{3}

\displaystyle=\bar{k}_{1}\wedge k_{1}+\alpha S_{11}\wedge S_{01},\qquad r_{4}=\alpha_{1}S_{11}\wedge S_{01}+\alpha_{2}S_{11}\wedge A_{01},

(754)

\displaystyle r_{5}

\displaystyle=k_{0}\wedge k_{1}-\bar{k}_{0}\wedge\bar{k}_{1}+\gamma\bar{k}_{1}\wedge k_{1},\qquad r_{6}=k_{0}\wedge k_{1},

(755)

\displaystyle r_{7}

\displaystyle=S_{11}\wedge k_{0}+S_{01}\wedge k_{1}+A_{01}\wedge\bar{k}_{1}+\beta S_{11}\wedge k_{1},

(756)

\displaystyle r_{8}

\displaystyle=S_{11}\wedge(k_{0}+k_{1})+\alpha S_{11}\wedge A_{01},

(757)

\displaystyle r_{9}

\displaystyle=S_{01}\wedge\bar{k}_{1}+S_{11}\wedge k_{1}+\alpha_{1}S_{00}\wedge S_{01}+\alpha_{2}S_{11}\wedge A_{01},

(758)

\displaystyle r_{10}

\displaystyle=A_{01}\wedge k_{1}+\alpha_{1}S_{11}\wedge S_{01}+\alpha_{2}S_{00}\wedge A_{01},

(759)

\displaystyle r_{11}

\displaystyle=S_{11}\wedge k_{1}+S_{00}\wedge(\alpha S_{11}+\alpha_{1}S_{01}+\alpha_{2}A_{01})+\tilde{\alpha}S_{11}\wedge A_{01},

(760)

\displaystyle r_{12}

\displaystyle=(S_{11}+S_{00})\wedge\bar{k}_{0}+\alpha_{1}S_{11}\wedge S_{00}+\alpha_{2}S_{01}\wedge A_{01},

(761)

\displaystyle r_{13}

\displaystyle=(S_{11}-S_{00})\wedge\bar{k}_{0}+\alpha_{1}S_{11}\wedge S_{00}+\alpha_{2}S_{01}\wedge A_{01},

(762)

\displaystyle r_{14}

\displaystyle=S_{11}\wedge\bar{k}_{0}+\alpha_{1}S_{11}\wedge S_{00}+\alpha_{2}S_{01}\wedge A_{01},

(763)

\displaystyle r_{15}

\displaystyle=S_{01}\wedge k_{0}+\alpha_{1}S_{11}\wedge S_{00}+\alpha_{2}S_{01}\wedge A_{01},

(764)

\displaystyle r_{16}

\displaystyle=S_{11}\wedge k_{0}+\alpha_{1}S_{11}\wedge S_{00}+\alpha_{2}S_{01}\wedge A_{01},

(765)

\displaystyle r_{17}

\displaystyle=S_{11}\wedge(k_{0}+\beta\bar{k}_{0})+\alpha S_{01}\wedge A_{01}.

(766)
